Y:=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)
.
By our choice of
u
and
v
, we know their adjacency in
G_{\star}
was determined by case
(F.

2

)
and the following are equivalent.

1.

The adjacency between
u
and
v
got flipped when going from
G
to
G_{\star}
.

2.

s(X)\in S(Y)
.
(by
(F.

2

)
)

3.

s(u)\in N(v)\cap S

(by definition)

4.

v
is adjacent to
s(u)
in
G
.
(by definition)

5.

v
is adjacent to
u
in
G
.
(by (
5
))

The equivalence between the first and the last item establishes that
u
and
v
are non-adjacent in
G_{\star}
, a contradiction.

•

Finally, we assume that
u\in C[j,h]
for some
j\in I_{\star}
.
As we know that
u\notin N^{G_{\star}}_{h-1}[a_{i}]
,
10.2
yields
i\neq j
.
Then
10.1
applied to
u\in C[j,h]
and
v\in C[i,h]
yields

j=\mathrm{ex}(u)\neq i=\mathrm{ex}(v),

which together with the sampling property gives

\mathrm{atp}(u/C[i,h])=\mathrm{atp}(s(u)/C[i,h])\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{atp}(v/C[j,h])=\mathrm{atp}(s(v)/C[j,h]).

(6)

Let
X:=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u)
and
Y:=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)
.
By our choice of
u
and
v
, we know their adjacency in
G_{\star}
was determined by case
(F.

1

)
and the following are equivalent.

1.

The adjacency between
u
and
v
got flipped when going from
G
to
G_{\star}
.

2.

s(Y)\in S(X)
or
s(X)\in S(Y)
.
(by
(F.

1

)
)

3.

s(v)\in N(u)\cap S
or
s(u)\in N(v)\cap S
.
(by definition)

4.

u
is a neighbor of
s(v)
in
G
or
v
is a neighbor of
s(u)
in
G
.
(by definition)

5.

u
is a neighbor of
v
in
G
or
v
is a neighbor of
u
in
G
.
(by (
6
))

6.

u
is a neighbor of
v
in
G
.

The equivalence between the first and the last item establishes that
u
and
v
are non-adjacent in
G_{\star}
, a contradiction.

Having exhausted all other possibilities, we conclude that
u\in C[i,h+1]
, which proves the claim.
∎

The combination of
10.2
,
10.3
, and
10.4
proves
property
(U.

1

)
.
Hence,
\mathcal{C}:=(C,\mathcal{K}_{\star},F_{\star},F_{\star})
is an insulator of cost
\mathrm{const}(k,t)
in
G
.
This proves that
\mathcal{C}
is the desired row extension.
It remains to analyze the running time.

Running Time.

Let
n:=|V(G)|
.
The application of
Lemma

9.20
runs in time
O_{t}(n^{2})
.
In the case where a sample set
S
is returned, we can calculate the
witnessing function
s
in time
O_{t}(n^{2})
by comparing each vertex
v\in V(G)
with each of the
\mathrm{const}(t)
many vertices from
S
over every column
of
\mathcal{A}|_{I}
.
With the function
s
at hand, we can also build the row-extension
C
of
B
in time
O_{t}(n^{2})
.
The construction of
\mathcal{K}_{\star}
and
F_{\star}
runs in time
O_{k,t}(n)
.
This yields an overall running time of
O_{k,t}(n^{2})
.
∎

10.2
Extending Ordered Insulators

See
7.7

Proof.

We first apply
Lemma

9.5
to
\mathcal{A}
, which yields a subsequence
I\subseteq J
of size
U_{t}(|J|)
and a set
S\subseteq V(G)\setminus\mathcal{A}|_{I}
of size
\mathrm{const}(t)
such that either

•

G
contains a prepattern of order
t
on
\mathcal{A}|_{I}
, or

•

S
samples
G
on
\mathcal{A}|_{I}
with margin
2
.

In the first case we are done, so assume the second case.
By possibly taking a subsequence and applying
Lemma

9.8
, we can assume the following.

Property 10.5
.

I
does not contain the first and last two elements of
J
.

Let
B
be the grid of
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{A}|_{I}
and let
I_{\star}:=\mathrm{tail}(I)
be the sequence indexing
B
.
As
S
samples
G
on
\mathcal{B}
with margin
2
, there exist functions

\mathrm{ex}:V(G)\rightarrow I_{\star}
and
s_{<},s_{>}:V(G)\rightarrow S
, such that every
v\in V(G)
is
(2,\mathrm{ex}(v),s_{<}(v),s_{>}(v))
-sampled in
\mathcal{B}
.
We can assume
\mathrm{ex}
is chosen maximal in the following sense: for every vertex
v\in V(G)
and index
i\in I_{\star}
, if
\mathrm{atp}(v/B[{\leqslant}i,*])=\mathrm{atp}(s_{<}(v)/B[{\leqslant}i,*])
then
\mathrm{ex}(v)\geqslant i
.

Defining the Grid.

We will build a row-extension
C
of
B
.
By definition of a row-extension we have
C[i,r]:=B[i,r]
for all
i\in I_{\star}
and
r\in[h]
.
It remains to define the row
C[*,h+1]
.
For every
i\in I_{\star}
, we set
C[i,h+1]
to contain every vertex
v
such that

•

v
is not contained in
B
, and

•

\mathrm{atp}(v/B[i,h])\neq\mathrm{atp}(s_{<}(v)/B[i,h])
and
i
is the minimal index in
I_{\star}
with this property.

It is easy to see that cells of
C
are pairwise disjoint and
C
is a valid grid.
Furthermore, we have the following property.

Claim 10.6
.

For every
i\in I_{\star}
and
v\in C[i,*]
, we have
\mathrm{ex}(v)\in\{i-1,i\}
.

Proof.

If
v\in C[i,\leqslant h]=B[i,*]
then, since
B[i,*]
contains
v
but neither
s_{<}(v)
nor
s_{>}(v)
,
the atomic type of
v
differs from the atomic types of both
s_{<}(v)
and
s_{>}(v)
over
B[i,*]
. By the sampling property with margin
2
, we have that
i=\mathrm{ex}(v)
or
i=\mathrm{ex}(v)+1
.

If
v\in C[i,h+1]
, then by construction the atomic type of
v
differs from the atomic type of
s_{<}(v)
over
B[i,*]
.
This yields
\mathrm{ex}(v)\leqslant i
.
As we have chosen
i
minimal, we have

\mathrm{atp}(v/B[{\leqslant}i-1,*])=\mathrm{atp}(s_{<}(v)/B[{\leqslant}i-1,*])
.
As we have chosen
\mathrm{ex}
maximal, we have
\mathrm{ex}(v)\geqslant i-1
.
∎

The rest of the proof will be devoted to constructing
\mathcal{K}_{\star}
,
F_{\star}
, and
R_{\star}
such that the row-extension
\mathcal{C}:=(C,\mathcal{K}_{\star},F_{\star},R_{\star})
is an insulator of cost
\mathrm{const}(k,t)
in
G
.

Defining the Insulator.

We build
\mathcal{K}_{\star}
as a refinement of
\mathcal{K}
by encoding into the color of every vertex
v\in V(G)
for every color
X\in\mathcal{K}
and sample vertex
s\in S
the information

(C.
1
)

whether
v\in X
,

(C.
2
)

whether
v\in C
,

(C.
3
)

whether
v\in C[*,h]
,

(C.
4
)

whether
v\in C[*,h+1]
,

(C.
5
)

whether
v\in N(s)
,

(C.
6
)

whether
v\in\mathrm{int}(B)
,

(C.
7
)

whether
s_{<}(v)=s
,

(C.
8
)

whether
s_{>}(v)=s
,

(C.
9
)

whether
v
has a neighbor in
X\cap\mathrm{int}(B)
.

As
\mathcal{K}
has size
k
and
S
has size
\mathrm{const}(t)
, we have
|\mathcal{K}_{\star}|=\mathrm{const}(k,t)
.
By
(C.

7

)
,
(C.

8

)
,
(C.

5

)
, and
(C.

1

)
, we can define for every color
X\in\mathcal{K}_{\star}

•

a left sample
s_{<}(X)\in S
, such that for all
v\in X
we have
s_{<}(v)=s_{<}(X)
,

•

a right sample
s_{>}(X)\in S
, such that for all
v\in X
we have
s_{>}(v)=s_{>}(X)
,

•

sample neighbors
S(X)\subseteq S
, such that for all
v\in X
we have
N(v)\cap S=S(X)
, and

•

a color
\mathcal{K}(X)\in\mathcal{K}
, such that for all
v\in X
we have
\mathcal{K}(v)=\mathcal{K}(X)
.

We define
F_{\star}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\star}^{2}
via the following four cases.
Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{K}_{\star}
.

(F.
1
)

If
X\subseteq C[*,h+1]
and
Y\subseteq C[*,h]
, then

(X,Y)\in F_{\star}\Leftrightarrow s_{<}(X)\in S(Y).

(F.
2
)

If
X\subseteq C[*,h]
and
Y\subseteq C[*,h+1]
, then

(X,Y)\in F_{\star}\Leftrightarrow s_{<}(Y)\in S(X).

(F.
3
)

If
X\subseteq C[*,h+1]
and
Y\subseteq C[*,{<}h]
, or vice-versa, then

(X,Y)\in F_{\star}\Leftrightarrow\text{there is an edge between $X$ and $Y$ in $G$}.

(F.
4
)

Otherwise,
(X,Y)\in F_{\star}\Leftrightarrow\big(\mathcal{K}(X),\mathcal{K}(Y)\big)\in F
.

In order for
F_{\star}
to define a valid flip,
F_{\star}
has to be symmetric.
This is satisfied by our definition. The cases
(F.

1

)
and
(F.

2

)
are dual and for
(F.

3

)
and
(F.

4

)
the symmetry follows from the symmetry of their conditions and the symmetry of the edge relation and
F
.

We define
R_{\star}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{\star}^{2}
via the following three cases.

(R.
1
)

If
X\subseteq C[*,h+1]
and
Y\subseteq C[*,{\leqslant}h]
, or

(R.
2
)

if
X\subseteq C[*,h]
and
Y\subseteq C[*,h]
, then

(X,Y)\in R_{\star}\Leftrightarrow s_{>}(X)\in S(Y).

(R.
3
)

Otherwise,
(X,Y)\in R_{\star}\Leftrightarrow\big(\mathcal{K}(X),\mathcal{K}(Y)\big)\in R
.

Proving Properties of the Insulator.

Let
G^{\prime}:=G\oplus_{\mathcal{K}}F
and
G_{\star}:=G\oplus_{\mathcal{K}_{\star}}F_{\star}
.
We prove the various insulator properties.

Claim 10.7

(
(O.

1

)
)
.

Every two vertices in different rows of
C
have different colors in
\mathcal{K}_{\star}
.

Proof.

If none of the two vertices is in
C[*,h+1]
then the property holds as
\mathcal{K}_{\star}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{K}
, which satisfied this property in
B
.
Otherwise, exactly one of them is contained in
C[*,h+1]
and we can distinguish them using
(C.

4

)
.
∎

Claim 10.8

(
(O.

2

)
)
.

Every vertex
u\in C[i,r]
with
r>1
has a neighbor in the cell
C[i,r-1]
in
G_{\star}
.

Proof.

If
r\leqslant h
, then by
(O.

2

)
of
\mathcal{B}
, there is a vertex
v\in C[i,r-1]
that is adjacent to
u
in
G^{\prime}
.
By
(F.

4

)
,
u
and
v
are also adjacent in
G_{\star}
.

It remains to check the case where
u\in C[i,h+1]
.
By construction there exists a vertex
v\in C[i,h]
in the symmetric difference of
N(u)
and
N(s_{<}(u))
.
By
(C.

4

)
and
(C.

3

)
, we have
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u)\subseteq C[*,h+1]
and
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)\subseteq C[*,h]
.
Case
(F.

1

)
applies, and the following are equivalent.

1.

The adjacency between
u
and
v
got flipped from
G
to
G_{\star}
.

2.

s_{<}(\mathcal{K}(u))\in S(\mathcal{K}(v))
.
(by
(F.

1

)
)

3.

s_{<}(u)\in N(v)\cap S
.
(by definition)

4.

s_{<}(u)
is adjacent to
v
in
G
.
(by definition)

5.

u
is non-adjacent to
v
in
G
.
(
v
is in the sym. diff. of
N(s_{<}(u))
and
N(u)
)

The equivalence between the first and the last item establishes that
u
and
v
are adjacent in
G_{\star}
.
∎

Claim 10.9

(
(O.

3

)
)
.

For every
v\notin C
and
X\in\mathcal{K}_{\star}
,
v
is homogeneous to
X\cap\mathrm{int}(C)
in
G
.

Proof.

Let
X_{C}:=X\cap\mathrm{int}(C)
.
As
C
is an extension of
B
, we also have
v\notin B
.
By construction of
C
and
(C.

6

)
and
(C.

3

)
, we have either
X_{C}\subseteq\mathrm{int}(B)
or
X_{C}\subseteq C[*,h]=B[*,h]
.
In the first case we conclude by
(O.

3

)
of
\mathcal{B}
.
In the second case, since
v
did not get sorted into
C
, we know by construction

\mathrm{atp}(v/C[*,h])=\mathrm{atp}(s_{<}(v)/C[*,h]).

By
(C.

5

)
,
s_{<}(v)
is homogeneous to
X
, and so is
v
.
∎

In the following claims we prove
(O.

4

)
.
For this purpose let
u\in C[i,r]
for some
i\in I_{\star}
and
r<h+1
, and let
v\in C
.
Up to renaming, we additionally assume
I_{\star}=(1,\ldots,n)
.

Claim 10.10

(
(O.

4

)(O.4

.1

)
)
.

If
u\in\mathrm{int}(C)
and
u
and
v
are in rows that are not close, then they are non-adjacent in
G_{\star}
.

Proof.

Assume first
v\in C[*,h+1]
. Then
u\in C[*,{<}h]
, as
u
and
v
are in rows that are not close.
By construction of
C
this yields
u\in\mathrm{int}(B)

and
v\notin B
.
Using the properties of our coloring, we conclude the following.
Let
X:=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u)
and
Y:=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)
.

•

X\subseteq\mathcal{K}(X)\cap\mathrm{int}(B)
.
(by
(C.

1

)
and
(C.

6

)
)

•

No vertex of
Y
is contained in
B
.
(by
(C.

2

)
)

•

Every or no vertex in
Y
has a neighbor in
\mathcal{K}(X)\cap\mathrm{int}(B)
.
(by
(C.

9

)
)

Combining the above with
(O.

3

)
of
\mathcal{B}
, we know that the connection between
X
and
Y
is homogeneous in
G
.
Additionally,
X\subseteq C[*,{<}h]
and
Y\subseteq C[*,h+1]
so
case
(F.

3

)
applies and the following are equivalent.

1.

The adjacency between
u
and
v
got flipped from
G
to
G_{\star}
.

2.

There is an edge between
X
and
Y
in
G
. (by
(F.

3

)
)

3.

There is an edge between
u
and
v
in
G
. (as
X\ni u
and
Y\ni v
are homogeneous)

The equivalence between the first and the last item establishes that
u
and
v
are non-adjacent in
G_{\star}
.

If
v\notin C[*,h+1]
, then since also
u\notin C[*,h+1]
, case
(F.

4

)
applies and
u
and
v
have the same adjacency in
G_{\star}
as in
G^{\prime}
.
Note that in this case, by construction and
5.8
,
u
and
v

are both contained in both grids
A
and
B
and in both grids, they are in rows that are not close.
If
v\in C[*,h]
, then again
u\in\mathrm{int}(B)
and
u
and
v
non-adjacent in
G^{\prime}
by
(O.

4

)(O.4

.1

)
of
\mathcal{B}
.
Otherwise,
v\in C[*,{<}h]

and by
10.5
and
5.8
we have
v\in\mathrm{int}(A)
.
Now
u
and
v
non-adjacent in
G^{\prime}
by
(O.

4

)(O.4

.1

)
of
\mathcal{A}
.

∎

Claim 10.11

(
(O.

4

)(O.4

.2

)
)
.

If
v\in C[{<}i,r-1]\cup C[{>}i,r+1]
, then
u
and
v
are non-adjacent in
G_{\star}
.

Proof.

If at least one of
u
and
v
is contained in
C[*,{<}h]=B[*,{<}h]
, then case
(F.

4

)
applies and
u
and
v
have the same adjacency in
G_{\star}
as in
G^{\prime}
.
Possibly exchanging the roles of
u
and
v
, we can apply
(O.

4

)(O.4

.2

)
of
\mathcal{B}
to deduce that
u
and
v
are non-adjacent in
G^{\prime}
.

In the remaining case we have
u\in C[i,h]=B[i,h]
and
v\in C[{>}i,h+1]
and
by construction of
C
:

\mathrm{atp}(v/B[i,h])=\mathrm{atp}(s_{<}(v)/B[i,h]).

Also,
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u)\subseteq C[*,h]
and
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)\subseteq C[*,h+1]
by
(C.

3

)
and
(C.

4

)
.
Hence, case
(F.

2

)
applies, and the following are equivalent.

1.

The adjacency between
u
and
v
got flipped from
G
to
G_{\star}
.

2.

s_{<}(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v))\in S(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u))
.
(by
(F.

2

)
)

3.

s_{<}(v)\in N(u)\cap S
.
(by definition)

4.

u
and
s_{<}(v)
are adjacent in
G
.
(by definition)

5.

u
and
v
are adjacent in
G
.
(by
\mathrm{atp}(v/B[i,h])=\mathrm{atp}(s_{<}(v)/B[i,h])
)

The equivalence of the first and the last item establishes that
u
and
v
are non-adjacent in
G_{\star}
.

∎

Claim 10.12

(
(O.

4

)(O.4

.3

)
)
.

If
v\in C[{>}i+1,\{r,r-1\}]
, then
G\models E(u,v)\Leftrightarrow(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u),\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v))\in R_{\star}
.

Proof.

If either
u\in C[*,<h]
or
v\in C[*,<h]
, then case
(R.

3

)
applies,
we have

(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u),\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v))\in R_{\star}\Leftrightarrow(\mathcal{K}(u),\mathcal{K}(v))\in R,

and it remains to establish

G\models E(u,v)\Leftrightarrow(\mathcal{K}(u),\mathcal{K}(v))\in R.

If
u\in C[*,{<}h]
, we argue using
(O.

4

)(O.4

.3

)
of
\mathcal{B}
.
Otherwise, we have
v\in C[*,{<}h]
and argue using
(O.

4

)(O.4

.4

)
of
\mathcal{B}
, where we exchange the roles of
u
and
v
.

We can now assume
u,v\in C[*,h]
.
By
(C.

3

)
, also
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u),\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)\subseteq C[*,h]
, and case
(R.

2

)
applies.
By assumption, we have
v\in C[i^{\prime},h]
for
i+1<i^{\prime}
.
By
10.6
, we have that
\mathrm{ex}(u)+1<i^{\prime}
, and the following are equivalent.

1.

u
and
v
are adjacent in
G
.

2.

s_{>}(u)
and
v
are adjacent in
G
.
(by the sampling property)

3.

s_{>}(u)\in N(v)\cap S
.
(by definition)

4.

s_{>}(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u))\in S(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v))
.
(by definition)

5.

(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u),\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v))\in R_{\star}
.
(by
(R.

2

)
)

∎

Claim 10.13

(
(O.

4

)(O.4

.4

)
)
.

If
v\in C[{<}i-1,\{r,r+1\}]
, then
G\models E(u,v)\Leftrightarrow(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v),\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u))\in R_{\star}
.

Proof.

If either

•

one of
u
and
v
is contained in
C[*,{<}h]
, or

•

both
u
and
v
are contained in
C[*,h]
,

then we can exchange
u
and
v
and the property follows from the already established property
(O.

4

)(O.4

.3

)
.
It remains to prove the case where
u\in C[i,h]
and
v\in C[{<}i-1,h+1]
.
We have
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u)\subseteq C[*,h]
and
\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)\subseteq C[*,h+1]
, and case
(R.

1

)
applies for
X=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)
and
Y=\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v)
.
By assumption, we have
v\in C[i^{\prime},h+1]
for
i^{\prime}+1<i
.
By
10.6
, we have that
\mathrm{ex}(v)+1<i
, and the following are equivalent.

1.

u
and
v
are adjacent in
G
.

2.

u
and
s_{>}(v)
are adjacent in
G
.
(by the sampling property)

3.

s_{>}(v)\in N(u)\cap S
.
(by definition)

4.

s_{>}(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v))\in S(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u))
.
(by definition)

5.

(\mathcal{K}_{\star}(v),\mathcal{K}_{\star}(u))\in R_{\star}
.
(by
(R.

1

)
)

∎

This proves property
(O.

4

)
.
Finally, property
(O.

5

)
only concerns the first row
C[*,1]=B[*,1]
, so its truth carries over from
\mathcal{B}
.
Having proven all properties, it follows that
\mathcal{C}
is an insulator.
Its cost is
|\mathcal{K}_{\star}|=\mathrm{const}(k,t)
.
This proves that
\mathcal{C}
is the desired row extension.
It remains to analyze the running time.

Running Time.

Let
n:=|V(G)|
.

Lemma

9.5
runs in time
O_{t}(n^{2})
.
Similarly as in the proof of
Lemma

7.6
,
we can build the row-extension
C
of
B
in time
O_{t}(n^{2})
.
The construction of
\mathcal{K}_{\star}
,
F_{\star}
, and
R_{\star}
runs in time
O_{k,t}(n)
.
This yields an overall running time of
O_{k,t}(n^{2})
.
∎

Having proven the insulator growing lemmas, this concludes
Part

I
.

Part II
Non-Structure

In
Part

I
, we have shown that for any graph class the following implications hold:

\text{prepattern-free}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{insulation-property}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{flip-breakable}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{mon.\ dependent}

In
Part

II
, we close the circle of implications by showing:

\text{not prepattern-free}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{large flipped crossings/comparability grids}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{mon.\ independent}

The main goal of
Part

II
is to prove the following.

Proposition 10.14
.

Let
G
be a graph containing a prepattern of order
n
on an insulator of height
h
and cost
k
.
Then
G
contains as an induced subgraph either

•

a flipped star
r
-crossing of order
m
, or

•

a flipped clique
r
-crossing of order
m
, or

•

a flipped half-graph
r
-crossing of order
m
, or

•

the comparability grid of order
m
,

for some
m\geqslant U_{h,k}(n)
and
1\leqslant r<8h
.

This is achieved in
Section

11
and
Section

12
. Additionally, in
Section

12.6
we prove that classes
which exhibit large patterns as listed in
Proposition

10.14
are monadically independent.
Finally, in
Section

13
, we wrap up the proof of the main results of the paper.

Convention:

We often write that some property or function
P(\bar{x})

“is the same for all tuples
\bar{x}
from a given domain.”

This means that for all
\bar{x},\bar{y}
from a specified domain,
P(\bar{x})=P(\bar{y})
.

11
Transformers

As a first step, in Section
11
, starting from large prepatterns, we will extract well-structured, but rather abstract objects called
transformers
.
After that, in Section
12
, transformers will be analyzed at a low level, and crossings will be extracted from them.

11.1
Meshes and Transformers

Definition 11.1
.

A
mesh
in a graph
G

is an injective function
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
,
where
I
and
J
are two non-empty indexing sequences of the same length.
We denote
V(M):=\{M(i,j):i\in I,j\in J\}
.
For a mesh
M
as above, by
M^{\mathsf{T}}
denote the mesh
M^{\mathsf{T}}\colon J\times I\to V(G)
such that
M^{\mathsf{T}}(i,j)=M(j,i)
for all
i\in I,j\in J
.
A mesh
M
has
order

m
if
|I|=|J|=m
.

For
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
,
by
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
we denote the mesh
obtained by restricting
M
to
I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}
.
We call
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
a
submesh
of
M
.

Definition 11.2
.

Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a mesh in a (possibly colored) graph
G
.
Then
M
is
vertical
in
G
if
|I|=|J|\leqslant 3
, or if there is a function
a\colon I\to V(G)

such that

•

\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J
, and

•

\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J
.

We say that
M
is
horizontal
in
G
if
M^{{\mathsf{T}}}
is vertical in
G
.
Note that a mesh can be both horizontal and vertical.

Definition 11.3
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be meshes in a (possibly colored) graph
G
. We say that in
G
the pair
(M,M^{\prime})
is

•

regular
if

\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))

depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})

for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
,

•

homogeneous

if
\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is the same

for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
,

•

conducting
if either
|I|=|J|\leqslant 3
or
(M,M^{\prime})
is regular but not homogeneous in
G
.

Definition 11.4
.

A
conductor
of order
n
and length
h
is a sequence

M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
of meshes of order
n
,
such that each pair
(M_{s},M_{s+1})
is conducting for
s=1,\ldots,h-1
.

We now define the central notion of
Section

11
.

Definition 11.5
.

A
transformer
of order
n
and length
h
is a conductor

M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
of order
n
and length
h
,
such that
M_{1}
is vertical, and
M_{h}
is horizontal.

The main result of
Section

11
is the following.

Proposition 11.6
.

Let
G
be a graph containing a prepattern of order
n
on an insulator of height
h
and cost
k
.
Then
G
contains a transformer of order
U_{h,k}(n)
and length at most
4h-1
.

We start with some simple observations.

Observation 11.7
.

Let
M
be a mesh in a graph
G
.
If
M
is horizontal/vertical in
G
then this also holds for every submesh of
M
.

Observation 11.8
.

Let
M_{1},M_{2}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be meshes in a graph
G
such that the pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
is conducting in
G
.
For all
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|
the pair
(M_{1}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},M_{2}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}})
is also conducting in
G
.

Lemma 11.9
.

Let
G
be a graph and
G^{+}
be a coloring of
G
.
If a pair of meshes
(M,M^{\prime})
is conducting in
G^{+}
then it is also conducting in
G
.

Proof.

Obviously the pair
(M,M^{\prime})
is also regular in
G
.
Via contrapositive, it remains to assume that

(M,M^{\prime})
is homogeneous in
G
,
and show that it also is homogeneous in
G^{+}
.
To this end, observe that the atomic type
\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))

depends only on
\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))

as well as the colors of
M(i,j)
and
M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
The former has the desired properties by homogeneity in
G
,
and the latter by regularity in
G^{+}
.
∎

Lemma 11.10
.

Let
G
be a graph and
G^{+}
be a coloring of
G
. If a mesh
M
is vertical in
G^{+}
then it is also vertical in
G
.

Proof.

Let
a(\cdot)
be the function witnessing that
M
is vertical in
G^{+}
.
We argue as in the proof of
Lemma

11.9
.
Here, instead of regularity, we use verticality to argue that all vertices in the range of
a
(respectively all vertices in the range of
M
) have the same atomic type in
G^{+}
.
∎

11.2
From Prepatterns to Transformers

In this section, we prove
Proposition

11.6
,
extracting transformers from prepatterns.
Let us first give an overview of the proof in the case where
G
contains a bi-prepattern.
The mono-prepattern case later falls out as a subcase.
The bi-prepattern in
G
is witnessed by an insulator
\mathcal{A}
whose columns are indexed by a sequence
K
containing two subsequences
I
and
J
. Every pair of columns
(i,j)\in I\times J
is “matched up” by an element
c_{i,j}
through quantifier-free formulas (cf.
Definition

6.1
).

Fig.

12
is a schematic depiction of how the transformer will embed into the insulator
\mathcal{A}
.
The transformer will be assembled from two conductors
C=M_{0},\ldots,M_{t}
and
C^{\prime}=M^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,M^{\prime}_{t}
.
The columns of the meshes of
C
and
C^{\prime}
are contained in the columns of the insulator
\mathcal{A}
indexed by
I
and
J
, respectively.
The structure of
\mathcal{A}
imposes that
M_{t}
and
M^{\prime}_{t}
are both vertical.
The meshes
M_{0}
and
M^{\prime}_{0}
are chosen from the vertices
c_{i,j}
such that
M_{0}={M^{\prime}_{0}}^{\mathsf{T}}
.
It follows that we can glue the conductor
C
to the transposed meshes from
{C^{\prime}}
yielding the desired transformer

M_{t},M_{t-1},\ldots,M_{1},M_{0}=M_{0}^{\prime{\mathsf{T}}},M_{1}^{\prime{\mathsf{T}}},\ldots,M_{t-1}^{\prime{\mathsf{T}}},M_{t}^{\prime{\mathsf{T}}},

where
M_{t}
is vertical and
M_{t}^{\prime{\mathsf{T}}}
is horizontal.

The construction of
C
and
C^{\prime}
is implemented by
Lemma

11.16
or
Lemma

11.18
(
\clubsuit_{1}
or
\clubsuit_{2}
in the picture) depending on the choice of
\sim_{1}
and
\sim_{2}
in the definition of a bi-prepattern.

Lemma

11.16
iteratively extends the conductor by “stepping down” the insulator.
A single step is performed using
Lemma

11.12
(
\blacklozenge
), which creates meshes
embedded into
descending rows of the insulator (cf.
Definition

11.11
).
The structure of the insulator is used to establish conductivity between successive meshes/rows.
This process continues until either a vertical mesh is produced or we reach the bottom row of the insulator.
If we reach the bottom row, we use
Lemma

11.15
(
\spadesuit
) to further extend the conductor to reach a vertical mesh.
Here we use the special structure of the bottom row: In the unordered case, the cells how the bottom row contain only a single vertex each, which implies that the mesh in the bottom row is already vertical.
In the ordered case the mesh in the bottom row is
connected through short parts to a vertical mesh and we can bridge those short paths by a conductor.
This finishes the sketch of
Lemma

11.16
(
\clubsuit_{1}
).

Lemma

11.18
(
\clubsuit_{2}
) is a rather technical case distinction which reduces the construction of the conductor to some previously established subcase.

Figure 12:
Constructing a transformer from a bi-prepattern.

This concludes the overview of the proof of
Proposition

11.6
. We now give a detailed proof.

Definition 11.11
.

Let
G
be a graph containing an insulator
\mathcal{A}
of height
h
with grid
A
and indexed by a sequence
K
.
Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a mesh of order
n
in
G
with
I\subseteq K
.
Let
r\in[h]
.
We say that
M

embeds into row
r
of
\mathcal{A}
if for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
.

M(i,j)\in A[i,r].

Lemma 11.12

(
\blacklozenge
in
Fig.

12
)
.

Let
G
be a graph containing an insulator
\mathcal{A}
of height
h
with grid
A
and indexed by a sequence
K
.
Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a mesh of order
n
in
G
with
I\subseteq K
.
Let
\alpha(x,y)
be a quantifier-free formula in a
k
-coloring
G^{+}
of
G
,
and let
r\in[h]
be such that for all
i\in I,j\in J

\displaystyle i=\min\{i^{\prime}\in I:\exists v\in A[i^{\prime},{\leqslant}r]\ :\ G^{+}\models\alpha(v,M(i,j))\}.

(7)

Then there are meshes
M_{1},M_{2}
of order
U_{k,h}(n)

such that

•

M_{1}
is a submesh of
M
, and

•

the pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
is conducting in
G
, and

•

M_{2}
is vertical in
G
or

M_{2}
embeds into some row
r^{\prime}\leqslant r
of
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Note that by
Lemma

11.9
and
Lemma

11.10
, it does not matter whether we show conductivity and verticality in
G
or
G^{+}
.
For every
i\in I
and
j\in J
, pick a vertex
M^{\prime}(i,j)\in A[i,\leqslant r]

such that
G^{+}\models\alpha(M^{\prime}(i,j),M(i,j))
.
Note that
M
is not necessarily a mesh, as it may not be injective.

Claim 11.13
.

There are sequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J

with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|\geqslant U_{k,h}(|I|)=U_{k,h}(n)
, and
a row number
r^{\prime}\in[r]

such that

(R.
1
)

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M^{\prime}(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))

depends
only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})

for
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, and

(R.
2
)

M^{\prime}(i,j)\in A[i,r^{\prime}]
for all
i\in I^{\prime}
.

The proof of the claim is a straightforward application of Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
).
Readers experienced in Ramsey theory are invited to skip it.
However, due to the importance of Ramsey type arguments for this section, we include the details of an exemplary application here.

Proof of
11.13
.

Up to renaming we can assume
I=J=[n]
.
Let
\Pi
be the set of possible atomic types of four tuples in
k
-colored graphs.
There exists a constant
k^{*}\leqslant\mathrm{const}(k,h)
such that there is a bijection
b\colon\Pi\times[r]\to[k^{*}]
.
We now define the coloring
c\colon[n]^{2}\times[n]^{2}\to[k^{*}]
as

c\bigl((i,i^{\prime}),(j,j^{\prime})\bigr):=b\Bigl(\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}\bigl(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M^{\prime}(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\bigr),\ r^{\prime}\Bigr)

where
r^{\prime}
is the unique row such that
M^{\prime}(i,j)\in A[i,r^{\prime}]
.
Applying Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
) to the defined coloring yields sequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J

with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|\geqslant U_{k,h}(|I|)=U_{k,h}(n)

such that
c\bigl((i,i^{\prime}),(j,j^{\prime})\bigr)
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})

for
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
By the construction of the coloring, this proves
(R.

1

)
.
To see that the row containing
M^{\prime}(i,j)
is the same for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
, notice that

c\bigl((i,i),(j,j)\bigr)=c\bigl((i^{\prime},i^{\prime}),(j^{\prime},j^{\prime})\bigr)\quad\text{for all $i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}$ and $j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}$.}

Therefore,
M(i,j)
and
M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are in the same row,
which proves
(R.

2

)
.
∎

Let
I^{\prime}
,
J^{\prime}
, and
r^{\prime}
be as from the previous claim. We set
M_{1}:=M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
and
M_{2}:=M^{\prime}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
.

Claim 11.14
.

Either
M_{1}
is vertical, or

M_{2}
is a mesh (that is,
M_{2}
is injective).

Proof.

Assume
|I^{\prime}|>3
, as otherwise
M_{1}
is vertical by definition.
Assume that
M_{2}
is not injective.
Then there exist distinct pairs
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}
such that
M_{2}(i,j)=M_{2}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
Since
M_{2}(i,j)
(equivalently
M_{2}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
) is from the
i
th (equivalently
i^{\prime}
th) column of
A
, we have that
i=i^{\prime}
. Hence,
j\neq j^{\prime}
.

By
(R.

1

)
we have
a(i):=M_{2}(i,j)=M_{2}(i,j^{\prime})
for all
i\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Now also by
(R.

1

)
,

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(M_{1}(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J^{\prime}
.
Finally, by (
7
) and
|I^{\prime}|>3
,
\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(M_{1}(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
is not the same
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J^{\prime}
.
In summary:
a(\cdot)
witnesses that
M_{1}
is vertical.
∎

Assume now the mesh
M_{1}
is vertical.
By
(R.

1

)
, the pair
(M_{1},M_{1})
is regular.
As witnessed by the equality type, the pair is not homogeneous and therefore conducting.
We can return
(M_{1},M_{1})
.

Otherwise,
M_{2}
is a mesh and
embeds into the row
r^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}
by
(R.

2

)
.
Furthermore, the pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
is conducting: regularity follows from
(R.

1

)
and non-homogeneity from (
7
).
∎

Lemma 11.15

(
\spadesuit
in
Fig.

12
)
.

Let
G
be a graph containing an insulator

\mathcal{A}
of cost
k
and height
h
and let
M
be a mesh of order
n
that embeds into row
1
of
\mathcal{A}
.
There exists a conductor

M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}
for
t\leqslant h
of order
U_{k,h}(n)
such that
M_{1}
is a submesh of
M
and
M_{t}
is vertical.

Proof.

Let
I,J
be the sequences indexing the mesh
M
.
Let
A
be the grid of
\mathcal{A}
.
Assume first that
\mathcal{A}
is orderless.
Then by the insulator property
(U.

1

)
, each cell in row
1
of
\mathcal{A}
contains only a single vertex. As
M
embeds into this row, it has order
1
. Then
M
is vertical and the conductor consisting only of
M
satisfies the conditions of the lemma.

Assume now that
\mathcal{A}
is ordered.
By
(O.

5

)
, there exists a
k
-flip
H
of
G
and some radius
r<h
such that the radius-
r
balls around the vertices in
A[*,1]
are pairwise disjoint.
Moreover, there are vertices

\{b(v)\in N_{r}^{H}[v]:v\in A[*,1]\}
and

\{c_{i}\in V(G):i\in I\}

and a symbol
{\sim}\in\{{\leqslant},{\geqslant}\}
such that for all
i,j\in I
and
v\in A[j,1]

G\models E(c_{i},b(v))

if and only if

i\sim j
.

Let
G^{+}
be the
k
-coloring of
G
in which the edge relation of the flipped graph
H
can be expressed by a quantifier-free formula.
For each
i\in I
and
j\in J
let
\pi(i,j)
be the tuple of vertices forming a shortest path from
M(i,j)
to
b(M(i,j))
in
H
.
By
(O.

5

)
, these paths have equal length for all
i,j
and consist of at most
h
vertices.
By Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
),
there exist sequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq I
of length at least
U_{k,h}(n)
such that

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(\pi(i,j),c(i),\pi(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),c(i^{\prime}))

depends
only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})

for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.

For distinct pairs
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
in
I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}
,
\pi(i,j)
and
\pi(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
have no vertex in common, as they stem from two disjoint balls in
H
.
Therefore, each of the functions
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}\colon I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}\to V(G)
, where
M_{t}(i,j)
is defined as the
t
th component of
\pi(i,j)
, is injective and forms a mesh.
By construction,
M_{1}
is a submesh of
M
.
By Ramsey and
(O.

5

)
,
M_{h}
is vertical.
We next show that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
is a conductor in
G^{+}
.
By Ramsey, any pair of successive meshes
(M_{t},M_{t+1})
in the sequence is regular in
G^{+}
. It remains to show that
(M_{t},M_{t+1})
is not homogeneous in
G^{+}
.
Consider two distinct pairs
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}
.
As
\pi(i,j)
and
\pi(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are constructed via paths through disjoint balls in
H
, we have that

M_{t}(i,j)
is adjacent to
M_{t+1}(i,j)
and non-adjacent to
M_{t+1}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
in
H
.
It follows that
(M_{t},M_{t+1})
is not homogeneous in
G^{+}
.
Therefore,
(M_{t},M_{t+1})
is conducting in
G^{+}
.
Hence,
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
is a conductor in
G^{+}
, and by
Lemma

11.9
and
Lemma

11.10
also in
G
.
∎

Lemma 11.16

(
\clubsuit_{1}
in
Fig.

12
)
.

Let
G
be a graph containing an insulator
\mathcal{A}
of cost
k
and height
h
with grid
A
and indexed by a sequence
K
.
Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a mesh of order
n
in
G
with
I\subseteq K
.
Let
\alpha(x,y)
be a quantifier-free formula in a
k
-coloring
G^{+}
of
G
such that for all
i\in I,j\in J

i=\min\{i^{\prime}\in I:\exists v\in A[i^{\prime},*]\ :\ G^{+}\models\alpha(v,M(i,j))\}.

Then there is a conductor
M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}
of order
U_{k,h}(n)
and length at most
2h
in
G
such that
M_{1}
is a submesh of
M
and
M_{t}
is vertical in
G
.

Proof.

Note that by
Lemma

11.9
and
Lemma

11.10
, it does not matter whether we show conductivity and verticality in
G
or
G^{+}
.
Denote
M_{0}:=M
and
r_{0}:=h+1
.
We inductively construct a sequence of meshes
M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{t}

where for each
s=1,2,\ldots,t
, the mesh
M_{s}
satisfies the following conditions:

•

M_{s}
has order
U_{s,k,h}(n)
,

•

there is a row
r_{s}\in[r_{s-1}-1]

such that

M_{s}
embeds into row
r_{s}
of
\mathcal{A}
,

•

there is a submesh
M_{s-1}^{\prime}
of
M_{s-1}
such that the pair
(M_{s-1}^{\prime},M_{s})
is conducting.

To construct
M_{1}
, we apply
Lemma

11.12
to the mesh
M_{0}
and the formula
\alpha(x,y)
to obtain
a conducting pair of meshes
(M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1})
of order
U_{k,h}(n)

where
M_{0}^{\prime}
is a submesh of
M_{0}
.
If
M_{1}
is vertical then
M_{0}^{\prime},M_{1}
is the desired conductor, and we conclude the proof of the lemma.
Otherwise,
M_{1}
embeds into some row
r_{1}\in[h]
of
\mathcal{A}
and satisfies the induction hypothesis.

Suppose the sequence
M_{1},\ldots,M_{s}
has been constructed for some
s\geqslant 1
.
Below we either extend the sequence by one, or terminate the process.

Assume first
r_{s}>1
.
As the sequence
r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{s}
is strictly decreasing we have
s<h
.
Let
\beta(x,y)
be the formula expressing adjacency in the flip
G^{\prime}
associated to
\mathcal{A}
. As
G^{\prime}
is a
k
-flip of
G
,
\beta
is expressible in a
k
-coloring of
G
.
Let
I_{s}
and
J_{s}
be the sequences indexing
M_{s}
.
For every
i_{0}<i\in I_{s}
and
j\in J_{s}
, we have that
M_{s}(i,j)
has a
\beta
-neighbor in
A[i,r_{s}-1]
but no
\beta
-neighbor in
A[i_{0},r_{s}-1]
.
This is by the insulator property
(U.

1

)
if
\mathcal{A}
is orderless and by
(O.

2

)
and
(O.

4

)(O.4

.2

)
if
\mathcal{A}
is ordered.
Hence, we can apply
Lemma

11.12
to
the mesh
M_{s}
, the row number
r_{s}-1
, and
the formula
\beta(x,y)
.
We obtain
a conducting pair of meshes
(M_{s}^{\prime},M_{s+1})
of order
U_{s,k,h}(n)

where
M_{s}^{\prime}
is a submesh of
M_{s}
.
If
M_{s+1}
is vertical then we conclude the proof of the lemma returning the conductor

M_{0}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},M_{1}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},\ldots,M_{s}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},M_{s+1}

of length at most
h+1
, where
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
are the indexing sequences of
M_{s+1}
.
Otherwise,
M_{s+1}
embeds into some row
r_{s+1}\in[r_{s}-1]
of
\mathcal{A}
and satisfies the induction hypothesis. We continue the process.

Assume now
r_{s}=1
. Note that
s\leqslant h
.
Then
M_{s}
embeds into the first row of
\mathcal{A}
, and we can apply
Lemma

11.15
.
This yields a conductor
M_{1}^{\star},\ldots,M_{t^{\star}}^{\star}
of length
t^{\star}\leqslant h

indexed by sequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
of length
U_{s,k,h}(n)
such that
M_{1}^{\star}
is a submesh of
M_{s}
, and
M_{t^{\star}}^{\star}
is vertical.
Now we conclude the proof of the lemma returning the following conductor of length at most
2h
:

M_{0}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},\ldots,M_{s-1}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},M^{\star}_{1},\ldots,M^{\star}_{t^{\star}}.\qed

Lemma 11.17
.

Assume
G
contains a mono-prepattern of order
n
on an insulator
\mathcal{A}
of cost
k
and height
h
.
Then
G
contains a transformer of order
U_{k,h}(n)
and length at most
2h
.

Proof.

Let
(c_{j}:j\in J)
and
(b_{i,j}:i\in I,j\in J)

form a mono-prepattern of order
n=|I|=|J|
on the insulator
\mathcal{A}
.
Define the function
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
where
M(i,j)=b_{i,j}
.
By definition of a mono-prepattern, all the
b_{i,j}
are distinct, so
M
is a mesh.
By Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
) and
Definition

6.2
,
there exist sequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq I
of length at least
U(n)
such that

\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),c_{j^{\prime}})
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
for all
i\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),c_{j^{\prime}})
is not the same for all
i\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
, the submesh
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is horizontal.

We apply
Lemma

11.16
to the mesh
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
and formula
\alpha(x,y):=(x=y)
.
This yields a conductor

M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}
of length at most
2h
and order
U_{h,k}(n)
such that
M_{1}
is a submesh of
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
, and
M_{t}
is vertical.
As
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is horizontal, the same holds for
M_{1}
, and thus
M_{t},\ldots,M_{1}
is the desired transformer.
∎

Lemma

11.16
is complemented by the following more technical
Lemma

11.18
.
Together, these two lemmas accommodate the two possible choices for the symbols
\sim_{1},\sim_{2}\in\{=,\neq\}
in the definition of a bi-prepattern (
Definition

6.1
).

Lemma 11.18

(
\clubsuit_{2}
in
Fig.

12
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an insulator of cost
k
and height
h
indexed by a sequence
K
, let
I,J
be indexing sequences with
I\subseteq K
,
let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a mesh of order
n
, and let
\alpha
be a quantifier-free formula in a
k
-coloring
G^{+}
of
G
,
such that for all
i\in I,j\in J

i=\min\{i^{\prime}\in I:\neg\exists v\in A[i^{\prime},*]\ :\ G^{+}\models\alpha(v,M(i,j))\}.

Then there is a conductor
M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}
of order
U_{k,h}(n)
and length at most
2h
such that
either

•

M_{1}
is a submesh of
M
and
M_{t}
is vertical in
G
, or

•

M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}
is a transformer in
G
.

Proof.

For all
i^{*},i\in I
with
i^{*}<i
and
j\in J
,
fix a vertex
F(i^{*},i,j)\in A[i^{*},*]
such that

G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i,j),M(i,j))

By Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
), there are sets
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|\geqslant U(|I|)
,
such that for all
i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
with
i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}
, and for all
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
,

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))\quad\text{depends only on $\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})$ and $\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})$}.

(
*
)

In particular this holds for the fact whether
G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))
.
For an exemplary application of Bipartite Ramsey that illustrates how to obtain (

∗

‣
11.2
), see the proof of
11.13
.
Denote

•

i_{\min}:=\min(I^{\prime})
,

•

i_{\max}:=\max(I^{\prime})
,

•

i^{\prime}_{\max}:=\max(I^{\prime}\setminus i_{\max})
,

•

j_{\min}:=\min(J^{\prime})
,

•

j_{\max}:=\max(J^{\prime})
,

•

j^{\prime}_{\max}:=\max(J^{\prime}\setminus j_{\max})
.

For convenience, we redefine
I:=I^{\prime}\setminus\{i_{\min},i_{\max},i^{\prime}_{\max}\}
,
and
J:=J^{\prime}\setminus\{i_{\min},i_{\max},i^{\prime}_{\max}\}
.
We do so to ensure that all elements in
I
and
J
have the same order type
with respect to the previously chosen extremal elements.

Figure 13:
A depiction of
Case 1
. On top: the desired vertical submesh of
M
. On the bottom: the insulator
\mathcal{A}
, whose columns contain the
F(i^{*},i,j)
vertices (depicted as
\bullet
).
Here, the outermost columns correspond to
i^{*}
-coordinates of
F(i^{*},i,j)
, and
within each outermost column, the inner columns and rows correspond to

i
- and
j
-coordinates, respectively.
The
F(i^{*},i,j)
vertices are only defined for indices
i^{*}<i
,
thus going rightwards the columns are filled up with placeholders (depicted as
\circ
).

Case 1:

Assume

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))
is not the same for all

i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}
and
j<j^{\prime}
(here
j<j^{\prime}
is the crucial assumption).
In this case, we work towards proving that
M
contains a large vertical submesh.
The situation is depicted in
Fig.

13
.
We set
a({i^{\prime}}):=F(i_{\min},i^{\prime},j_{\max})
for all
i^{\prime}\in I
.
Let us now argue that
\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(a(i^{\prime}),M(i,j))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J
.

By assumption,
there exist indices

•

i_{1}^{*},i_{1},i_{1}^{\prime}\in I
and
j_{1},j_{1}^{\prime}\in J
with
i_{1}^{*}<i_{1},i_{1}^{\prime}
and
j_{1}<j_{1}^{\prime}
, and

•

i_{2}^{*},i_{2},i_{2}^{\prime}\in I
and
j_{2},j_{2}^{\prime}\in J
with
i_{2}^{*}<i_{2},i_{2}^{\prime}
and
j_{2}<j_{2}^{\prime}

such that

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i_{1}^{*},i^{\prime}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}),M(i_{1},j_{1}))\neq\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i_{2}^{*},i^{\prime}_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}),M(i_{2},j_{2})).

As we removed the extremal elements from
I
and
J
, we have

•

\mathrm{otp}(i_{1}^{*},i_{1},i_{1}^{\prime})=\mathrm{otp}(i_{\min},i_{1},i_{1}^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j_{1},j_{1}^{\prime})=\mathrm{otp}(j_{1},j_{\max})
, as well as

•

\mathrm{otp}(i_{2}^{*},i_{2},i_{2}^{\prime})=\mathrm{otp}(i_{\min},i_{2},i_{2}^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j_{2},j_{2}^{\prime})=\mathrm{otp}(j_{2},j_{\max})
.

Now applying (

∗

‣
11.2
), we get

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(\underbrace{F(i_{\min},i^{\prime}_{1},j_{\max}}_{a(i^{\prime}_{1})}),M(i_{1},j_{1}))\neq\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(\underbrace{F(i_{\min},i^{\prime}_{2},j_{\max}}_{a(i^{\prime}_{2})}),M(i_{2},j_{2})).

Thus,
\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(a(i^{\prime}),M(i,j))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J
.
Additionally, as
j<j_{\max}
for all
j\in J
, we have that
\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(a(i^{\prime}),M(i,j))
only depends on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and no longer on
j
, for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J
.
It follows that
M_{I\times J}
is vertical and forms the desired conductor (in this case of length one).

Case 2:

Assume

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))
is not the same for all

i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}
and
j>j^{\prime}
(here
j>j^{\prime}
is the crucial assumption).
We proceed as in the previous case, but with
j_{\min}
instead of
j_{\max}
.

Figure 14:
A depiction of the mono-prepattern we discover in
Case 3
. In this example, we have that
G\models E(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))\Leftrightarrow j=j^{\prime}
.

Case 3:

Assume

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))
is not the same for all

i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i^{*}<i^{\prime}<i
(here
i^{\prime}<i
is the crucial assumption).
Now let

c(j):=M(i_{\max},j)\quad
and
\quad b(i^{*},j^{\prime}):=F(i^{*},i_{\max}^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in A[i^{*},*]\quad
for all
i^{*}\in I
and
j\in J
.

Our goal is to show that the ranges of
b(\cdot,\cdot)
and
c(\cdot)
form a mono-prepattern on
\mathcal{A}
.
The situation is depicted in
Fig.

14
.
As in
Case 1
, we argue via (

∗

‣
11.2
) that for all
i^{*}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J

(A.
1
)

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
, and

(A.
2
)

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
is not the same.

We claim that for all
i^{*}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
then also

(E.
1
)

G\models E(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
, and

(E.
2
)

G\models E(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
is not the same.

By definition of an atomic type,
(A.

1

)
implies
(E.

1

)
.
To show
(E.

2

)
we argue similarly to the proof of
Lemma

11.9
:
We know that there are indices

i^{*}_{1},i^{*}_{2}\in I
and
j_{1},j^{\prime}_{1},j_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}\in J
and atomic types
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
such that

\tau_{1}=\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(\underbrace{b(i^{*}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}),c(j_{1})}_{\bar{u}})\neq\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(\underbrace{b(i^{*}_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}),c(j_{2})}_{\bar{v}})=\tau_{2}.

By (

∗

‣
11.2
), all
b(\cdot,\cdot)
elements have the same atomic type in
G^{+}
and all
c(\cdot)
elements have the same atomic type in
G^{+}
.
This means that the difference in the atomic types of
\bar{v}
and
\bar{v}^{\prime}
must be caused by either a difference in their equality or adjacency type.
If the difference is witnessed in the equality type, then by symmetry we can assume that

b(i^{*}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1})=c(j_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad b(i^{*}_{2},j^{\prime}_{2})\neq c(j_{2}).

It follows that
b(i^{*},j^{\prime}_{1})=c(j_{1})
for all
i^{*}\in I
, since all of these pairs have the same order type
\mathrm{otp}(j^{\prime}_{1},j_{1})
.
This is a contradiction to the columns of
\mathcal{A}
being disjoint.
Therefore, the difference in the types of
\bar{u}
and
\bar{v}
must be witnessed by their adjacency type and we have

G\models E(b(i^{*}_{1},j^{\prime}_{1}),c(j_{1}))\quad\text{if and only if}\quad G\not\models E(b(i^{*}_{2},j^{\prime}_{2}),c(j_{2})),

which proves
(E.

2

)
.

Having proven
(E.

1

)
and
(E.

2

)
it is easily verified that the ranges of
b(\cdot,\cdot)
and
c(\cdot)
form a mono-prepattern on
\mathcal{A}
.
Applying
Lemma

11.17
to this mono-prepattern yields the desired transformer.

Case 4:

Assume

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))
is not the same for all

i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i^{*}<i<i^{\prime}
(here
i<i^{\prime}
is the crucial assumption).
We argue as in the previous case, exchanging the role of
i_{\max}
and
i_{\max}^{\prime}
.
This means we define

c(j):=M(i_{\max}^{\prime},j)\quad
and
\quad b(i^{*},j^{\prime}):=F(i^{*},i_{\max},j^{\prime})\in A[i^{*},*]\quad
for all
i^{*}\in I
and
j\in J
.

As in
Case 3
, the ranges of
b(\cdot,\cdot)
and
c(\cdot)
form a mono-prepattern on
\mathcal{A}
and
we conclude by
Lemma

11.17
.

Case 5:

If none of the previous cases hold, then

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))
and in particular
G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))

is the same for all

i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

and
i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}
.
By (

∗

‣
11.2
), this also holds for the extremal elements that are in
I^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}
, but not in
I
and
J
.
Let hence
\gamma\in\{\mathit{true},\mathit{false}\}

be such that

G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))\iff\gamma

for all

i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

and
i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}
.

Figure 15:
Illustration for
Case 5.1
.
Depicted is the insulator
\mathcal{A}
, whose columns contain the
F(i^{*},i,j)
vertices.
The
F(i^{*},i,j)
vertices are only defined for indices satisfying
i^{*}<i
, so going rightwards, the columns are filled up with placeholders (depicted as
\circ
).
The vertices in the
i
th column of the submesh
M|_{I\times J}
are
\alpha
-connected to all the
F(i^{*},i,j)
vertices in
A[{<}i,*]
(marked in red), but
\neg\alpha
-connected to all the
F(i^{*},i,j)
vertices in
A[i,*]
(marked in blue).

Case 5.1:

Assume
\gamma=\mathit{true}
.
At the beginning of the proof, we chose
F
such that

G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i,j),M(i,j))
.
Therefore,

G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))\quad\text{ for all }i^{*},i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}\text{ with }i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}\text{ and }j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}.

(8)

Our goal is to reduce to
Lemma

11.16
(
\clubsuit_{1}
).
The situation is depicted in
Fig.

15
.
Let
P=\{F(i^{*},i,j)\mid i^{*},i\in I^{\prime},j\in J^{\prime}\text{ with }i^{*}<i\}
and let
G^{\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}}
be the coloring of
G^{+}
where the vertices of
P
are marked with an additional fresh color predicate.
(By the definition of colored graphs given in the preliminaries,
we are technically required to give every vertex
exactly
one color,
and it would of course be trivial, though more cumbersome, to take this into account.)
Define the quantifier-free formula
\beta(x,y):=x\in P\wedge\neg\alpha(x,y)
in the signature of
G^{\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}}
.
Let us now verify that

i=\min\{i^{\prime}\in I:\exists v\in A[i^{\prime},*]\ :\ G^{\mathbin{{+}\mspace{-8mu}{+}}}\models\beta(v,M(i,j))\}

(9)

for all
i\in I,j\in J
.
As all elements in
I
are smaller than
i_{\max}
,
for every
i\in I
, we have that the column
A[i,*]
contains at least one element, say,
F(i,i_{\max},j_{\max})\in P
.
By the assumption of the lemma,
M(i,j)
is not
\alpha
-connected to any element in
A[i,*]
and therefore
\beta
-connected to
F(i,i_{\max},j_{\max})
.
By (
8
) and definition of
P
,
M(i,j)
is
\alpha
-connected (and therefore not
\beta
-connected) to all elements from
P
in columns to the left of
I
, as desired.
Having verified (
9
), we conclude by
Lemma

11.16
.

Case 5.2:

Assume
\gamma=\mathit{false}
.
For all

i,i^{\prime},i^{*}\in I^{\prime}
with
i^{*}<i,i^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
,
as
G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i,j),M(i,j))
,
it follows that

G^{+}\models\alpha(F(i^{*},i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),M(i,j))\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad(i,j)=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}).

Let
c(j):=M(i_{\max},j)
and
b(i^{*},j^{\prime}):=F(i^{*},i_{\max},j^{\prime})
for all
i^{*}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
.
Then by (

∗

‣
11.2
), and as

G^{+}\models\alpha(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
if and only if
j=j^{\prime}
, we have that

•

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
, and

•

\mathrm{atp}_{G^{+}}(b(i^{*},j^{\prime}),c(j))
is not the same

for all
i^{*}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
.
As in
Case 3
, the ranges of
b(\cdot,\cdot)
and
c(\cdot)
form a mono-prepattern on
\mathcal{A}
and
we conclude by
Lemma

11.17
.

Having exhausted all cases, this proves the lemma.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
11.6
, which we restate for convenience.

See
11.6

Proof.

If the prepattern is a mono-prepattern, we conclude by Lemma
11.17
.
Therefore, suppose that the prepattern is a bi-prepattern.
Let the sequences
I,J
, vertices
(c_{i,j}:i\in I,j\in J)
,
vertices
s_{1},s_{2}
, quantifier-free formulas
\alpha_{1}(x;y,z_{1}),\alpha_{2}(x;y,z_{1})
,
and symbols
{\sim_{1}},{\sim_{2}}\in\{=,\neq\}

be as in the definition of a bi-prepattern (see
Definition

6.1
).

Let
G^{+}
be the expansion of
G
by
four unary predicates, representing the sets
\{s_{1}\},\{s_{2}\},N_{G}(s_{1})
, and
N_{G}(s_{2})
.
Then there are quantifier-free formulas
\beta_{1}(x,y)
and
\beta_{2}(x,y)

such that for all
u,v\in V(G)
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have
G^{+}\models\alpha_{i}(u,v,s_{i})\iff\beta_{i}(u,v)
.

Define the mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)

with
M(i,j)=c_{i,j}
for
i\in I,j\in J
.
Then
M
with the formula
\beta_{1}
satisfies
the assumptions of
Lemma

11.16
if

\sim_{1}
is
\neq
,
and the assumptions of
Lemma

11.18
if
\sim_{1}
is
=
.
We apply the appropriate lemma.
In case of
Lemma

11.18
, this might yield a transformer and we are done.
Otherwise, we obtain a conductor
M_{1},\ldots,M_{t}\colon I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}\to V(G)

with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|=U_{h,k}(n)
,
t\leqslant 2h
,
where

M_{t}
is vertical, and
M_{1}=M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
.

Denote
M^{\prime}:=M_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\colon J^{\prime}\times I^{\prime}\to V(G)
.
Then
M^{\prime}
with the formula
\beta_{2}

satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
11.16

(with the roles of
I^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}
swapped) if

\sim_{2}
is
\neq
,
and the assumptions of Lemma
11.18
if
\sim_{2}
is
=
.
We again apply the appropriate lemma.
Either this yields a transformer and we are done, or
we obtain a conductor
M_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,M_{u}^{\prime}\colon J^{\prime\prime}\times I^{\prime\prime}\to V(G)

with
|J^{\prime\prime}|=|I^{\prime\prime}|=U_{h,k}(|I^{\prime}|)
,
u\leqslant 2h
,
where
M_{u}^{\prime}
is vertical and
M_{1}^{\prime}=M^{\prime}|_{J^{\prime\prime}\times I^{\prime\prime}}
.
Notice that

M^{\prime}_{u}|_{J^{\prime\prime}\times I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,M^{\prime}_{1}|_{J^{\prime\prime}\times I^{\prime\prime}}=M^{\mathsf{T}}_{1}|_{J^{\prime\prime}\times I^{\prime\prime}},\dots,M^{\mathsf{T}}_{t}|_{J^{\prime\prime}\times I^{\prime\prime}}

is a conductor of order
U_{h,k}(n)
and length
u+t-1\leqslant 4h-1
.
Observe that
M^{\prime}_{u}
and
M_{t}
are both vertical, and that
M^{\mathsf{T}}_{t}
is horizontal.
Thus, the sequence forms a transformer.
∎

11.3
Regular and Minimal Transformers

In this section, we normalize the transformers derived in
Section

11.2
.

Definition 11.19
.

A transformer
T=(M_{1},\ldots,M_{h})
in a graph
G
is
regular

if for all
s,t\in[h]
, the pair
(M_{s},M_{t})
is a regular pair of meshes (in particular also for
s=t
).
We say that
T
is
minimal
if it is regular
and for all
s,t\in[h]
the following conditions hold:

•

M_{s}
is vertical if and only if
s=1
,

•

M_{s}
is horizontal if and only if
s=h
,

•

M_{s}\neq M_{t}
if
s\neq t
(that is, no two meshes are identical),

•

the pair
(M_{s},M_{t})
is conducting if
|s-t|=1
,

•

the pair
(M_{s},M_{t})
is homogeneous if
|s-t|>1
.

In particular, in a minimal transformer
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
,
we have that either
h=1
and
M_{1}=M_{h}
is both horizontal and vertical,
or
h>1
and
M_{1}
is vertical and not horizontal,

M_{h}
is horizontal and not vertical, and
M_{2},\ldots,M_{h-1}
are neither horizontal nor vertical.

Lemma 11.20
.

If
G
contains a transformer of length
h
and order
n
then
G
contains a regular transformer of length
h
and order
U_{h}(n)
.

Proof.

Let
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
be a transformer of length
h
in
G
.
We can assume
I,J=[n]
.
For all
i,j\in[n]
, let
\pi_{i,j}\in V(M)^{h}

be the
h
-tuple

\pi_{i,j}:=(M_{1}(i,j),\ldots,M_{h}(i,j)).

By Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
), there are sets
I^{\prime},J^{\prime}\subseteq[n]

with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|\geqslant U_{h}(n)

such that
\mathrm{atp}(\pi_{i,j},\pi_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})

depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
,
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
It follows that for all
s,t\in[h]
,
the meshes
M_{s}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}

and
M_{t}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
form a regular mesh pair.
Thus, the sequence
M_{1}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}},\ldots,M_{h}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is a regular transformer.
∎

Lemma 11.21
.

If
G
contains a transformer of length
h
and order
n

then
G
contains a minimal transformer of length at most
h
and order
U_{h}(n)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.20
, there is
a regular transformer
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
of length
h
and order
U_{h}(n)
in
G
.
Consider the graph
\mathcal{G}
whose vertices are the meshes
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
,
and edges are pairs
M_{i}M_{j}
such that the pair
(M_{i},M_{j})
is conducting.
Clearly,
\mathcal{G}
contains a path of length
h
which starts in a vertical mesh and ends in a horizontal mesh.
Let
\pi:=M_{i_{1}},\ldots,M_{i_{p}}
be a shortest path in
\mathcal{G}

which starts in a vertical mesh and ends in a horizontal mesh.
Then the path
\pi
is an induced path of length at most
h
in
\mathcal{G}
,
which means that a pair
M_{i_{s}},M_{i_{t}}
, for distinct
s,t\in[p]

is conducting if and only if
|s-t|=1
.
As every pair
M_{i_{s}},M_{i_{t}}
is regular,
it follows that
M_{i_{1}},\ldots,M_{i_{p}}
is a minimal transformer of length
p\leqslant h
and order
U_{h}(n)
.
∎

12
Converters and Crossings

Our next goal is to analyze the structure of minimal transformers in graphs.
We will arrive at a notion of a converter, which is similar to a crossing.
Finally, from converters, we will obtain crossings.

12.1
Regular Pairs of Meshes

We study the structure of regular pairs of meshes in graphs.
We introduce the following notions.

Definition 12.1
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be two meshes in a graph
G
. We say that the pair
(M,M^{\prime})
is

•

disjoint
if
V(M)\cap V(M^{\prime})=\emptyset
,

•

matched
if
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
,

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\quad\text{if and only if}\quad(i,j)=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}).

•

co-matched
if the pair
(M,M^{\prime})
is matched in the complement graph
\bar{G}
,

•

non-adjacent
if
V(M)
and
V(M^{\prime})
are non-adjacent in
G
,

•

fully adjacent
if
uv\in E(G)
for all
u\in V(M)
and
v\in V(M^{\prime})
.

We prove some preliminary observations regarding regular pairs of meshes.

Lemma 12.2
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a regular pair of meshes of order
n>2
in a graph
G
.
Then
M
and
M^{\prime}
are either identical, or disjoint.

Proof.

We show that if
M(i,j)=M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
,
then
(i,j)=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
By regularity of the pair
(M,M^{\prime})
, this implies that
M(i,j)=M^{\prime}(i,j)
for all
i,j\in[n]
, so
M
and
M^{\prime}
are identical.
So suppose that
M(i,j)=M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J

with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
Up to reversing the order of
I
and up to exchanging the role of
I
and
J
, we can assume that
i<i^{\prime}
. Pick
i_{1}<i_{2}<i_{3}\in I
.
Then by regularity we have that
M(i_{1},j)=M^{\prime}(i_{2},j^{\prime})

and
M(i_{1},j)=M^{\prime}(i_{3},j^{\prime})
.
Thus,
M^{\prime}(i_{2},j^{\prime})=M^{\prime}(i_{3},j^{\prime})
,
contradicting injectivity of
M^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a regular pair of meshes of order
n
in a graph
G
.
Suppose that
G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
j<j^{\prime}
,
or is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
j>j^{\prime}
.
Then there exist subsequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
of order
U(n)
such that the submeshes
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
and
M^{\prime}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
are both vertical.

Proof.

Let
j_{\min}:=\min(J)
,
j_{\max}:=\max(J)
,
J^{\prime}:=J-\{j_{\min},j_{\max}\}
,
I^{\prime}:=I-\{\min(I),\max(I)\}
.
We show the argument for
M
, while the case for
M^{\prime}
follows by symmetry.
Suppose the first case holds,
that is,
G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
j<j^{\prime}
.
The other case proceeds by the same argument, exchanging the roles of
j_{\max}
and
j_{\min}
.

Let
a(i^{\prime})=M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j_{\max})
for
i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
.
Then by regularity,

\mathrm{atp}(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
, for
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j\in J^{\prime}
.
Furthermore,
G\models E(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j\in J^{\prime}
, by the assumption and by regularity.
Hence,
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is vertical.
∎

Lemma 12.4
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a regular pair of meshes of order
n
in a graph
G
. Suppose that
G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I,j,j^{\prime}\in J
with

(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
Then there exist subsequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
of order
U(n)
such that the submeshes
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
and
M^{\prime}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
are either both vertical, or both horizontal.

Proof.

It follows from the assumption
that one of the following cases holds:

•

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
j<j^{\prime}
,

•

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
j>j^{\prime}
,

•

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i<i^{\prime}
,

•

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i>i^{\prime}
.

In the first two cases we conclude by
Lemma

12.3
. In the last two cases we conclude by applying the same lemma to
M^{\mathsf{T}}
and
M^{\prime{\mathsf{T}}}
.
∎

Lemma 12.5
.

Let
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a conducting pair of disjoint meshes of order
n
in
G
.
Then there exist subsequences
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
of order
U(n)
such that
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
and
M^{\prime}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
are
either

1.

both vertical,

2.

both horizontal,

3.

matched, or

4.

co-matched.

Proof.

Since
M
and
M^{\prime}
are conducting, the pair is regular but not homogeneous.
For all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
we have that

(
1
)

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
depends only on

\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
, (by regularity)

(
2
)

\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not always the same, (by non-homogeneity)

(
3
)

the equality type of
M(i,j)
and
M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
is always
(\neq)
, and (by disjointness)

(
4
)

the adjacency between
M(i,j)
and
M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
is not always the same. (by
(

2

)
and
(

3

)
)

Assume
G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
Up to replacing
G
with
\bar{G}
,
by
(

4

)
we have that for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
,

G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\quad\text{if and only if }(i,j)=(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}).

Thus,
M
and
M^{\prime}
are matched or co-matched.

Otherwise,
G\models E(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, and we conclude that
M
and
M^{\prime}
are both vertical or both horizontal by
Lemma

12.4
.
∎

Lemma 12.6
.

Let
G
contain a minimal transformer of order
n
and length
h
. Then

G
contains a minimal transformer
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h^{\prime}}
of order
U_{h}(n)
and length
h^{\prime}\leqslant h
such that the following conditions are satisfied for
s,t\in[h^{\prime}]
:

1.

If
s\neq t
, then
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
are disjoint.

2.

If
|s-t|>1
then
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
are either non-adjacent or fully adjacent.

3.

If
|s-t|=1
then
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
are either matched or co-matched.

Proof.

If
n\leqslant 3
, as the minimal transformer we take any transformer of order 1 and length 1, so assume
n>3
.
The first two properties hold in any minimal transformer
T
of order
n>3
.
Indeed, as all meshes of
T
are pairwise distinct and regular,
by Lemma
12.2
they are pairwise disjoint.
Also, any pair of non-consecutive meshes is regular and not conducting, hence (as
n>3
) homogeneous.

We argue that we can find a minimal transformer satisfying additionally the last property. Let

T=(M_{1},\ldots,M_{h})

be a minimal transformer of order

n
.

Applying Lemma
12.5
to every pair
of consecutive meshes in
T

(and each time reducing the order of the transformer to
U_{h}(n)
)
we may assume that for every pair
(M_{s},M_{s+1})
of consecutive meshes in
T
,
the pair is either matched, or co-matched, or both meshes are vertical, or both are horizontal. Let
M_{s},\ldots,M_{t}
be a
subsequence of
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
of shortest length
such that
M_{s}
is vertical and
M_{t}
is horizontal.
It follows that every two consecutive meshes in
T^{\prime}
are matched or co-matched,
and that
T^{\prime}=(M_{s},\ldots,M_{t})
is a minimal transformer. Thus, the last property in the statement is satisfied.
∎

12.2
Regular Meshes

We now analyze the structure of single meshes, depending on whether they are horizontal and/or vertical.
We introduce some notation.

Definition 12.7
.

A single mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
is
regular
in a graph
G
, if the pair
(M,M)
is.
That is,

\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))

depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
.

Note that every mesh in a regular/minimal transformer is regular.

Definition 12.8
.

A
mesh pattern

is a subset
P
of the four lines in the following diagram:

All
2^{4}
possible mesh patterns are depicted below (including the empty pattern
).

Let
P
be a mesh pattern.
A regular mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
with
|I|,|J|\geqslant 2
is a
P
-mesh
in a graph
G
if for all
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in I\times J
with
i\leqslant i^{\prime}
, the vertices
M(i,j)
and
M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent in
G
if and only if one of the following conditions holds:

•

i=i^{\prime}
and
j\neq j^{\prime}
and

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in P
,

i<i^{\prime}
and

.

For example,
M
is a
-mesh if and only if
V(M)
induces an independent set,
and if
M
is a
-mesh, then

V(M)
induces a rook graph in
G
,
and if
M
is a
-mesh, then
V(M)
induces a comparability grid in
G
.
(Recall that the
comparability grid
of order
n
consists of vertices

\{a_{i,j}\mid i,j\in[n]\}
and edges between vertices
a_{i,j}
and
a_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
if and only if either
i=i^{\prime}
, or
j=j^{\prime}
,
or
i<i^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow j<j^{\prime}
.)

Definition 12.9
.

A
generalized grid
in a graph
G
is a regular mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)

satisfying the following conditions:

•

G\models E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
does not depend only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
, for
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, and

•

G\models E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
does not depend only on
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
, for
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

P

M

Lemma 12.10
.

Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a regular mesh in a graph
G
.
Then
M
is vertical, or is horizontal, or is a
-mesh in
G
or in
\bar{G}
.

Proof.

Suppose that

G\models E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
Then
V(M)
forms an independent set or a clique in
G
.
Otherwise, the statement follows by Lemma
12.4
, applied to
M=M^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 12.11
.

Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a regular mesh in a graph
G
,
and assume
G\models E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i<i^{\prime}
.
Then either in
G
or in
\bar{G}
,
M
is a
-mesh or a
-mesh.

Proof.

Replacing
G
with
\bar{G}
if needed,
we may assume that

G\models\neg E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i<i^{\prime}
.
By symmetry of the edge relation, we have that
G\models\neg E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
holds for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i\neq i^{\prime}
.
If
G\models E(M(i,j),M(i,j^{\prime}))
for some
i\in I
and distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in J
, then
M
is a
-mesh.
Otherwise,
M
is a
-mesh.
∎

Lemma 12.12
.

Let
M
be a regular mesh in a graph
G

which is not horizontal.
Then either in
G
or in
\bar{G}
,
M
is a
-mesh or a
-mesh.

Proof.

As
M^{\mathsf{T}}
is not vertical,
we can apply the contrapositive of Lemma
12.3
to
M^{\mathsf{T}}
and
M^{\mathsf{T}}
.
We conclude that

G\models E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
i<i^{\prime}
. The conclusion follows from Lemma
12.11
.
∎

Definition 12.13
.

A mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
is
capped
if there is a function

a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)
, where
I^{\prime}=I-\{\min(I),\max(I)\}
, such that one of the following conditions holds in
G
or in
\bar{G}
:

(
=
)

for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j\in J
,
M(i,j)
is adjacent to
a(i^{\prime})
if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
, or

(
\leqslant
)

for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
and
j\in J
,
M(i,j)
is adjacent to
a(i^{\prime})
if and only if
i\leqslant i^{\prime}
.

More precisely, a capped mesh
M
is
\alpha
-capped,
for
\alpha\in\{=,\leqslant\}
, if the above condition
\alpha
holds.

Lemma 12.14
.

Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a vertical mesh in a graph
G
with
|J|>1
.
Then
M
is capped.

Proof.

Since
M
is vertical, there is some
a\colon I\to V(G)

such that, for
J^{\prime}=J-\{\min(J),\max(J)\}
,

•

\mathrm{atp}(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
, depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
, for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J^{\prime}
, and

•

\mathrm{atp}(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))
is not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J^{\prime}
.

First observe that the ranges of the functions
a
and
M
are disjoint. Assume otherwise, that is, that
a(i^{\prime})=M(i,j)
for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j\in J
.
Pick
j^{\prime}\in J
distinct from
j
, which exists since we assume that
|J|>1
.
Then we have that
a(i^{\prime})=M(i,j^{\prime})
, by the first defining condition of the function
a
. This contradicts the fact that
M
is an injective function.

Let
p_{<},p_{=},p_{>}\in\{0,1\}

be such that for each
R\in\{<,=,>\}
,

G\models E(M(i,j),a(i^{\prime}))\quad\iff\quad p_{R}=1\quad\textit{for all $j\in J^{\prime}$, $i,i^{\prime}\in I$ with $i\,R\,i^{\prime}$}.

By the assumption on
a
,
the values
p_{<},p_{=},p_{>}
are not all equal.
Replacing
G
with
\bar{G}
if needed, we can assume that

p_{>}=0
. Thus, one of three cases occurs:

1.

(p_{<},p_{=},p_{>})=(0,1,0)
,

2.

(p_{<},p_{=},p_{>})=(1,1,0)
,

3.

(p_{<},p_{=},p_{>})=(1,0,0)
.

Let
I^{\prime}=I-\{\min(I),\max(I)\}
and let
a|_{I^{\prime}}
be the restriction of
a
to the domain
I^{\prime}
.
In the first case,
(M,a|_{I^{\prime}})
is a
=
-capped mesh.
In the second case,
(M,a|_{I^{\prime}})
is a
\leqslant
-capped mesh.
Suppose the third case occurs,
and let
b\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)
be such that
b(i)=a(i_{+})
where

i_{+}
is the successor of
i
in
I
.
Then
(M,b)
is a
\leqslant
-capped mesh.
∎

Lemma 12.15
.

Let
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
be a regular mesh of order
n>1
in a graph
G
. Then the following hold.

1.

If
M
is not horizontal and not vertical, then
M
forms a
-mesh in
G
or in
\bar{G}
.

2.

If
M
is vertical and not horizontal, then one of two cases occurs in
G
or in
\bar{G}
:

(a)

M
is a
-mesh, or

(b)

M

is a
-mesh and capped.

3.

If
M
is both horizontal and vertical, then one of four cases occurs in
G
or in
\bar{G}
:

(a)

M
is a generalized grid,

(b)

M
is a
-mesh and
M^{\mathsf{T}}
is capped,

(c)

M
is a
-mesh and
M
is capped, or

(d)

M
is a
-mesh and both
M
and
M^{\mathsf{T}}
are capped.

Proof.

The first item is by Lemma
12.10
.

We prove the second item. By Lemma
12.12
, either in
G
or in
\bar{G}
,
M
is a
-mesh or a
-mesh.
In the first case we are done.
In the second case, Lemma
12.14

yields the conclusion.

Finally, we prove the third item.
Assume that
M
is both vertical and horizontal.
Then, by Lemma
12.14
, both
M
and
M^{\mathsf{T}}
are capped.
Suppose
M
is not a generalized grid, as otherwise condition
3(a)
holds, and we are done.
Then either
G\models E(M(i,j),M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
, or it depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
,
for all distinct
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in I\times J
.
Suppose it depends only on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
,
while the other case follows by replacing
M
with
M^{\mathsf{T}}
.
By Lemma
12.11
we have that either in
G
or in
\bar{G}
,

M
is a
-mesh or a
-mesh.
If
M
is a
-mesh, condition
3(b)
holds.
If
M
is a
-mesh, condition
3(d)
holds.

This concludes the lemma.
∎

, then
V(M)
induces a comparability grid of order

in

It remains to show that if
G
has a
P
-mesh of order
m=n^{2}
, for some
P\in\{\hbox to5.69pt{\vbox to5.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84528pt\lower-2.84528pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Consider the function

is a

i\leqslant i^{\prime}

G
if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
