i_{1}i_{2}:=ij
,
i_{1}^{\prime}i_{2}^{\prime}:=i^{\prime}j^{\prime}
,
j_{1}j_{2}:=ji
, and
j_{1}^{\prime}j_{2}^{\prime}:=j^{\prime}i^{\prime}
.

Assume first that
i=i^{\prime}
.
In this case we want to show that
M^{\prime}(i,j)
and
M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent.
As
ij
and
i^{\prime}j^{\prime}
are distinct, we either have
j<j^{\prime}
or
j^{\prime}<j
.
If
j<j^{\prime}

then
ij<_{\mathrm{lex}}i^{\prime}j^{\prime}
and
ji<_{\mathrm{lex}}j^{\prime}i^{\prime}
, and we conclude using the first disjunct of (
10
).
If
j^{\prime}<j

then
i^{\prime}j^{\prime}<_{\mathrm{lex}}ij
and
j^{\prime}i^{\prime}<_{\mathrm{lex}}ji
, and we conclude using the second disjunct of (
10
).

Assume now that
i\neq i^{\prime}
, so
i<i^{\prime}
.
In this case we want to show that
M^{\prime}(i,j)
and
M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent if and only if
j\leqslant j^{\prime}
.
As
ij<_{\mathrm{lex}}i^{\prime}j^{\prime}
, by (
10
) we have that
M^{\prime}(i,j)
and
M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent if and only if
ji<_{\mathrm{lex}}j^{\prime}i^{\prime}
, which is the case if and only if
j\leqslant j^{\prime}
, as desired.

Having exhausted all cases, we conclude that
M^{\prime}
is a
-mesh of order
n
,
and therefore,
G
contains an induced comparability grid of order
n
.
∎

12.3
Converters

The following notion is aimed at providing a low-level description of minimal transformers.

Definition 12.17
.

Fix
h,n\geqslant 1
and a graph
G
.
A
converter
of length
h
and order
n
in
G
consists of

•

meshes
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}\colon[n]\times[n]\to V(G)
in
G
,

•

two functions
a,b\colon[n]\to V(G)
; we denote the range of these functions by
V(a),V(b)
,

such that the following conditions hold:

(G.
1
)

the sets
V(M_{1}),\ldots,V(M_{h})
are pairwise disjoint,

(G.
2
)

for
s,t\in\{1,\ldots,h\}
, if
|s-t|=1
, then

M_{s}
is matched or co-matched with
M_{t}
,

(G.
3
)

for
s,t\in\{1,\ldots,h\}
, if
|s-t|>1
, then

V(M_{s})
is fully adjacent or non-adjacent to
V(M_{t})
,

(G.
4
)

for
s\in\{2,\ldots,h-1\}
,
V(M_{s})
is an independent set or a clique,

(G.
5
)

Let
(M,f)\in\{(M_{1},a),(M_{h}^{\mathsf{T}},b)\}
.
Then one of the following three conditions holds in
G
or in
\bar{G}
:

(C)

M
is a
-mesh, or
h=1
and
M
is a
-mesh.
Moreover,

M(i,j)
is adjacent to
f(i^{\prime})
if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
, for all
i,i^{\prime},j\in[n]
,

(S)

M
is a
-mesh, or
h=1
and
M
is a
-mesh. Moreover,

M(i,j)
is adjacent to
f(i^{\prime})
if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
, for all
i,i^{\prime},j\in[n]
,

(H)

M
is a
-mesh, or
h=1
and
M
is a
-mesh. Moreover,
M(i,j)
is adjacent to
f(i^{\prime})
if and only if
i^{\prime}\leqslant i
, for all
i,i^{\prime},j\in[n]
.

Say that
(M,f)
as above
has
kind

\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S}
, or
\mathbf{H}
respectively,
if it satisfies the corresponding condition above
(which stand for
clique
,
star
, and
half-graph
, respectively).
A converter has
kind

(\alpha,\beta)
, where
\alpha,\beta\in\{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{H}\}
, if
(M_{1},a)
has kind
\alpha
and
(M_{h}^{\mathsf{T}},b)
has kind
\beta
.

A converter is
proper
if the following hold:

•

the sets
V(a),V(M_{1}),\ldots,V(M_{h}),V(b)
are pairwise disjoint,

•

the sets
V(a)
and
V(M_{2})\cup\dots\cup V(M_{h})
are homogeneously connected,

•

the sets
V(b)
and
V(M_{1})\cup\dots\cup V(M_{h-1})
are homogeneously connected,

•

the sets
V(a)
and
V(b)
are homogeneously connected,

•

each of the sets
V(a)
and
V(b)
induces an independent set or a clique.

Lemma 12.18
.

Let
G
contain a converter of length
h
and order
n
. Then, for some number
m\geqslant U_{h}(n)
,
either
G
contains a proper converter of length at most
h
and order
m
, or
m=1
.

Proof.

Let
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}\colon[n]\times[n]\to V(G)
and
a,b\colon[n]\to V(G)
form a converter.
For every pair
i,j\in[n]
let
\pi_{i,j}

denote the sequence
(a(i),M_{1}(i,j),\ldots,M_{h}(i,j),b(j))
.
Apply Bipartite Ramsey (
Lemma

4.4
) to get sets

I\subseteq[n]
and
J\subseteq[n]
of size
m=U_{h}(n)
,
so that

\mathrm{atp}(\pi_{i,j}\pi_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})
only depends on
\mathrm{otp}(i,i^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})
for
i\in I
and
j\in J
.
Observe that if
a(i)=M_{s}(i^{\prime},j)
for some
i,i^{\prime}\in I
,
j\in J
, and
s\in[h]
, then also
a(i)=M_{s}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
for some other
j^{\prime}
, which implies
M_{s}(i^{\prime},j)=M_{s}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
and contradicts injectivity of
M_{s}
, unless
|J|=m=1
.
Similarly, if
a(i)=b(j)
for some
i\in I
and
j\in J
, then we conclude that
a(i)=a(i^{\prime})
for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
, which implies that
|I|=m=1
, since
the conditions
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{H}
imply that
a
is injective.
A similar argument can be made for
b
.

The tuple
M_{1}^{\prime}:=M_{1}|_{I\times J},\ldots,M_{h}^{\prime}:=M_{h}|_{I\times J},a^{\prime}:=a|_{I},b^{\prime}:=b|_{J}
, with
I
and
J
both reindexed to
[m]
,
forms a converter of order
m
.
As discussed above and by
(G.

1

)
, we observe the sets
V(a^{\prime}),V(b^{\prime}),V(M^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,V(M^{\prime}_{h})
to be pairwise disjoint.
Further note that
V(a^{\prime})
and
V(b^{\prime})
each induce an independent set or a clique by construction.
Lastly, to have a
proper
converter,
we have to ensure that
V(M_{t}^{\prime})
and
V(b^{\prime})
are homogeneous for all
1\leqslant t<h
,
and that
V(M_{t}^{\prime})
and
V(a^{\prime})
are homogeneous for all
1<t\leqslant h
.

We only sketch this last argument.
Suppose for example that
V(b^{\prime})
and
V(M_{t}^{\prime})

are not homogeneous, for some
1\leqslant t<h
.
Then
M_{t}^{\prime}
is either horizontal, or is vertical, as witnessed by
b^{\prime}
. We can therefore obtain a converter of the same order and smaller length.
Thus, by replacing the converter
a^{\prime},M_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,M_{h}^{\prime},b^{\prime}
by a shorter one if needed, we arrive at a proper converter of order
m
.
∎

Lemma 12.19
.

Let
G
contain a transformer of length
h
and order
n
. Then there is a number
m\geqslant U_{h}(n)
such that,

G
contains a converter of length at most
h
and order
m
,
or contains a comparability grid of order
m
as an induced subgraph.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.21
and Lemma
12.6
,
G
contains a minimal transformer
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h^{\prime}}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
of order
U_{h}(n)
and length
h^{\prime}\leqslant h
,
such that:

•

the sets
V(M_{1}),\ldots,V(M_{h}^{\prime})
are pairwise disjoint,

•

for
s,t\in[h^{\prime}]
with
|s-t|=1
,
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
are matched or co-matched,

•

for
s,t\in[h^{\prime}]
with
|s-t|>1
,
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
are fully adjacent or fully non-adjacent.

Let
I^{\prime}=I-\{\min(I),\max(I)\}
and
J^{\prime}=J-\{\min(J),\max(J)\}
.

Suppose first that
h^{\prime}=1
.
Then
M_{1}
is both vertical and horizontal.
By Lemma
12.15
,
one of the following holds for
M:=M_{1}
in either
G
or
\bar{G}
:

(a)

M
is a generalized grid,

(b)

M
is a
-mesh and
M^{\mathsf{T}}
is
\alpha
-capped for some
\alpha\in\{=,\leqslant\}
,

(c)

M
is a
-mesh and
M
is
\alpha
-capped for some
\alpha\in\{=,\leqslant\}
, or

(d)

M
is a
-mesh and
there are some
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\{=,\leqslant\}
such that
M
is
\alpha_{1}
-capped and
M^{\mathsf{T}}
is
\alpha_{2}
-capped.

In case
(a)
,
M
is by definition a generalized grid in both
\bar{G}
and
G
.
By Lemma
12.16
,
M
is a
P
-mesh with
P\in\{\hbox to6.09pt{\vbox to6.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.04527pt\lower-3.04527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\}
in
G
,
or
G
contains or a comparability grid of order
U_{h}(n)
as an induced graph.
If
M
is a
P
-mesh, then
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
together with
a,b
has kind
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C})
,
where
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)
is defined by
a(i)=M(i,\min(J))
for
i\in I^{\prime}
, and
where
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V(G)
is defined by
b(j)=M(\min(I),j)
for
j\in J^{\prime}
.
In either case, the statement holds.

Suppose we are in case
(b)
.
Let
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V(G)

witness that
M^{\mathsf{T}}
is
\alpha
-capped.
Let
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)

be defined by
a(i)=M(i,\min(J))
.
Then
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
,
a
and
b
form a converter of kind
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S})

if
\alpha
is
=
, and a converter of kind
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{H})
if
\alpha
is
\leqslant
.
The case
(c)
is symmetric.

Finally, suppose we are in case
(d)
.
Let
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)

witness that
M
is
\alpha_{1}
-capped,
and
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V(G)

witness that
M^{\mathsf{T}}
is
\alpha_{2}
-capped.
Then
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
,
a
, and
b

form a converter of kind
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
, where

\tau_{i}=\mathbf{S}
if
\alpha_{i}
is
=
,
and
\tau_{i}=\mathbf{H}
if
\alpha_{i}
is
\leqslant
, for
i=1,2
.

This concludes the case of length
h^{\prime}=1
.
Suppose now that
h^{\prime}>1
.
Then, by the minimality assumption (
Definition

11.19
),

•

M_{1}
is vertical and not horizontal,

•

M_{h^{\prime}}
is horizontal and not vertical, and

•

M_{2},\ldots,M_{h^{\prime}-1}
are neither vertical nor horizontal.

By Lemma
12.15
,
we conclude that each of
M_{2},\ldots,M_{h^{\prime}-1}
is a
-mesh in either
G
or
\bar{G}
.
By Lemma
12.15
applied to
M_{1}
, one of two cases holds in either
G
or
\bar{G}
:

(a)

M_{1}
is a
-mesh, or

(b)

M_{1}
is a
-mesh and is
\alpha
-capped for some
\alpha\in\{=,\leqslant\}
.

In the first case,
M_{1}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
together with
a
has kind
\mathbf{C}
,
where
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)
is defined by
a(i)=M(i,\min(J))
for
i\in I^{\prime}
.
In the second case, let
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)

witness that
M_{1}
is
\alpha
-capped.
Then

M_{1}|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
together with
a
has kind
\mathbf{S}
if

\alpha
is
=
, and has kind
\mathbf{H}
if
\alpha
is
\leqslant
.

Similarly, by Lemma
12.15
applied to
M_{h^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we conclude that

M_{h^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{T}}|_{J^{\prime}\times I^{\prime}}
together with some
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V(G)
has kind
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S}
or
\mathbf{H}
.
We thus conclude that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h^{\prime}}
induced on
I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}
, together with
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V(G)
and
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V(G)
,
form a converter of length
h^{\prime}
.
∎

12.4
Crossings

The last step is to go from converters to crossings, whose definition we recall for convenience.
For
r\geqslant 1
, the
star
r
-crossing
of order
n
is the
r
-subdivision of
K_{n,n}
.
More precisely, it consists of
roots

a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
and
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}

together with
r
-vertex paths
\{\pi_{i,j}\mid i,j\in[n]\}
that are pairwise vertex-disjoint (see
Figure

4
).
We denote the two endpoints of a path
\pi_{i,j}
by
\mathrm{start}(\pi_{i,j})
and
\mathrm{end}(\pi_{i,j})
.
We require that roots appear on no path, that each root
a_{i}
is adjacent to
\{\mathrm{start}(\pi_{i,j})\mid j\in[n]\}
,
and that each root
b_{j}
is adjacent to
\{\mathrm{end}(\pi_{i,j})\mid i\in[n]\}
.
The
clique
r
-crossing
of order
n
is the graph obtained from the star
r
-crossing of order
n

by turning the neighborhood of each root into a clique.
Moreover, we define the
half-graph
r
-crossing
of order
n
similarly to the star
r
-crossing of order
n
,
where each root
a_{i}
is instead adjacent to
\{\mathrm{start}(\pi_{i^{\prime},j})\mid i^{\prime},j\in[n],i\leqslant i^{\prime}\}
,
and each root
b_{j}
is instead adjacent to
\{\mathrm{end}(\pi_{i,j^{\prime}})\mid i,j^{\prime}\in[n],j\leqslant j^{\prime}\}
.
Each of the three
r
-crossings contains no edges other than the ones described.

Figure 17:

(
i
) star 4-crossing of order 4.
(
ii
) clique 4-crossing of order 4.
(
iii
) half-graph 4-crossing of order 4.
In (
i
), (
ii
), (
iii
), the roots are adjacent to all vertices in their respective colorful strip.

We partition the vertex sets of the
r
-crossings into
layers
:
The 0th layer consists of the vertices
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\}
.
The
l
th layer, for
l\in[r]
, consists of the
l
th vertices of the paths
\{\pi_{i,j}\mid i,j\in[n]\}

(that is, the 1st and
r
th layer, respectively, are

\{\mathrm{start}(\pi_{i,j})\mid i,j\in[n]\}
and

\{\mathrm{end}(\pi_{i,j})\mid i,j\in[n]\}
).
Finally, the
(r+1)
th layer consists of the vertices
\{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\}
.
A
flipped
star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing
is a graph obtained from a
star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing
by performing a flip where the parts of the specifying partition are the layers of the
r
-crossing.

We observe that a flipped
r
-crossing is the same as a proper converter of kind
(\alpha,\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in\{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S},\mathbf{H}\}
.
So the goal is to show that
from a converter of kind
(\alpha,\beta)
we can extract a converter
with
\alpha=\beta
.
This is achieved in the next lemma.

Lemma 12.20
.

Let
G
be a graph
containing proper converter
C
of length
h
and order
n
.
Then
G
contains as an induced subgraph either

•

a flipped star
r
-crossing, or

•

a flipped clique
r
-crossing, or

•

a flipped half-graph
r
-crossing,

of order
\lfloor\sqrt{n}\rfloor
, for some
1\leqslant r\leqslant 2h+1
.

Proof.

Suppose the meshes
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}\colon[n]\times[n]\to V(G)
and functions
a,b\colon[n]\to V(G)
form a proper converter of kind
(\alpha,\beta)
and order
n
in
G
.
If
(\alpha,\beta)\neq(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H})
,
then either
\alpha\in\{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S}\}
or
\beta\in\{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S}\}
.
By replacing
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
with the converter
M_{h}^{\mathsf{T}},\ldots,M_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}
of kind
(\beta,\alpha)
if needed,
we may assume that either

\beta\in\{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S}\}
,
or
\alpha=\beta=\mathbf{H}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{V(a),V(M_{1}),\ldots,V(M_{h}),V(b)\}
.
By taking an induced subgraph if needed,
we may assume that
V(G)=\bigcup\mathcal{P}
.
It follows from the definition of a proper converter that there is a unique
\mathcal{P}
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G

with the following properties:

•

the meshes
M_{s}
and
M_{t}
are non-adjacent if
|t-s|>1
and are matched if
|t-s|=1
,

•

for
1<s\leqslant h
,
V(M_{s})
is non-adjacent to
V(a)
,

•

for
1\leqslant s<h
,
V(M_{s})
is non-adjacent to
V(b)
,

•

V(a)
and
V(b)
are non-adjacent,

Figure 18:
Construction of an
\ell
-crossing from a proper converter.
Edges whose presence depends on the values of
\alpha
and
\beta
are dashed.

In the case
\alpha=\beta=\mathbf{H}

we construct a flipped half-graph
h
-crossing of order
n
in
G
.
For this, we choose the roots
a_{1}:=a(1),\dots,a_{n}:=a(n)
on one side, and
b_{1}:=b(1),\dots,b_{n}:=b(n)
on the other side,
and connect them via the paths
\pi_{i,j}

with
\pi_{i,j}[s]=M_{1}(i,j)
for
s=1,\ldots,h
.
It follows that
G
contains a flipped half-graph
h
-crossing of order
n
as an induced subgraph.

Consider now the case where
\beta\in\{\mathbf{C},\mathbf{S}\}
.
We observe that

•

for all
i,j,j^{\prime}\in[n]
,
M_{h}(i,j)
is adjacent to
b(j^{\prime})
if and only if
j=j^{\prime}
.

Set
\ell:=2h
if
\beta=\mathbf{C}
and
\ell:=2h+1
if
\beta=\mathbf{S}
.
We construct an
\ell
-crossing.
For all
i,i^{\prime},j\in[n]
with
i\neq i^{\prime}
consider the unique

\ell
-vertex path

\sigma_{i,i^{\prime},j}
in
G^{\prime}

from
M_{1}(i,j)
to
M_{1}(i^{\prime},j)

with the following properties:

•

\sigma_{i,i^{\prime},j}
has length
\ell
,

•

\sigma_{i,i^{\prime},j}[d]=M_{d}(i,j)
for
d=1,\ldots,h
,

•

\sigma_{i,i^{\prime},j}[\ell+1-d]=M_{d}(i^{\prime},j)
for
d=1,\ldots,h
,

•

if
\beta=\mathbf{S}
then
\sigma_{i,i^{\prime},j}[h+1]=b(j)
.

Note that
\sigma_{i,j,j^{\prime}}
is an induced path in
G^{\prime}
.
Let
m=\lfloor\sqrt{n}\rfloor
and pick any injective function
f\colon[m]\times[m]\to[n]
.
For
i,i^{\prime}\in[m]
, let

\pi_{i,i^{\prime}}:=\sigma_{i,i^{\prime}+m,f(i,i^{\prime})}
.
Observe that there are no edges in
G
between any pair of paths
\pi_{i,i^{\prime}}
,
i,i^{\prime}\in[m]
.
We construct a flipped star/clique/half-graph
\ell
-crossing in
G
, corresponding to the cases
\alpha=\mathbf{S},\mathbf{C},\mathbf{H}
, respectively.
For this, we choose the roots
a_{1}:=a(1),\dots,a_{m}:=a(m)
on one side, and
b_{1}:=a(m+1),\dots,b_{m}:=a(2m)
on the other side
and connect them via the paths
\pi_{i,i^{\prime}}
for
i,i^{\prime}\in[m]
.
Note that if
\alpha=\mathbf{H}
, then
\{a_{i}\mid i\in[m]\}
is fully connected to
\{\mathrm{end}(\pi_{i,i^{\prime}})\mid i,i^{\prime}\in[m]\}
.
The construction is illustrated in
Figure

18
.
It follows that
G
contains a flipped
\ell
-crossing of order
m
as an induced subgraph.
∎

12.5
From Prepatterns to
Flipped Crossings and Comparability Grids

We finally prove the main non-structure result.

See
10.14

Proof.

By Proposition
11.6
,

G
contains a transformer of order
U_{k,h}(n)
and length at most
4h-1
. By Lemma
12.19
,

G
contains a comparability grid of order
U_{k,h}(n)
as an induced subgraph,
or a converter of length at most
4h-1
and order
U_{k,h}(n)
. In the latter case, we can make the converter proper
by Lemma
12.18
. By Lemma
12.20
, we obtain the required flipped
r
-crossing of order
U_{k,h}(n)

for some
r\leqslant 2\cdot(4h-1)+1=8h-1
.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain the following.

Proposition 12.21
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a graph class that is not prepattern-free.
Then there exists
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{C}
contains as an induced subgraph either

•

a flipped star
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped clique
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped half-graph
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a comparability grid of order
k
.

12.6
From
Flipped Crossings and Comparability Grids to Independence

The next result states that the patterns are a witness to monadic independence.

Proposition 12.22
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a graph class and
r\geqslant 1
, such that for every
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{C}
contains as an induced subgraph

•

a flipped star
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped clique
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped half-graph
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a comparability grid of order
k
.

Then
\mathcal{C}
is monadically independent.

Proof sketch.

We will be brief, as a more rigorous proof of a stronger statement is later given in
Proposition

14.5
, where we show that the hereditary closure of
\mathcal{C}

interprets
the class of all graphs.
For this sketch, we use the fact that transductions are transitive: if
\mathcal{C}
transduces
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}
transduces
\mathcal{E}
, then already
\mathcal{C}
transduces
\mathcal{E}
.
We transduce the class of all graphs from
\mathcal{C}
by concatenating multiple simpler transductions,
each of which depends only on
r
(and not on
\mathcal{C}
or
k
).

As there is a transduction which produces from
\mathcal{C}
all its induced subgraphs, we can assume that
\mathcal{C}
is hereditary.
Additionally, there is a fixed transduction that turns flipped
r
-crossings into their non-flipped versions (or more generally: a transduction that maps
\mathcal{C}
to all of its
(r+2)
-flips).
Now by the pigeonhole principle, we can assume that
\mathcal{C}
is either the class of all (non-flipped) star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossings or the class of all comparability grids.

We start with describing a transduction
which, when given a star
r
-crossing with roots
A
and
B
,
can create an arbitrary bipartite graph
(A,B,E)
.
The transduction colors the roots in
A
and
B
with colors
C_{A}
and
C_{B}
respectively,
and the vertices on the paths between
a\in A
and
b\in B

with color
C_{+}
if
\{a,b\}\in E
and color
C_{-}
otherwise.
It is then trivial to connect
a\in A
with
b\in B

by a first-order formula checking if there is a path of color
C_{+}
between them.

Let us further argue that there is a transduction that takes as input a clique or half-graph
r
-crossing
with root sets
A
and
B
, and creates a star
r
-crossing with the same roots.
For clique
r
-crossings this is easy to see, as it suffices to turn the neighborhood of each root into a star.
For half-graph
r
-crossings, we first recover the linear order of the roots.
Focusing on the side
A
first, we observe for
a,a^{\prime}\in A
that
a\leqslant a^{\prime}
if and only if
N(a)\subseteq N(a^{\prime})
.
The latter condition is expressible in first-order logic, therefore a transduction can remove all edges from a vertex
a\in A
to
\bigcup_{a^{\prime}>a}N(a^{\prime})
.
By also proceeding similarly for the roots in
B
, this turns a half-graph
r
-crossing into a star
r
-crossing.

The case of comparability grids proceeds similarly as the case of half-graph
1
-crossings above. Namely,
for a bipartite graph
G=(A,B,E)
with
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\}

and
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{\ell}\}
,
we consider a comparability grid with vertex set
\{0,\ldots,k\}\times\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
,
and identify
B
with the vertices
[k]\times\{0\}
,
and
A
with the vertices
\{0\}\times[\ell]
,
and then proceed as in the case of half-graph
1
-crossings
(see also the proof of Lemma
16.8
where this construction is formalized).

In summary, by chaining transductions, we can take a flipped
r
-crossing (or comparability grid),
undo the flips, turn it into a star
r
-crossing,
and then into any bipartite graph on the same root sets.
From the class of bipartite graphs one can trivially transduce the class of all graphs.
Hence,
\mathcal{C}
is monadically independent.
∎

We formulate a lemma which follows from the construction
presented in the proof sketch above, and will be used in the proofs of
Theorem

3.1
and
Theorem

1.9
.

We recall the notion of radius-
r
encodings of a bipartite graph
G
.
Fix an integer
r\geqslant 1
.
Let
G=(A,B,E)
be a bipartite graph with
|A|=|B|=n
for some
n
,
and let
A=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
and
B=\{b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}\}
.
Consider a graph
H_{0}
which is either a star
r
-crossing, or a clique
r
-crossing, or a half-graph
r
-crossing
with roots
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
.
Recall that
V(H_{0})
can be partitioned into
r+2

layers
, and there are
n^{2}
distinguished
r
-vertex
paths

\pi_{i,j}
.
Let
H
be a graph obtained from
H_{0}
by:

1.

adding arbitrary edges within each layer of
H_{0}
,

2.

removing all vertices of the paths
\pi_{i,j}
for
i,j\in[n]
such that
\{a_{i},b_{j}\}\notin E(G)
,

3.

flipping pairs of layers arbitrarily.

We call
H
a
radius-
r
encoding of
G
.

Lemma 12.23
.

For every fixed
r
there is a number
k
and a formula
\varphi(x,y)

in the signature of
k
-colored graphs, with the following property.
For every bipartite graph
G

and radius-
r
encoding
H
of
G
,
there is a
k
-coloring
H^{+}
of
H

such that the graph

\varphi(H^{+})
with vertices

V(H)

and edges

\{\{u,v\}:u,v\in V(H),H^{+}\models\varphi(u,v)\}

contains the
1
-subdivision of
G
as an induced subgraph.

Proof sketch.

We modify slightly the construction described in the proof of
Proposition

12.22
.
There, a transduction was described which, given a star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing
H_{0}
with roots
A
and
B
, or a comparability grid, can output an arbitrary bipartite graph
G=(A,B,E)
.
Essentially, we observe that the vertices marked
C_{-}
in the proof of
Proposition

12.22
can be removed from
H_{0}
without breaking the construction (with one detail in the case of half-graph
r
-crossings, commented below).
Note that, differing from
r
-crossings, radius-
r
encodings
may contain additional arbitrary edges between vertices in the same layer.
However, by coloring each of the at most
r+2
layers in a different color palette (only increasing the number of colors by a factor of
r+2
), we can ignore these arbitrary edges.

To prove the lemma, we therefore proceed the same way as in that construction,
but instead of representing
G
in the appropriate
r
-crossing with roots
A
and
B
,
we represent it in a radius-
r
encoding
H
of
G
.

In the case of
r
-half-graph crossings, we additionally mark (doubling the number of colors) the vertices of
A
and
B
that are
isolated in
G
. The key observation is that for two non-isolated roots
a,a^{\prime}\in A

we still have that
a\leqslant a^{\prime}
if and only if
N(a)\subseteq N(A^{\prime})
,
and similarly for non-isolated roots in
B
.
This is enough to recover the edges of
G
using a fixed first-order formula, just as
in
Proposition

12.22
.

Technically, this way we produce three formulas
\varphi_{\mathbf{S}}^{r}(x,y),\varphi_{\mathbf{C}}^{r}(x,y),\varphi_{\mathbf{H}}^{r}(x,y)
,
rather than a single formula
\varphi(x,y)
, corresponding to the cases where the host crossing is a star
r
-crossing, a clique
r
-crossing, or a half-graph
r
-crossing.
To obtain a single formula
\varphi(x,y)
,
we can triple the number of colors used in the construction to give each of the three cases its own color palette.
The formula
\varphi(x,y)
can then be written as a boolean combination of the formulas
\varphi_{\mathbf{S}}^{r}(x,y),\varphi_{\mathbf{C}}^{r}(x,y),\varphi_{\mathbf{H}}^{r}(x,y)
, which tests which color palette was used.
∎

13
Wrapping Up

We are now ready to prove the main results of this paper,

Theorems

1.3
and
1.6
.
They are implied by the following.

Theorem 13.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a graph class.
Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent;

(ii)

For every
r\geqslant 1
there exists
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}

such that
\mathcal{C}
excludes as induced subgraphs

•

all flipped star
r
-crossings of order
k
, and

•

all flipped clique
r
-crossings of order
k
, and

•

all flipped half-graph
r
-crossings of order
k
, and

•

the comparability grid of order
k
;

(iii)

\mathcal{C}
is prepattern-free;

(iv)

\mathcal{C}
has the insulation property;

(v)

\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable.

Proof.

We have already proven all the necessary implications:

•

\ref{itm:circle1}\Rightarrow\ref{itm:circle2}
:
Proposition

12.22

•

\ref{itm:circle2}\Rightarrow\ref{itm:circle3}
:
Proposition

12.21

•

\ref{itm:circle3}\Rightarrow\ref{itm:circle4}
:
Proposition

7.10

•

\ref{itm:circle4}\Rightarrow\ref{itm:circle5}
:
Proposition

8.1

•

\ref{itm:circle5}\Rightarrow\ref{itm:circle1}
:
Proposition

8.8
∎

We obtain the following algorithmic version of flip-breakability by combining
Theorem

13.1
and
Proposition

8.1
.

Theorem 13.2
.

For every monadically dependent class
\mathcal{C}
and radius
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists an unbounded function

f_{r}:\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, a constant
k_{r}\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, and an algorithm that, given a graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
, computes in time
O_{\mathcal{C},r}(|V(G)|^{2})
two subsets
A,B\subseteq W
with
|A|,|B|\geqslant f_{r}(|W|)

and a
k_{r}
-flip
H
of
G
such that:

\textnormal{dist}_{H}(A,B)>r.

See
3.1

Proof.

The implication
(ii)
\rightarrow
(iii)
is immediate.
We prove the implications
(i)
\rightarrow
(ii)
and
(iii)
\rightarrow
(i)
.

(i)
\rightarrow
(ii)
.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
,
r\geqslant 1
and a graph class
\mathcal{C}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{r}
denote the class of bipartite graphs
G
such that some radius-
r
encoding of
G
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
,
and let
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{(1)}
denote the class of
1
-subdivisions of the bipartite graphs in
\mathcal{B}_{r}
.
By Lemma
12.23
, the class
\mathcal{C}
transduces the class
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{(1)}
.
As
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent,
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{(1)}
is as well. As
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{(1)}
is
weakly sparse
6
6

6

A graph class
\mathcal{C}
is
weakly sparse
if there is a bound
t
such that
\mathcal{C}
excludes the biclique
K_{t,t}
of order
t
as a subgraph. In our case
\mathcal{C}
consists of subdivided graphs and excludes
K_{2,2}
as a subgraph.
and monadically dependent, it is nowhere dense
(this follows from
[
24
]
, see
[
45
]
). As the class
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{(1)}
of
1
-subdivisions of graphs from
\mathcal{B}_{r}
is nowhere dense,
it follows that
\mathcal{B}_{r}
is nowhere dense
(indeed, if
\mathcal{B}_{r}
contains some
k
-subdivided clique
K_{n}
as a subgraph, for some
k,n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, then
\mathcal{B}_{r}^{(1)}
contains the
(2k+1)
-subdivided clique
K_{n}
as a subgraph).
Since
\mathcal{B}_{r}
is nowhere dense, it follows from
[
23
]
(see also
[
43
]
) that

|E(G)|\leqslant O_{\mathcal{B}_{r},\varepsilon}(|V(G)|^{1+\varepsilon})\leqslant O_{\mathcal{C},r,\varepsilon}(|V(G)|^{1+\varepsilon})
. The conclusion follows.

(iii)
\rightarrow
(i)
.
We proceed by contrapositive. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary, monadically independent graph class. By
Theorem

13.1
,
there is some
r\geqslant 1
such that for every
n
,

\mathcal{C}
contains some flipped
r
-crossing of order
n
,
or the comparability grid of order
n+1
.
As mentioned earlier, those contain as induced subgraphs radius-
r
encodings (
r=1
in the case of comparability grids) of the complete bipartite graph
K_{n,n}
of order
n
,
which has
|E(K_{n,n})|=n^{2}
and
|V(K_{n,n})|=2n
,
and therefore
|E(K_{n,n})|\geqslant|V(K_{n,n})|^{2}/4
.
As
n
is arbitrarily large, this proves the negation of condition
(iii)
, and finishes the proof of the implication.
∎

Part III
Lower Bounds

In this part we present several algorithmic and combinatorial lower bounds for hereditary, monadically independent graph classes.

14
Hardness of Model Checking

This section is devoted to proving the following theorem.

See
1.7

We show this by reducing from the first-order model checking problem on the class of all graphs, which is
\mathrm{AW}[*]
-complete
[
19
]
.
Our main tool is that of an
interpretation
.

Definition 14.1
.

Let
\delta(x)
and
\varphi(x,y)
be formulas, where
\varphi
is symmetric and irreflexive.
The
interpretation

I_{\delta,\varphi}
is defined as the operation that maps a given input graph
G
to the output graph
I_{\delta,\varphi}(G):=H
where
H
has vertex set
V(H):=\{v\in V(G):G\models\delta(v)\}
and edge set
\{(u,v)\in V(H)^{2}:G\models\varphi(u,v)\}.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}

efficiently interprets
a class
\mathcal{D}
if there exists
a polynomial-time computable
function
f\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
such that

I(f(H))=H
for all
H\in\mathcal{D}
.

Note that since the graph
f(H)
is computed in polynomial time given
H
, in particular,

|V(f(H))|\leqslant p(|V(H)|)
, for some fixed polynomial
p\colon\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
.

The following property of interpretations is well known and follows by a simple formula rewriting procedure (see, e.g.,
[
39
, Thm. 4.3.1]
).

Lemma 14.2
.

For every interpretation
I
and formula
\psi(\bar{x})
, we can compute a formula
\psi_{\star}(\bar{x})
, such that for all graphs
G
and
H
satisfying
G=I(H)
and every tuple
\bar{a}\in V(G)^{|\bar{x}|}
,

G\models\psi(\bar{a})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad H\models\psi_{\star}(\bar{a}).

Corollary 14.3

(Transitivity)
.

Let
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E}
be classes of graphs such that
\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}
efficiently interprets
\mathcal{E}
.
Then also
\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets
\mathcal{E}
.

Corollary 14.4

(Reduction)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of graphs that efficiently interprets the class of all graphs. Then the first-order model checking problem is
\mathrm{AW[*]}
-hard on
\mathcal{C}
.

We spend the rest of this section proving the following proposition.

Proposition 14.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary graph class and
r\geqslant 1
, such that for all
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{C}
contains

•

a comparability grid of order
k
, or

•

a flipped star
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped clique
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped half-graph
r
-crossing of order
k
.

Then
\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets the class of all graphs.

Using
Theorem

1.6
, this is equivalent to the
Theorem

1.8
, which we restate for convenience.

See
1.8

By
Corollary

14.4
, this then immediately implies
Theorem

1.7
,
stating
\mathrm{AW}[*]
-hardness of the model checking problem on every hereditary, monadically independent graph class.
Moreover,
Theorem

1.8
implies
that a hereditary graph class is dependent if and only if it is monadically dependent.
This equivalence was previously proven by Braunfeld and Laskowski for the more general setting
of hereditary classes of relational structures
[
12
]
.

Note that
it is rather straightforward to prove
that, under the assumptions of
Proposition

14.5
,
some class
\mathcal{C}^{+}
of
colored
graphs
from
\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets the class of all graphs. Indeed, using colors we can mark the

r+2
layers of (an induced subgraph of) a flipped
r
-crossing representing the input graph, and then use the colors to “undo” the flip, thus obtaining
an unflipped
r
-crossing,
with which we can proceed similarly as in the sketched proof of
Proposition

12.22
.
This, however, would yield a significantly weaker statement than
Theorem

1.7
:
that the first-order model checking problem
on
colored
graphs from
\mathcal{C}

is AW[
*
]-hard, for every hereditary, monadically independent class
\mathcal{C}
.

14.1
Crossings and Comparability Grids

Since we explicitly refer to the individual vertices of our
r
-crossing patterns in this section,
let us restate their definition in greater detail and explicitly name their vertex sets.

Definition 14.6

(
r
-crossings)
.

For every radius
r\geqslant 1
we define the
star, clique, and half-graph
r
-crossing
of order
n
as the graph whose vertex set

\{a_{i}:i\in[n]\}\cup\{b_{i}:i\in[n]\}\cup\{p_{i,j,t}:i,j\in[n],t\in[r]\}

is partitioned into
l:=r+2

layers

\mathcal{L}:=\{L_{0},\ldots,L_{r+1}\}
with

•

L_{0}:=\{a_{i}:i\in[n]\}
,

•

L_{t}:=\{p_{i,j,t}:i,j\in[n]\}
for all
t\in[r]
,

•

L_{r+1}:=\{b_{j}:j\in[n]\}
,

and whose edges are defined as follows.
The vertices
(a_{i},p_{i,j,1},\ldots,p_{i,j,r},b_{j})
form a path for all
i,j\in[n]
.
Each of the three types enforces separate additional edges.

•

The star
r
-crossing contains no additional edges.

•

For the clique
r
-crossing,

–

p_{i,j,1}
and
p_{i,j^{\prime},1}
are adjacent for all
j\neq j^{\prime}\in[n]
, and

–

p_{i,j,r}
and
p_{i^{\prime},j,r}
are adjacent for all
i\neq i^{\prime}\in[n]
.

•

For the half-graph
r
-crossing,

–

a_{i}
is adjacent to
p_{i^{\prime},j,1}
for all
i\leqslant i^{\prime}\leqslant n
and for all
j\in[n]
, and

–

b_{j}
is adjacent to
p_{i,j^{\prime},r}
for all
i\in[n]
and for all
j\leqslant j^{\prime}\leqslant n
.

For every
r\geqslant 1
, let
\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r}
,
\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}
be the hereditary closure of the class of all star, clique, and half-graph
r
-crossings, respectively.

Lastly, recall that the
comparability grid
of order
n
is the graph with vertex set
\{a_{i,j}:i,j\in[n]\}
where for all
i\leqslant i^{\prime}
,
a_{i,j}
and
a_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
are adjacent if and only if
i=i^{\prime}
or
j\leqslant j^{\prime}
.
Denote by
\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}
the hereditary closure of the class of all comparability grids.

14.2
Twins

Two vertices
u
and
v
are
twins
in a graph
G
, if
N_{G}(u)\setminus\{u,v\}=N_{G}(v)\setminus\{u,v\}
.
This relation is transitive and definable in first-order logic:

\mathrm{twins}(x,y):=\forall z:(z\neq x\wedge z\neq y)\rightarrow(E(z,x)\leftrightarrow E(z,y)).

The
twin classes
of a graph
G
are the equivalence classes of the twin-relation of
G
.
For every fixed
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
the formulas

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#twins}_{\geqslant k}(x):=\exists z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}:\bigwedge_{(i,j)\in\binom{k}{2}}z_{i}\neq z_{j}\wedge z_{i}\neq x\wedge\mathrm{twins}(z_{i},x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#twins}_{=k}(x):=\mathrm{\#twins}_{\geqslant k}(x)\wedge\neg(\mathrm{\#twins}_{\geqslant k+1}(x))

express that
x
has at least or exactly
k
twins, respectively
(equivalently, the twin class containing
x
has at least or exactly
k+1
elements).

14.3
Reversing Flips

Combining hereditariness and the pigeonhole principle, we observe the following.

Observation 14.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary graph class and
r\geqslant 1
, such that for all
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{C}
contains

•

a comparability grid of order
k
, or

•

a flipped star
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped clique
r
-crossing of order
k
, or

•

a flipped half-graph
r
-crossing of order
k
.

Then
\mathcal{C}
contains either
\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}

or there is
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}\}
, such that
\mathcal{C}
contains
a
layer-wise
flip of each graph in
\mathcal{B}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r}
/
\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r}
/
\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}
contains all the
induced subgraphs
of all star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossings.
A
layer-wise
flip of a graph
G
in
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}\}

is an
\{L_{0},\dots,L_{r+1}\}
-flip of
G
: the flip respects the layered structure of the class
\mathcal{B}
.
As the vertices of the graphs in
\mathcal{B}
are named (cf.
Definition

14.6
), it is clear in which layer each vertex of
G
is located.

In this subsection we use interpretations to undo the flips and recover the graphs from
\mathcal{B}
.
However, we only recover graphs without twins and without isolated vertices.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be the class of all graphs containing twins.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be the class of all graphs containing isolated vertices.

Lemma 14.8
.

Fix
r\geqslant 1
. Let
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}\}

and let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary class containing a layer-wise flip of each graph from
\mathcal{B}
.

\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets
\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
.

Proof.

Denote by
B_{n}
the star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing of order
n
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is hereditary
and by the pigeonhole principle,
we can assume that there exist an integer
k\leqslant r+2
, a mapping
\mathrm{lc}:\{0,\dots,r+1\}\rightarrow[k]
(for
l
ayer
c
olor), and a symmetric relation
R\subseteq[k]^{2}
with the following property.

•

For every
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the
k
-coloring of
V(B_{n})
in which each layer
L_{0},\dots,L_{r+1}
of
B_{n}

is monochromatically colored by the color
\mathrm{lc}(0),\dots,\mathrm{lc}(r+1)
, respectively.
Note that two different layers might be assigned the same color.

The graph
\mathrm{flip}(B_{n}):=B_{n}\oplus_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}R
is contained in
\mathcal{C}
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume
k
to be minimal in the following sense.

•

Every color is used: the map
\mathrm{lc}
is surjective.

•

No two colors can be merged: for all
i\neq j\in[k]
there exists
d\in[k]
such that

(i,d)\in R\Leftrightarrow(j,d)\not\in R.

Having fixed
\mathrm{lc}
, from now on we assume every
B_{n}
to be implicitly
k
-colored,
and that
\mathcal{B}
is the hereditary closure of these
k
-colored graphs.
We extend our notion
\mathrm{flip}(G):=G\oplus_{\mathcal{K}}R
to all graphs
G\in\mathcal{B}
,
where
\mathcal{K}
is the aforementioned
k
-coloring associated with
G
.
Note that
\mathrm{flip}(G)\in\mathcal{C}
for all
G\in\mathcal{B}
.
Our notion of layers carries over to all graphs
G
, and
\mathrm{flip}(G)
in the obvious way.
We use twin classes to uniquely quantify representative vertices of each color class.
The representatives are added as follows.
For any
k
-colored graph
G
, let
\mathrm{prep}(G)
be the graph
obtained from
G
by adding, for each color
i\in[k]
,

(i+1)
many isolated vertices
s_{i,1},\ldots,s_{i,(i+1)}
of color
i
to
G
.

Claim 14.9
.

If
G\in\mathcal{B}
, then the same holds for
\mathrm{prep}(G)
.

Proof.

By assumption, there exists an embedding
f
from
G
to
B_{n}
, for some
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
c:=r\cdot(l+1)
and
m:=n+2c
.
We show how to embed
\mathrm{prep}(G)
into
B_{m}
.
First note that the function

g(\cdot):=\bigcup_{\mathclap{i,j\in[n],t\in[r]}}\,\{a_{i}\mapsto a_{i+c},\quad b_{j}\mapsto b_{j+c},\quad p_{i,j,t}\mapsto p_{i+c,j+c,t}\}

is an embedding from
B_{n}
to
B_{m}
.
It follows that
h(\cdot):=g\circ f
is an embedding from
G
to
B_{m}
.
Let
h(G):=\{h(v):v\in V(G)\}
.
Importantly, every vertex
a_{i}
,
b_{j}
, or
p_{i,j,t}
in
h(G)
satisfies

i,j\in\{c+1,\ldots,c+n\}\quad\text{and}\quad t\in[k].

We next choose a set
S(L)
of
(l+1)
vertices from each layer
L\in\{L_{0},\dots,L_{r+1}\}
as follows.

•

S(L_{0}):=\{a_{c+n+i}:i\in[l+1]\}
,

•

S(L_{t}):=\{p_{i^{\prime}+i,i^{\prime}+i,t}:i\in[l+1]\}
for every
t\in[r]
and
i^{\prime}:=(t-1)\cdot(l+1)
,

•

S(L_{r+1}):=\{b_{c+n+j}:j\in[l+1]\}
.

See
Figure

19
for a visualization.

Figure 19:
A visualization of how the sets
S(L)
are embedded into
B_{m}
for the case
r=3
.
To preserve readability in the visualization, each set
S(L)
has size
3
instead of
l+1=6
.
The black vertices correspond to the embedding of
B_{n}
into
B_{m}
.

We define
I:=S(L_{0})\cup\ldots\cup S(L_{r+1})
.
By construction,
I
and
h(G)
are disjoint.
Let us now argue that every vertex from
I
is isolated in the induced subgraph
B^{\prime}_{m}:=B_{m}[h(G)\cup I]
.

•

Let
a_{i}\in I
. Then
i>c+n
.
All the neighbors of
a_{i}
in
B_{m}
are of the form
p_{i^{\prime},j,t}
for some
i^{\prime}\geqslant i
.
All vertices
p_{i^{\prime},j,t}
in
B^{\prime}_{m}
satisfy
i^{\prime}\leqslant c+n
, so they are non-adjacent to
a_{i}
.

•

Let
b_{j}\in I
. The same reasoning as in the previous case applies.

•

Let
p_{i,j,t}\in I
. Then
i\leqslant c
and
j\leqslant c
. By the same reasoning as before,
p_{i,j,t}
is non-adjacent to all vertices of the form
a_{i^{\prime}}
or
b_{j^{\prime}}
in
B^{\prime}_{m}
.
Furthermore, any neighbor of
p_{i,j,t}
of the form
p_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
in
B_{m}
must satisfy
i^{\prime}=i
or
j^{\prime}=j
. By construction,
B^{\prime}_{m}
contains no vertex
p_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
satisfying
i^{\prime}=i
or
j^{\prime}=j
, apart from
p_{i,j,t}
itself.

This proves that the set
I
is indeed isolated in
B^{\prime}_{m}
.
We want to stress that our argument works for each of the three classes
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}\}
.

We finally embed
\mathrm{prep}(G)
into
B^{\prime}_{m}
.
The vertices of
G
are embedded using
h
.
The additional vertices
s_{i,1},\ldots,s_{i,(i+1)}
that are added to
\mathrm{prep}(G)
for each color
i\in[k]
can now be mapped to distinct vertices from a set
S(L)
to which the layer coloring
\mathrm{lc}
assigns color
i
.
As desired, they have color
i
and are isolated.
∎

Claim 14.10
.

Let
G\in\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
.
The twin classes of
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))
consist exactly of

•

the singleton twin class
\{v\}
for every vertex
v\in V(G)
, and

•

the twin class
T_{i}:=\{s_{i,1},\ldots,s_{i,i+1}\}
for every color
i\in[k]
.

Proof.

The following is easy to see.

\begin{gathered}\text{For all vertices $u,v$ with the same color:}\\
\text{$u$ and $v$ are twins in $\mathrm{prep}(G)$ if and only if they are twins in $\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))$.}\end{gathered}

(11)

Additionally, we argue the following.

For all vertices
u,v
with different colors:
u
and
v
are not twins in
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G)).

(12)

Let
i\neq j
be the colors of
u
and
v
.
By the assumed minimality of the coloring, there exist a color
d\in[k]
such that
(i,d)\in R\Leftrightarrow(j,d)\not\in R
.
There exists at least one vertex
s_{d}\in\{s_{d,1},s_{d,2}\}
that has color
d
and is non-adjacent and non-equal to both
u
and
v
in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
.
It follows that in
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))
exactly one of
u
and
v
will be adjacent to
s_{d}
. Thus,
u
and
v
are no twins in
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))
.

Combining (
11
) and (
12
), we have that every two vertices
u
and
v
which are no twins in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
are also no twins in
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))
.
Since
G\notin\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I}
, in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
the vertices of
V(G)
neither have twins among
V(G)
nor among the isolated vertices added to build
\mathrm{prep}(G)
from
G
.
It follows that each vertex from
V(G)
is contained in a singleton twin class of
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))
as desired.
Finally, by (
11
), for every color
i\in[k]
there is a twin class
T_{i}
containing the set of isolated vertices
\{s_{i,1},\ldots,s_{i,i+1}\}
.
As argued before,
T_{i}
contains no vertices from
V(G)
.
By (
12
),

T_{i}
is disjoint from
T_{j}
for every other color
j\neq i
.
Then
T_{i}
is exactly
\{s_{i,1},\ldots,s_{i,i+1}\}
, as desired.
∎

Claim 14.11
.

For every color
i\in[k]
there exists a formula
\textnormal{col}_{i}(x)
such that
for every
G\in\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
and every vertex
v
in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
we have

\text{$v$ has color $i$}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\models\textnormal{col}_{i}(x).

Proof.

We argue that the following formula does the job.

\displaystyle\textnormal{col}_{i}(x):=\exists z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}:\bigwedge_{j\in k}x\neq z_{j}\wedge\mathrm{\#twins}_{=j}(z_{j})\wedge\bigl(E(x,z_{j})\leftrightarrow(i,j)\in R\bigr)

The formula quantifies vertices
\bar{z}=z_{1}\ldots z_{k}
containing for each color
j\in[k]
a vertex
z_{j}
such that

•

z_{j}
is not equal to
x
,

•

z_{j}
is from a twin class of size exactly
j+1
,

•

z_{j}
is adjacent to
x
if and only if
(i,j)\in R
.

Let
v
be a vertex in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
.
To prove the forwards direction of the claim, assume
v
has color
i
.
We can choose a satisfying valuation
\bar{w}
of
\bar{z}
as follows.
By
14.10
, for each color
j\in[k]
the twin class
T_{j}
has size exactly
j+1
and all its vertices are isolated in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
and have color
j
.
As
|T_{j}|\geqslant 2
, we can pick a vertex
w_{j}\in T_{j}
that is not equal to
v
.
As
v
and
w_{j}
are non-adjacent in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
and of color
i
and
j
respectively, we have
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\models E(v,w_{j})\Leftrightarrow(i,j)\in R
as desired.

For the backwards direction, assume towards contradiction that
v
has color
i^{\prime}\neq i
and there exists a satisfying valuation
\bar{w}
of
\bar{z}
.
By the assumed minimality of the coloring of
\mathrm{prep}(G)
, there exist a color
d\in[k]
such that

(i^{\prime},d)\in R\Leftrightarrow(i,d)\notin R.

Again
w_{d}
has color
d
and is non-adjacent to
v
in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
.
By definition of
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))
we have

\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\models E(v,w_{d})\Leftrightarrow(i^{\prime},d)\in R.

However, for
\bar{w}
to be a satisfying valuation of
\bar{z}
we must have

\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\models E(v,w_{d})\Leftrightarrow(i,d)\in R.

Combining the three equivalences gives the desired contradiction.
∎

Claim 14.12
.

For every formula
\varphi(\bar{x})
we can compute a formula
\mathrm{flip}(\varphi)(\bar{x})

such that for every graph
G\in\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
and every tuple
\bar{a}\in V(G)^{|x|}
,

\mathrm{prep}(G)\models\varphi(\bar{a})\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\models\mathrm{flip}(\varphi)(\bar{a}).

Proof.

Using
14.11
, it is easy to see that
for all graphs
G\in\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
and vertices
u
and
v
in
\mathrm{prep}(G)
,

\mathrm{prep}(G)\models E(u,v)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\models E(u,v)\text{ XOR }\bigvee_{i,j\in[k]}\textnormal{col}_{i}(x)\wedge\textnormal{col}_{j}(y)\wedge(i,j)\in R.

For every
\varphi(\bar{x})
, let
\mathrm{flip}(\varphi)(\bar{x})
be the formula obtained by replacing every occurrence of
E(x,y)
with the formula on the right side of the above equivalence.
It now easily follows by structural induction that
\mathrm{flip}(\varphi)(\bar{x})
has the desired properties.
∎

Let
\delta(x):=\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{hasNeighbor})(x)
and
\varphi(x,y):=\mathrm{flip}(E)(x,y)
, where
\mathrm{hasNeighbor}(x)
is the formula checking that
x
is not an isolated vertex.
Using
14.12
, we have

I_{\delta,\varphi}\big(\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep}(G))\big)=G

for every graph
G\in\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
.
As
\mathrm{flip}(\mathrm{prep})(G)
is contained in
\mathcal{C}
and can be computed in polynomial time from
G
, we have that
\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets
\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
.
∎

14.4
Encoding Bipartite Graphs

Having undone the flips, we interpret all bipartite graphs (without isolated vertices) from our intermediate classes
\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}\}
.
Targeting this class of bipartite graphs does not restrict the general case, as the following lemma shows.

Lemma 14.13
.

The class of all bipartite graphs without isolated vertices efficiently interprets the class of all graphs.

Proof.

Let
\delta(x)
be the formula stating that
x
has degree at least three and
\varphi(x,y)
be the formula stating that
x
and
y
are at distance exactly two.
For every graph
G
, we build the graph
B_{G}
as follows.
For every vertex
v\in V(G)
we create a star with three leaves and center
c_{v}
.
For every edge
(u,v)\in E(G)
, we add a new vertex adjacent to both
c_{u}
and
c_{v}
.
It is easy to see that
B_{G}
is bipartite, without isolated vertices, and
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{G})=G
.
∎

The following notation will be convenient.
For every bipartite graph
H
there exists at least one

bipartite representation of
H
, that is, a tuple

H^{\prime}=(U^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{\mathbb{N}},V^{\prime}\subseteq\mathrm{\mathbb{N}},E(H^{\prime})\subseteq U^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}),

such that there exist

•

a bipartition of
V(H)
into two independent sets
U
and
V
, and

•

two bijections
f:U\rightarrow U^{\prime}
and
g:V\rightarrow V^{\prime}
,

such that for all
u\in U
and
v\in V
:

(f(u),f(v))\in E(H^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow(u,v)\in E(H)
.
Note that
U^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
do not have to be disjoint and
E(H^{\prime})
is not necessarily symmetric.

Encoding Bipartite Graphs in Star and Clique
\boldsymbol{r}
-Crossings

Lemma 14.14
.

For every
r\geqslant 1
and
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r}\}
,

\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
efficiently interprets the class of all bipartite graphs.

Proof.

First assume
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r}
.
Let
\delta(x)
be the formula checking whether
x
has degree at least three, and let
\varphi(x,y)
be the formula checking whether the distance between
x
and
y
is exactly
r+1
.
To prove the lemma, we show that for every bipartite graph
H
, we can construct a graph
B_{H}\in\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
such that
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{H})=H
.
Let

H^{\prime}=([n],[m],E(H^{\prime})\subseteq[n]\times[m])

be a bipartite representation of
H
for some
n,m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
.
We build the graph
B_{H}
as follows.
For every
i\in[n]
we create a
1
-subdivided star with three leaves consisting of: a center
c_{i}
and for every
s\in\{0,1,2\}
a subdivision vertex
c_{i,s,1}
and a leaf
c_{i,s,2}
such that
(c_{i},c_{i,s,1},c_{i,s,2})
form a path.
We do the same for every
j\in[m]
, giving us vertices
d_{j},d_{j,s,1},d_{j,s,2}
for every
s\in\{0,1,2\}
.
Finally, for every edge
(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime})
we add vertices
\{q_{i,j,t}:t\in[r]\}
and connect
(c_{i},q_{i,j,1},\ldots,q_{i,j,r},d_{j})
to form a path of length
r+1
.
It is easy to see that
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{H})=H
and that

B_{H}
contains neither twins nor isolated vertices.
It remains to show that
B_{H}
is an induced subgraph of a star
r
-crossing.

Let
N:=3(n+m)
and
B_{N}
be the star
r
-crossing of order
N
.
We give an embedding
h:V(B_{H})\rightarrow V(B_{N})
of
B_{H}
into
B_{N}
.
Let
f(i):=3i-2
and
g(j):=3n+3j-2
. We define
h
as follows for all
i\in[n]
,
j\in[m]
,
s\in\{0,1,2\}
, and
t\in[r]
.

•

h(c_{i}):=a_{f(i)}

•

h(c_{i,s,1}):=p_{f(i),f(i)+s,1}

•

h(c_{i,s,2}):=\begin{cases}p_{f(i),f(i)+s,2}&\text{if }r>1\\
b_{f(i)+s}&\text{if }r=1\end{cases}

•

h(q_{i,j,t}):=p_{f(i),g(j),t}

•

h(d_{j}):=b_{g(j)}

•

h(d_{j,s,1}):=p_{g(j)+s,g(j),r}

•

h(d_{j,s,2}):=\begin{cases}p_{g(j)+s,g(j),r-1}&\text{if }r>1\\
a_{g(j)+s}&\text{if }r=1\end{cases}

See
Figure

20
for a visualization of the embedding.
It is easily checked that
h
is an embedding. This finishes the case
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r}
.

Figure 20:
A visualization of how
B_{H}
embeds into
B_{N}
for the case where
r=3
and
H
is the biclique of order
3
.

For the case
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r}
, we take the same edge formula
\varphi
and update the domain formula to state

\delta(x):=\text{``$x$ has degree at least three and the neighborhood of $x$ is a clique''}.

We build
B_{H}
as in the previous case but add additional edges.
For every
i\in[n]
we turn the set

\bigcup\bigl\{\{c_{i,s,1},c_{i,s,2},q_{i,j,1}\}:j\in[m],(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime}),s\in\{0,1,2\}\bigr\}

that contains exactly the neighborhood of
c_{i}
into a clique.
Symmetrically, we do the same for every
j\in[m]
with the set

\bigcup\bigl\{\{d_{j,s,1},d_{j,s,2},q_{i,j,r}\}:i\in[n],(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime}),s\in\{0,1,2\}\bigr\}

of all neighbors of
d_{j}
.
Again
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{H})=H
,
B_{H}
contains neither twins nor isolated vertices, and
h
is an embedding of
B_{H}
into the clique
r
-crossing of order
3(n+m)
.
∎

Encoding Bipartite Graphs in Half-Graph
\boldsymbol{r}
-Crossings and in Comparability Grids

Lemma 14.15
.

For every
r\geqslant 1
and
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}\}
,

\mathcal{B}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
efficiently interprets the class of all bipartite graphs without isolated vertices.

Proof.

We first prove the statement for
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}
.
For every
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, we define
\llbracket n\rrbracket:=[n]\setminus\{1,n\}=\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
.
Let
H
be an arbitrary bipartite graph without isolated vertices and let

H^{\prime}=(\llbracket n\rrbracket,\llbracket m\rrbracket,E(H^{\prime})\subseteq\llbracket n\rrbracket\times\llbracket m\rrbracket)

be a bipartite representation of
H
for some
n,m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
.
We define
B_{H}
to be the subgraph of the half-graph
r
-crossing of order
\max(n,m)
induced by the vertices

•

A:=\{a_{i}:i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\}
corresponding to the left vertices of
H
,

•

B:=\{b_{j}:j\in\llbracket m\rrbracket\}
corresponding to the right vertices of
H
,

•

P:=\{p_{i,j,t}:(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime}),t\in[r]\}
corresponding to the edges of
H
,

•

\{a_{n},p_{n,1,1}\}
and
\{b_{m},p_{1,m,r}\}
which we use as auxiliary vertices.

See the left side of
Figure

21
for a visualization.

Figure 21:
On the left: a visualization of the vertices of
B_{H}
where
H
is the half-graph of order
4
and
r=3
.
The vertices in
A
,
B
, and
P
are colored red, blue, and black respectively.
On the right: a visualization of the embedding of
B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H}
into a comparability grid, where again
H
is the half-graph of order
4
.

Our goal is to interpret
H
from
B_{H}
.
Let

A_{\star}:=A\cup\{a_{n}\},\quad B_{\star}:=B\cup\{b_{m}\},\quad P_{\star}:=P\cup\{p_{n,1,1},p_{1,m,r}\}.

Claim 14.16
.

N_{B_{H}}(p_{n,1,1})=A_{\star}

and

N_{B_{H}}(p_{1,m,r})=B_{\star}
.

Proof.

The claim follows by the definition of the adjacencies in half-graph crossings.

A_{\star}
is included in the neighborhood of
p_{n,1,1}
because
i\leqslant n
for all
i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\cup\{n\}
.
All neighbors of
B_{\star}
are of the form
p_{i,j,t}
for some
j\geqslant 2
, so no vertex of
B_{\star}
is included in the neighborhood of
p_{n,1,1}
.
The vertex
p_{n,1,2}
is not contained in
P_{\star}
, so no vertex of
P_{\star}
is included in the neighborhood of
p_{n,1,1}
.
We argue symmetrically to determine the neighborhood of
p_{1,m,r}
.
∎

Observation 14.17
.

N_{B_{H}}(a_{n})=\{p_{n,1,1}\}
and

N_{B_{H}}(b_{m})=\{p_{1,m,r}\}
.

Claim 14.18
.

Except for
a_{n}
and
b_{m}
, all vertices have degree at least two.

Proof.

All vertices in
A
are adjacent to
p_{n,1,1}
and at least one other
p_{i,j,1}
vertex since
H
contains no isolated vertices.
A symmetric statement holds for
B
and
p_{1,m,r}
.
The vertices in
P
are inner vertices of paths from
A
to
B
, so they have degree at least two.
The vertices
p_{n,1,1}
and
p_{1,m,r}
have high degree by the previous claim.
∎

Observation 14.19
.

B_{H}
contains no isolated vertices.

Claim 14.20
.

B_{H}
contains no twins.

Proof.

The vertices in
A_{\star}
can be distinguished from the vertices in
B_{\star}
and
P_{\star}
by their adjacency to
p_{n,1,1}
.
Two vertices
a_{i}
and
a_{i^{\prime}}
from
A_{\star}
with
i<i^{\prime}\leqslant n
can be differentiated by a vertex
p_{i,j,1}
for some
j\in\llbracket m\rrbracket
which is adjacent to
a_{i}
but not to
a_{i^{\prime}}
.
This
j
exist since no vertex in
H
is isolated.
It follows that
A_{\star}
contains no twins and by a symmetric argument, neither does
B_{\star}
.

It remains to distinguish the vertices inside
P_{\star}
.
The auxiliary vertices
p_{n,1,1}
and
p_{1,m,r}
each have a private neighbor in
a_{n}
and
b_{m}
, so we can focus our attention on the set
P
.
Let
p_{i,j,t}
and
p_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
be two distinct vertices from
P
. By symmetry, we can assume that either
i<i^{\prime}
or
j<j^{\prime}
or
t<t^{\prime}
.
If
i<i^{\prime}
or
t<t^{\prime}
then
the vertex

d:=\begin{cases}p_{i,j,t-1}&\text{if }t>1,\\
a_{i}&\text{if }t=1,\end{cases}

is adjacent to
p_{i,j,t}
but non-adjacent to
p_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},t^{\prime}}
.
If
j<j^{\prime}
we argue symmetrically using either
p_{i,j,t+1}
or
b_{j}
if
t=r
.
We want to stress that the argument works also for the case of
r=1
.
∎

We have proven that
B_{H}\in\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
.
Let us continue to show that
B_{H}
interprets
H
.
Combining
14.17
,
14.16
,
14.18
, we have that the formula

\delta(x):=\text{``$x$ has degree at least $2$ and is at distance exactly $2$ from a degree $1$ vertex''}

is true exactly on the vertices from
A\cup B
.
It can therefore act as the domain formula of our interpretation.
It remains to define the edge relation.
First notice that the formula

\mathrm{sameSide}(x,y):=\exists z:\text{``$z$ has degree $1$ and is at distance exactly $2$ from both $x$ and $y$''}

distinguishes
A_{\star}
and
B_{\star}
: for all vertices
u
and
v

B_{H}\models\mathrm{sameSide}(u,v)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\{u,v\}\in A_{\star}\vee\{u,v\}\in B_{\star}.

We next construct a formula that resolves the half-graphs between
A
and
P_{\star}
and between
B
and
P_{\star}
in the following sense.

Claim 14.21
.

There exists a formula
E_{0}(x,y)
such that for all
i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket
,

\{v\in V(B_{H})\mid B_{H}\models E_{0}(a_{i},v)\}=\{p_{i,j,1}:(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime}),j\in\llbracket m\rrbracket\},

and for all
j\in\llbracket m\rrbracket
,

\{v\in V(B_{H})\mid B_{H}\models E_{0}(b_{j},v)\}=\{p_{i,j,r}:(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime}),i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\}.

Proof.

The formula

x\prec y:=\mathrm{sameSide}(x,y)\wedge\text{``$N(y)$ is a strict subset of $N(x)$''}

orders
A_{\star}
and
B_{\star}
respectively:
for all
a_{i}\in A_{\star}
and
b_{j}\in B_{\star}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl(B_{H}\models a_{i}\prec a\bigr)\Leftrightarrow\bigl(a\in\{a_{i^{\prime}}:i<i^{\prime}\leqslant n\}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad\bigl(B_{H}\models b_{j}\prec b\bigr)\Leftrightarrow\bigl(b\in\{b_{j^{\prime}}:j<j^{\prime}\leqslant m\}\bigr).

Now the following formula

E_{0}(x,y):=E(x,y)\wedge\neg\bigl(\exists x^{\prime}:x\prec x^{\prime}\wedge E(x^{\prime},y)\bigr)

has the desired properties.
∎

We can finally construct the formula interpreting the edges of
H

\varphi(x,y):=\neg\mathrm{sameSide}(x,y)\wedge\exists z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}:E_{0}(x,z_{1})\wedge E_{0}(y,z_{r})\wedge\bigwedge_{t<r}E(z_{t},z_{t+1}),

which states that
x
and
y
are from different sides and connected by a path containing
r+1
edges and whose first and last edge are
E_{0}
-edges.
The formula defines a bipartite graph with sides
A
and
B
:
it is symmetric, and we have
B_{H}\not\models\varphi(u,v)
for all vertices
u
and
v
such that
\{u,v\}\subseteq A
or
\{u,v\}\subseteq B
.

Claim 14.22
.

For all
i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket
and
j\in\llbracket m\rrbracket
:

B_{H}\models\varphi(a_{i},b_{j})\Leftrightarrow(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Assume
(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime})
. Then
p_{i,j,1},\ldots,p_{i,j,r}
is a valuation of
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
witnessing that
B_{H}\models\varphi(a_{i},b_{j})
.
For the backwards direction assume a satisfying valuation
p_{1},\ldots,p_{r}
of
z_{1},\ldots,z_{r}
.
By
14.21
we have
p_{1}=p_{i,j^{\prime},1}
and
p_{r}=p_{i^{\prime},j,r}
for some
j^{\prime}\in\llbracket m\rrbracket\setminus\{j\}
and
i^{\prime}\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\setminus\{i\}
.
The existence of
p_{2},\ldots,p_{r-1}
implies
i=i^{\prime}
and
j=j^{\prime}
.
Hence,
(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime})
.
∎

It follows that
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{H})=H
.
Since the definition of
\delta
and
\varphi
does not depend on
H
, and
H
was chosen to be an arbitrary bipartite graph without isolated vertices, we have that this interpretation interprets all such
H
from their corresponding preimage
B_{H}\in\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}\setminus(\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{I})
.
Furthermore, for every
H
the preimage
B_{H}
can be computed in polynomial time, so the interpretation is efficient.
This finishes the case of
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}
.

We next show that the interpretation that we constructed for the previous case, where
r=1
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{1}
,
also works for the case
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}
.
In the previous case, we constructed for every bipartite graph without isolated vertices
H
, a graph
B_{H}\in\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{1}
such that
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{H})=H
.
In this case, since
r=1
, the formula
\varphi
collapses to

\varphi(x,y):=\neg\mathrm{sameSide}(x,y)\wedge\exists z_{1}:E_{0}(x,z_{1})\wedge E_{0}(y,z_{1})

and the graph
B_{H}
consists of vertices

\underbrace{\{a_{i}:i\in\llbracket n\rrbracket\}}_{=A}\cup\underbrace{\{b_{j}:j\in\llbracket m\rrbracket\}}_{=B}\cup\underbrace{\{p_{i,j,1}:(i,j)\in E(H^{\prime})\}}_{=P}\cup\{a_{n},p_{n,1,1}\}\cup\{b_{m},p_{1,m,1}\}

where
H^{\prime}
is again the bipartite representation of
H
.
We now build a new graph
B^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}_{H}
from
B_{H}
by adding additional edges as follows.
We connect each vertex
p_{i,j,1}\in P
with all the vertices
p_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}\in P
such that
i\leqslant i^{\prime}
and
j\leqslant j^{\prime}
(but not with itself).
It is easily checked that all the previous claims for
B_{H}
, still hold true for
B^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}_{H}
:

•

We only modified adjacencies inside
P
,
so
14.16
and
14.17
also hold in
B^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}_{H}
.

•

The degree of vertices in
P
only increased,
so
14.18
and
14.19
also hold in
B^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}_{H}
.

•

As
r=1
,
P
is pairwise distinguished using only
A
and
B
, so
B^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}_{H}
contains no twins (
14.20
).

•

The construction of the formulas
\mathrm{sameSide}(x,y)
and
E_{0}(x,y)
only depends on the previous claims and the neighborhoods of
A
and
B
, so the formulas still work as intended in
B^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}_{H}
.

It follows that
B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H}
contains neither isolated vertices nor twins and that
I_{\delta,\varphi}(B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H})=I_{\delta,\varphi}(B_{H})=H
.
It remains to show that
B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H}\in\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}
.
We do this by showing that
B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H}
is an induced subgraph of
the comparability grid
G_{N}
of order
N:=(n+m-1)
whose vertex set is
\{a_{i,j}:i,j\in[N]\}
.
We witness this fact by constructing an embedding
f
of
B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H}
into
G_{N}
as follows:

•

f(a_{i}):=a_{m+i-1,n-i+1}
for all
a_{i}\in A_{\star}
,

•

f(b_{j}):=a_{m-j+1,n+j-1}
for all
b_{j}\in B_{\star}
,

•

f(p_{i,j,1}):=a_{m+i-1,n+j-1}
for all
p_{i,j,1}\in P_{\star}
.

See the right side of
Figure

21
for a visualization of the embedding.
Using this visualization, it is easy to check that
f
is indeed an embedding of
B^{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}_{H}
into
G_{N}
.
This finishes the case for
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}
and concludes the proof.
∎

14.5
Proof of
Proposition

14.5

See
14.5

Proof.

We first show that
\mathcal{C}
efficiently interprets the class of all bipartite graphs without isolated vertices.
By
14.7
, we distinguish three cases.

•

Either
\mathcal{C}
contains a layer-wise flip of each graph from
\mathcal{B}
, for
\mathcal{B}\in\{\mathcal{B}^{\star}_{r},\mathcal{B}^{\bullet}_{r}\}
,
and we can apply
Lemma

14.8
and
Lemma

14.14
,

•

or
\mathcal{C}
contains a layer-wise flip of each graph from
\mathcal{B}^{\scriptscriptstyle\blacktriangleleft}_{r}
,
and we can apply
Lemma

14.8
and
Lemma

14.15
,

•

or
\mathcal{C}
contains
\mathcal{B}^{{\vbox{\hbox{\rule{1.80835pt}{1.80835pt}}}}}
and we can apply
Lemma

14.15
.

From there,
Lemma

14.13
brings us to the class of all graphs.
∎

15
Almost Bounded Twin-Width

Twin-width is a graph parameter recently introduced in
[
9
]
.
We quickly recall its definition.
A
contraction sequence
for an
n
-vertex graph
G
is a sequence of partitions of
V(G)

\mathcal{P}_{1}=\{\{v\}:v\in V(G)\},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{V(G)\}

where
\mathcal{P}_{i+1}
is obtained from
\mathcal{P}_{i}
by merging two parts.
The
red-degree
of a part
P\in\mathcal{P}_{i}
is the number of parts
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{i}\setminus\{P\}
, such that
P
and
Q
are neither fully adjacent nor fully non-adjacent in
G
.
The
red-degree
of a partition is the largest red-degree among its parts.
The
twin-width
of a graph
G
, denoted
\mathrm{tww}(G)
, is the smallest integer
d
such that
G
has a contraction sequence in which every partition has red-degree at most
d
.
A graph class
\mathcal{C}
has
almost bounded twin-width

if for every
\varepsilon>0
we have

\mathrm{tww}(G)\leqslant O_{\mathcal{C},\varepsilon}(n^{\varepsilon})\qquad\text{for every $n$-vertex graph $G\in\mathcal{C}$.}

The goal of this section is to show the following.

See
1.10

Note that in Section
16

we prove that the more general classes of
almost bounded flip-width
are monadically dependent.
This immediately implies Theorem
1.10
, but we provide a self-contained proof in this section.

We prove the theorem by analyzing the induced subgraphs appearing in classes that are monadically independent:
flipped star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossings and comparability grids.

Lemma 15.1
.

Let
G
be a flipped star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing of order
n
.
Then
\mathrm{tww}(G)\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{r+1}}-2
.

The following proof closely follows the proof of
[
10
, Proposition 6.2]
, where it was proven that the
r
-subdivided clique of order
n
has twin-width
\Omega_{r}(n^{c})
for some
c>0
.
We adapt their proof to the bipartite setting (which slightly simplifies the setup), and generalize it to also work with half-graph crossings (which makes the proof slightly more complicated).

Proof of
Lemma

15.1
.

We use the notation for
r
-crossings presented in
Definition

14.6
.
Let
d
be the twin-width of
G
and
fix a witnessing contraction sequence.
By the bipartite symmetry of
G
, we can assume that there is a step in the sequence, where two vertices
a_{x},a_{y}\in L_{0}
with
x<y
are contracted, but no vertices from
L_{r+1}
have been contracted so far.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be the partition of
V(G)
at this step, and
P_{0}\in\mathcal{P}
be the part containing
a_{x}
and
a_{y}
.
For every set
S\subseteq[n]
define the sets
V_{t}(S):=\{p_{x,j,t}:j\in S\}
for
t\in[r]
and

V_{r+1}(S):=\{b_{j}:j\in S\}
.

Claim 15.2
.

For every
t\in[r+1]
there is
S_{t}\subseteq[n]
with
|S_{t}|\geqslant\frac{n}{(d+2)^{t}}

and
P_{t}\in\mathcal{P}
with
V_{t}(S_{t})\subseteq P_{t}
.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
t
.
By definition of a flipped crossing, each vertex in
V_{1}([n])
is adjacent exactly one vertex of
a_{x}
and
a_{y}
.
Consequently, there is a red edge between
P_{0}
and every
part
P\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{0}\}
that intersects
V_{1}([n])
.
Hence, at most
d+1
parts of
\mathcal{P}
intersect
V_{1}([n])
, so there is a part
P_{1}
containing at least
\frac{n}{d+1}
many vertices from
V_{1}([n])
, and we can choose
S_{1}
accordingly.

In the inductive step,
by the structure of a flipped crossing, the vertices from
V_{t+1}(S_{t})
have an inhomogeneous connection to
V_{t}(S_{t})
,
with the possible exception of the smallest vertex in
S_{t+1}
(if
t=r
and we consider a flipped half-graph crossing).
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the set containing
P_{t}
and the part of
\mathcal{P}
that contains the smallest vertex in
S_{t+1}
.
The aforementioned inhomogeneous connections ensure that
there is a red edge between
P_{t}
and every part
P\in\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
that contains a vertex from
V_{t+1}(S_{t})
.
Then there must be a part
P_{t+1}
that contains at least

\frac{|V_{t+1}(S_{t})|}{d+2}\geqslant\frac{n}{(d+2)^{t+1}}

vertices from
V_{t+1}(S_{t})
. We can choose
S_{t+1}
accordingly.
∎

The above claim yields a part
P_{r+1}
containing at least
\frac{n}{(d+2)^{r+1}}
vertices from
L_{r+1}\supseteq V_{r+1}([n])
.
As no two vertices from
L_{r+1}
have been contracted yet,
P_{r+1}
can contain at most one vertex from
L_{r+1}
. Then we have
d\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{r+1}}-2
.
∎

Lemma 15.3
.

Let
G
be a comparability grid of order
n+1
.
Then
\mathrm{tww}(G)\geqslant n^{\frac{1}{2}}-2
.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of
Lemma

15.1
.
Given a comparability grid of order
n+1
with vertex set
\{0,\dots,n\}\times\{0,\dots,n\}
,
we define
L_{0}=\{0\}\times[n]
and
L_{2}=[n]\times\{0\}
.
By symmetry, we consider the first step in a contraction sequence, where two vertices

(0,x),(0,y)\in L_{0}
with
x<y
are contracted, but no vertices from
L_{2}
have been contracted so far.
For every set
S\subseteq[n]
define the sets
V_{1}(S):=S\times\{x\}
and
V_{2}(S):=S\times\{0\}
.
The remainder of the proof proceeds as in
15.2
and onwards (with
r=1
).
∎

We finally prove
Theorem

1.10
, which we rephrase as follows.

Proposition 15.4
.

For every hereditary, monadically independent graph class
\mathcal{C}
there exists a real
\delta>0
such that for every
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, there is graph
G_{n}\in\mathcal{C}
with
|V(G_{n})|\geqslant n
and
\mathrm{tww}(G_{n})\geqslant|V(G_{n})|^{\delta}
.

Proof.

By
Theorem

13.1
and hereditariness of
\mathcal{C}
, there is
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{C}
contains either a flipped star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing of order
n
or the comparability grid of order
n
.
We choose
G_{n}
to be the flipped
r
-crossing or the comparability grid contained in
\mathcal{C}
.
In both cases we have
n\leqslant|V(G_{n})|\leqslant O_{r}(n^{2})
.
If
G_{n}
is a flipped crossing, we conclude using
Lemma

15.1
.
If
G_{n}
is a comparability grid, we conclude using
Lemma

15.3
.
∎

16
Almost Bounded Flip-Width

The radius-
r
flip-width of a graph
G
,
denoted
\fw_{r}(G)
,
is a graph width parameter recently introduced in
[
55
]
.
It is defined in terms of a generalization of the Cops and Robber game to dense graphs.
We refer the reader to
[
55
]
for a definition.
Using this notion,
a hereditary graph class
\mathcal{C}
has
almost bounded flip-width

if for every fixed radius
r\geqslant 1
and
\varepsilon>0
we have

\fw_{r}(G)\leqslant O_{\mathcal{C},\varepsilon}(n^{\varepsilon})\qquad\text{for every $n$-vertex graph $G\in\mathcal{C}$.}

In
[
55
, Conjecture 10.7]
,
the following conjecture is posed:

See
1.11

In this section, we confirm the forwards implication of Conjecture
1.11

by proving the following theorem.

Theorem 16.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary graph class
such that for every fixed
r\geqslant 1
,

\fw_{r}(G)\leqslant o_{\mathcal{C},r}(n^{\frac{1}{2}})\qquad\text{for every $n$-vertex graph $G\in\mathcal{C}$.}

Then
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent.
In particular, every hereditary class of almost bounded flip-width is monadically dependent.

Note that every class of almost bounded twin-width has almost bounded flip-width
by
[
55
, Thm. 10.21]
.
Therefore, Theorem
16.1

implies Theorem
1.10
.

To prove
Theorem

16.1
,
we show the following lemma.

Lemma 16.2
.

Fix
r\geqslant 1
. Let
\mathcal{D}
be either

•

the class of star
r
-crossings, or

•

the class of clique
r
-crossings, or

•

the class of half-graph
r
-crossings, or

•

the class of comparability grids.

Then
\fw_{r+1}(G)\geqslant\Omega_{r}({n^{\frac{1}{2}}})
for every
n
-vertex graph
G\in\mathcal{D}
.

Theorem
16.1
follows easily from Lemma
16.2
, as we now show.

Proof of Theorem
16.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary graph class, such that
for every fixed
r\geqslant 1
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
\fw_{r}(G)=o_{\mathcal{C},r}(|G|^{\frac{1}{2}})
.
We want to prove that
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent, so suppose otherwise.
By Theorem
13.1
,
there is some
r,\ell\geqslant 1
and a class
\mathcal{D}
as in Lemma
16.2
,
such that
\mathcal{C}
contains some
\ell
-flip
G^{\prime}
of every graph
G\in\mathcal{D}
.
Note that if
G^{\prime}
is an
\ell
-flip of
G
,
then from the definition of flip-width, it follows easily that
\fw_{r+1}(G)\leqslant\ell\cdot\fw_{r+1}(G^{\prime})

(see also
[
55
, Thm. 8.2]
with
q=0
).
Thus, for every
n
-vertex graph
G\in\mathcal{D}
which is a
\ell
-flip of a graph
G^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
we have by
Lemma

16.2
the contradiction

\Omega_{r}({n^{\frac{1}{2}}})\leqslant\fw_{r+1}(G)\leqslant\ell\cdot\fw_{r+1}(G^{\prime})\leqslant o_{\mathcal{C},r}(n^{\frac{1}{2}}).

This proves Theorem
16.1
.
∎

It remains to prove Lemma
16.2
.
The proof follows along the lines of the proof of
[
55
, Proposition 6.7]
,
which implies in particular that the
r
-subdivision
K^{(r)}_{n}
of
K_{n}
satisfies

\fw_{r+1}(K^{(r)}_{n})\geqslant\Omega_{r}(n^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

Fix
n,m\geqslant 1
and a symbol
S\in\{\leqslant,=\}
.
Let
H^{S}_{n,m}
denote the bipartite
graph with parts
L=[n]
and
R=[n]\times[m]
,
and edges
\{i,(i^{\prime},j)\}
, for
i,i^{\prime}\in[n]
and
j\in[m]
, such that

i\,S\,i^{\prime}
holds.
For
i\in[n]
denote
\tilde{N}(i):=[i]\times[m]\subseteq R
.

Lemma 16.3
.

Fix
k,n,m\geqslant 1
,
S\in\{\leqslant,=\}
, and let
H:=H^{S}_{n,m}=(L,R,E)
be as defined above.
Let
H^{\prime}
be a
k
-flip of
H
.
Then there is a set
X\subseteq L

of size
n-k

and an injective function
f\colon X\to L
such that for every
v\in X
,

|N^{H^{\prime}}(v)\cap\tilde{N}({f(v)})|\geqslant\frac{m}{2}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the partition of
V(H)
into
k
parts such that the graph
H^{\prime}
is a
\mathcal{P}
-flip of
H
.
First consider the case when all the vertices of
L
are in one part
A
of
\mathcal{P}
. Let
W
be the
union of the parts
B
of
\mathcal{P}
that are not flipped with
A
in the flip that produces
H^{\prime}
from
H
.
Let
X_{1}
consist of those vertices
v\in L
such that
|\tilde{N}(v)\cap W|\geqslant\frac{m}{2}
,
and let
X_{2}
consist of the remaining vertices in
L
.

Every vertex in
X_{1}
is adjacent in
H^{\prime}
to at least
\frac{m}{2}
vertices in
N^{H}(v)
(namely, to
N^{H}(v)\cap W
), so we can set
f(v)=v
for all
v\in X_{1}
.
On the other hand, if
v^{\prime}<v\in[n]
are two distinct vertices in
X_{2}
, then
N^{H^{\prime}}(v)\supseteq\tilde{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus W
and

|\tilde{N}(v^{\prime})\setminus W|>\frac{m}{2}
.
Set
X:=X_{1}\cup X_{2}\setminus\min(X_{2})
, and let
f\colon X_{1}\cup X_{2}\setminus\min(X_{2})\to X_{1}\cup X_{2}\setminus\max(X_{2})
be the injective function
that maps every vertex in
X_{1}
to itself, and every vertex in
X_{2}-\min(X_{2})
to its predecessor in
X_{2}
.
Then
|X|\geqslant|L|-1
, and every
v\in X

is adjacent in
H^{\prime}
to at least
\frac{m}{2}
elements of
\tilde{N}(f(v))
.

In the general case, partition
L
as
L=L_{1}\uplus\ldots\uplus L_{s}
, for some
s\leqslant k
, following the partition
\mathcal{P}
restricted to
L
.
For each
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
, let
H_{i}=H[L_{i}\cup R]
, and
H_{i}^{\prime}=H^{\prime}[L_{i}\cup R]
;
then
H_{i}^{\prime}
is a
k
-flip of
H_{i}
, and they fall into the special case considered above.
Hence, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant s
there is a set
X_{i}\subseteq L_{i}
with
|X_{i}|\geqslant|L_{i}|-1

and an injection
f_{i}\colon X_{i}\to L_{i}
. Set
X=X_{1}\uplus\ldots\uplus X_{s}
, and let
f\colon X\to L
be such that
f(x)=f_{i}(x)
for
x\in X_{i}
. Then
X
and
f
satisfy the required condition.
∎

For
r,\ell\geqslant 1
, let
G_{r,\ell}
denote the
disjoint union of

\ell
paths, each of length
r
(and with
r+1
vertices).
In each path, call one of the vertices
of degree one a
source
, and the other one a
target
.
We can use Lemma
16.3
(with
m=1
and
S
being
=
)
to prove the following by induction on
r\geqslant 1
.

Lemma 16.4
.

Fix
k,r,\ell\geqslant 1
.
If
G^{\prime}
is a
k
-flip of
G_{r,\ell}
, then
at least
\ell-rk
target vertices of
G_{r,\ell}

are joined by a path of length
r
in
G^{\prime}
with some
source vertex.

We present a modification of the notion of a hideout, defined in
[
55
]
.

Definition 16.5
.

Fix
r,k,d\geqslant 1
and a graph
G
.
A pair
(A,B)
of subsets of
V(G)

is a
(r,k,d)
-bi-hideout

if
|A|,|B|>d
and for every
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
,

\displaystyle|\{a\in A:|N^{G^{\prime}}_{r}[v]\cap B|\leqslant d\}|

\displaystyle\leqslant d,

\displaystyle|\{b\in B:|N^{G^{\prime}}_{r}[v]\cap A|\leqslant d\}|

\displaystyle\leqslant d.

The following is an adaptation of
[
55
, Lem. 5.16]
.

Lemma 16.6
.

Fix
r,k\geqslant 1
. Suppose that
G
has a
(r,k,d)
-bi-hideout for some
d\geqslant 1
. Then
{\fw_{r}(G)>k}
.

Proof.

Let
(A,B)
with
A,B\subseteq V(G)
be a
(r,k,d)
-bi-hideout.
We describe a strategy for the runner
in the flipper game on
G
with radius
r
and width
k
, which allows to elude the flipper indefinitely.
The strategy is as follows:
when the flipper announces a
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
in round
i
,
if
i
is odd then the runner moves to some vertex
v\in A

such that
|N_{r}^{G^{\prime}}(v)\cap B|>d
,
and if
i
is even then the runner moves to some vertex
v\in B

such that
|N_{r}^{G^{\prime}}(v)\cap A|>d
.

In the first move, pick any
v\in A
with
|N_{r}^{G}(v)\cap B|>d
.
Such a vertex exists by considering the flip
G^{\prime}=G
, since
|A|>d
.

We show it is always possible to make a move as described in the strategy.
Suppose at some point in the game,
say in an odd-numbered round (the other case is symmetric), the current position
v\in A
of the runner
is such that

\displaystyle|N_{r}^{P}(v)\cap B|

\displaystyle>d,

(13)

where
P
is the previous
k
-flip of
G
announced by the flipper (in the first round,
P=G
), and that the flipper now announces the next
k
-flip
N
of
G
. Since
(A,B)
is a
(r,k,d)
-bi-hideout,
the set
X\subseteq B
of vertices
w\in B

such that
|N_{r}^{N}(w)\cap A|\leqslant d

satisfies
|X|\leqslant d
.
By (
13
),

N_{r}^{P}(v)
contains at least one vertex
v^{\prime}\in B\setminus X
.
The runner moves from
v\in A
to
v^{\prime}\in B

along a path of length at most
r
in
P
.
As
v^{\prime}\in B\setminus X
, the invariant is maintained.
Therefore, playing according to this strategy, the runner can elude the flipper indefinitely, so
\fw_{r}(G)>k
.
∎

For a graph
G
and disjoint vertex sets
U,W\subseteq V(G)
, denote by
G[U,W]
the bipartite
graph with sides
U
and
W
and
u\in U
,
w\in W
adjacent if and only if
uw\in E(G)
.

Lemma 16.7
.

Fix
r,k\geqslant 1
and
n:=2(r+1)k+1
.
Let
G
be the star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing of order
n
.
Then
\fw_{r+1}(G)>k
.

Proof.

We use the notation of Definition
14.6
to denote the vertices of an
r
-crossing.
Let
A=L_{0}\subseteq
and
B=L_{r+1}
be the two sets of roots of the
r
-crossing
G
,
