with
|A|=|B|=n
.
Moreover,
p_{v,w,1}
refers to the first vertex on the path from
v\in A
to
w\in B
.
We show that
(A,B)

forms an
(r+1,k,k)
-bi-hideout in
G
. By Lemma
16.6
, this implies that
\fw_{r+1}(G)>k
.

Denote
A^{\prime}:=L_{1}
and
B^{\prime}:=L_{r}
.
Note that
A
and
A^{\prime}
are disjoint, and

G[A,A^{\prime}]
is a
bipartite graph isomorphic to

H^{\leqslant}_{n,n}
when
G
is a half-graph
r
-crossing, and isomorphic to
H^{=}_{n,n}
in the other two cases.
Similarly,
G[B,B^{\prime}]
is also isomorphic to either
H^{\leqslant}_{n,n}
or
H^{=}_{n,n}
.

Consider a
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
.
Applying Lemma
16.3
to the bipartite graph
H:=G[A,A^{\prime}]
and the
k
-flip
H^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}[A,A^{\prime}]
of
H

gives us a set
X\subseteq A
of size
n-k

and an injective function
f\colon X\to A
such that for all
v\in X
,

P(v)\subseteq N^{G^{\prime}}(v),\qquad\textnormal{ where }\qquad P(v)\subseteq\{p_{f(v),w,1}:w\in[n]\}\textnormal{ with }|P(v)|\geqslant\frac{n}{2}.

Let
v\in X
. By definition of
G
, there are at least
n/2

disjoint paths of length
r
between the aforementioned set
P(v)
and
B
in
G
.
Hence, by Lemma
16.4
, at least
n/2-rk>k
vertices in
P(v)
are joined in
G^{\prime}

by a path of length
r
with some root in
B
.
As
P(v)\subseteq N^{G^{\prime}}(v)
, we have

|N^{G^{\prime}}_{r+1}[v]\cap B|>k
for all
v\in X
.
Since
|X|\geqslant n-k
,

|\{v\in A:|N^{G^{\prime}}_{r+1}[v]\cap B|\leqslant k\}|\leqslant k.

By reversing the roles of
A
and
B
, we further obtain

|\{v\in B:|N^{G^{\prime}}_{r+1}[v]\cap A|\leqslant k\}|\leqslant k.

It follows that
(A,B)
forms an
(r+1,k,k)
-bi-hideout in
G
, and thus
\fw_{r+1}(G)>k
by Lemma
16.6
.
∎

Lemma 16.8
.

Let
G
be a comparability grid of order
n
.
Then
\fw_{2}(G)>(n-2)/8
.

Proof.

Consider the comparability grid
G
with vertex set
[n]\times[n]
,
and let
G^{+}
denote the
2
-colored graph
obtained by removing
(1,1)
and coloring the vertices
\{2,\ldots,n\}\times\{2,\ldots,n\}
with color
C_{1}
,
and the remaining vertices with color
C_{2}
.
The quantifier-free formula

\varphi(x,y)=E(x,y)\land\neg(C_{2}(x)\land C_{2}(y)),

when interpreted in the colored graph
G^{+}
,
defines a graph
H
which is isomorphic to the half-graph
1
-crossing of order
n-1
.
By
Lemma

16.7
,
we have

\fw_{2}(H)>\frac{(n-1)-1}{2\cdot(1+1)}=(n-2)/4.

By
[
55
, Thm. 8.2]
(with
q=0
),
a quantifier-free interpretation, when applied to a
c
-colored graph, can result in an at most
c
-fold increase of the flip-width.
Therefore,

\fw_{2}(G)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\fw_{2}(H).\qed

Lemma
16.2
now follows easily.

Proof of Lemma
16.2
.

Follows from Lemma
16.7
and Lemma
16.8
, as
r
-crossings/comparability grids have
O_{r}(n^{2})
vertices.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
16.1
.

17
Small Classes

Definition 17.1
.

A hereditary graph class
\mathcal{C}
is
small

if it contains at most
n!c^{n}
distinct labeled
n
-vertex graphs, for some constant
c
.

Formally, we implicitly assume that the class
\mathcal{C}
is closed under isomorphism. By a labeled
n
-vertex graph we mean a graph
G
with vertex set equal to
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Two labeled
n
-vertex graphs are then considered equal if their edge sets are identical.
Denoting by
\mathcal{C}_{n}
the set of labeled
n
-vertex graphs in a class
\mathcal{C}
, the class
\mathcal{C}
is small if for some constant
c
we have
|\mathcal{C}_{n}|\leqslant n!c^{n}
for all
n
.

The following theorem is a consequence of our results.

See
1.9

Let us start by giving some context on small classes.
It is known (see e.g.
[
10
, Sec. 3.6]
) that the class of bipartite subcubic graphs (that is, graphs with maximum degree at most
3
) is not small.
Thus, in particular, the converse implication in Theorem
1.9
fails,
as the class of all subcubic graphs is nowhere dense and therefore
monadically dependent.

On the other hand, it is known that every class of bounded twin-width is small
[
10
]
. In the same paper, it has been conjectured that the converse also holds, but this conjecture has been subsequently refuted in
[
6
]
. However, small classes of ordered graphs (that is, graphs equipped with a total order on the vertex set) have bounded twin-width
[
7
]
. Note that for classes of ordered graphs, bounded twin-width coincides with monadic dependence.

To prove
Theorem

1.9
, we prove the following lemma.
For a formula
\varphi(x,y)
and graph
G
,
by
\varphi(G)
we denote the graph with vertex set
V(G)

and edges
uv
such that
u,v\in V(G)
and
G,u,v\models\varphi(u,v)\lor\varphi(v,u)
.

Lemma 17.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary class which is monadically independent,
and is closed under isomorphism.
There is a positive integer
c
and a formula
\psi(x,y)
in the signature of
c
-colored graphs,
such that for every bipartite graph
G
with
n
vertices and
m
edges there is some
c
-coloring
G^{+}
of a graph from
\mathcal{C}

with at most
c\cdot(n+m)
vertices such that
G=\psi(G^{+})[V(G)]
.

We remark that by tracing the construction from Section
14
, it is possible to derive a stronger statement, where

G^{+}\in\mathcal{C}
is uncolored.
However, the statement above is sufficient for our purpose here, and is easier to argue by analyzing the construction sketched in the proof of
Proposition

12.22
.

Note that we may assume that in the statement of Lemma
17.2
,

G
is
identical
, not just isomorphic, to the subgraph
of
\psi(G^{+})
induced by
V(G)
.
This follows from the weaker statement that
\psi(G^{+})[W]
is isomorphic to
G
for some
W\subseteq V(G^{+})
,
as the class
\mathcal{C}
is assumed to be closed under isomorphism.
We stress the distinction between identical and isomorphic graphs, to highlight that
Lemma

17.2
implies that if
G
is a
labeled
graph, then there exists a coloring
G^{+}
of a
labeled
graph from
\mathcal{C}
with the desired properties.

Proof sketch.

By
Theorem

13.1
,
there is some
r\geqslant 1
such that

\mathcal{C}
contains a flipped star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing or a comparability grid of order
n
, for every
n\geqslant 1
.

Recall the notion of a radius-
r
encoding from
Section

12.6
. Observe that for every bipartite graph
G

with
n
vertices and
m
edges, and every radius-
r
encoding
H
of
G
, the graph
H
has at most
2n+r\cdot m\leqslant O_{r}(n+m)
vertices.
Moreover, a flipped star/clique/half-graph
r
-crossing or comparability grid of order
n
contains some radius-
r
encoding
H
of
G
as an induced subgraph.
It follows that
\mathcal{C}
contains some
r
-encoding
H
of every
bipartite graph
G
, and moreover
|V(H)|\leqslant O_{r}(|V(G)|+|E(G)|)
.

Let
\varphi(x,y)
be the formula from
Lemma

12.23
,
allowing to define the
1
-subdivision of
G

in some coloring
H^{+}
of any radius-
r
encoding
H
of
G
.
Then the formula

\psi(x,y):=\exists z.\varphi(x,z)\land\varphi(z,y)

allows to define
G
in
H^{+}
, as required in the statement.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a hereditary graph class which is monadically independent. As we want to show that
\mathcal{C}
is not small,
assume towards a contradiction that
\mathcal{C}
is small.
We show that the class
\mathcal{D}
of all bipartite subcubic graphs is small, which is a contradiction (see
[
10
, Sec. 3.6]
).
Let
\psi
and
c
be as in Lemma
17.2
.

By
\mathcal{D}_{n}
we denote the set of all labeled graphs with exactly
n
vertices in
\mathcal{D}
,
and let
\mathcal{C}_{{\leqslant}n}
denote the set of all labeled graphs with at most
n
vertices in
\mathcal{C}
.
Then
|\mathcal{C}_{{\leqslant}n}|\leqslant n!2^{O(n)}
for all
n
.

For every labeled bipartite subcubic graph
G\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
with
n
vertices, let
G^{+}
denote a
c
-colored labeled graph as in Lemma
17.2
,
so that
G=\psi(G^{+})[V(G)]
and
G^{+}
has at most
c(n+3n/2)\leqslant 3cn
vertices.

The function
f\colon\mathcal{D}_{n}\to\mathcal{C}_{{\leqslant}3cn}\times c^{[3cn]}\times 2^{[3cn]}
which maps
G\in\mathcal{D}_{n}
to

(G^{+},V(G))
is injective,
as we can recover
G=\psi(G^{+})[V(G)]
.
We then have

|\mathcal{D}_{n}|\leqslant|\mathcal{C}_{{\leqslant}3cn}|\cdot(2c)^{3cn}\leqslant(3cn)!\cdot 2^{O({3cn})}\leqslant n!\cdot 2^{O(n)}.

This proves that the class
\mathcal{D}
of bipartite subcubic graphs is small, a contradiction.
∎

Part IV
Restrictions and Extensions

18
Variants of Flip-Breakability

In this section we show how to characterize nowhere denseness, bounded cliquewidth, bounded treewidth, bounded shrubdepth, and bounded treedepth using natural restrictions of flip-breakability.

18.1
Deletion-Breakability Characterizes Nowhere Denseness

Definition 18.1
.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}
is
\emph{deletion-breakable}
,
if for every radius
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
there exists a function

N_{r}:\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
k_{r}\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
with

|W|\geqslant N_{r}(m)
there exist a set
S\subseteq V(G)
with
|S|\leqslant k_{r}
and subsets
A,B\subseteq W\setminus S
with
|A|,|B|\geqslant m
such that

\textnormal{dist}_{G-S}(A,B)>r.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}
is nowhere dense if for every radius
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
there exists a bound
N_{r}\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that no graph
from
\mathcal{C}
contains an
r
-subdivided clique of order
N_{r}
as a subgraph.

Theorem 18.2
.

A class of graphs is nowhere dense if and only if it is deletion-breakable.

Proof.

It is easy to see that deletion-breakability generalizes the notion of uniform quasi-wideness discussed in the introduction: In every huge set, we find a large set of vertices pairwise of distance greater than
r
after removing few vertices. We can partition them into two halves
A
and
B
to obtain deletion-breakabilitiy.
Since nowhere dense classes are uniform quasi-wide, this implies that they are also deletion-breakable.

For the other direction, assume towards a contradiction that
\mathcal{C}
is not nowhere dense but deletion-breakable with bounds
N_{r}(\cdot)
and
k_{r}
for every
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
.
By definition, there exists a radius
r>1
such that
\mathcal{C}
contains arbitrarily large
(r-1)
-subdivided cliques as subgraphs.
Let
G\in\mathcal{C}
be a graph containing an
(r-1)
-subdivided clique of size
N_{r}(k_{r}+1)
, whose principal vertices we denote with
W
.
By deletion-breakability,
W
contains two subsets
A
and
B
, each of size
k_{r}+1
, such that

\textnormal{dist}_{G-S}(A,B)>r

for some vertex set
S
of size at most
k_{r}
.
Since
W
is an
(r-1)
-subdivided clique in
G
, there exist
k_{r}+1
disjoint paths of length
r
, that each start in
A
and end in
B
.
As
|S|\leqslant k_{r}
, at most one of those paths must survive in
G-S
, witnessing that
\textnormal{dist}_{G-S}(A,B)\leqslant r
; a contradiction.
∎

18.2
Distance-
\infty
Flip-Breakability Characterizes Bounded Cliquewidth

Definition 18.3
.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}
is
distance-
\infty
flip-breakable
,
if there exists a function

N:\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
with

|W|\geqslant N(m)
there exist subsets
A,B\subseteq W
with
|A|,|B|\geqslant m

and a
k
-flip
H
of
G
such that in
H
, no two vertices
a\in A
and
b\in B
are in the same connected component.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following.

Theorem 18.4
.

A class of graphs has bounded cliquewidth if and only if it is distance-
\infty
flip-breakable.

To prove the theorem, we work with
rankwidth
, a parameter that is functionally equivalent to cliquewidth.
A graph
G

has rankwidth at most
k

if there is a tree
T
whose leaves
are the vertices of
G
,
and inner nodes have degree at most
3
,
such that for every edge
e
of the tree,
the bipartition
A\uplus B
of the leaves of
T

into the leaves on either side of
e
,
has
cut-rank
at most
k
.
The cut-rank of a bipartition
A\uplus B

of the vertex set of a graph
G
, denoted

\mathrm{rk}_{G}(A,B)
, is defined as the rank, over the two-element field, of the
(0,1)
-matrix with rows
A
and columns
B
, where the entry at row
a\in A
and column
b\in B
is
1
if
ab\in E(G)
and
0
otherwise.

Fact 18.5

(
[
40
, Proposition 6.3]
)
.

A class of graphs has bounded cliquewidth if and only if it has bounded rankwidth.

Lemma 18.6
.

Let
T
be a rooted subtree of a binary tree and let
W
be a subset of the leaves of
T
.
There exists an edge
e\in E(T)
such that the two subtrees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
obtained by removing
e
from
T
each contain at least
\frac{1}{4}|W|
vertices from
W
.

Proof.

For a vertex
v\in V(T)
, denote by
T(v)
the subtree rooted at
v
.
Let
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{m})
be a root-to-leaf path in
T
such that for all
1\leqslant i<m
, the vertex
v_{i+1}
is the child of
v_{i}
whose subtree contains the most elements from
W
, where ties are broken arbitrarily.
Let
i\in[m]
be the largest index such that
T(v_{i})
contains at least
\frac{1}{4}|W|
vertices from
W
.
By construction,
T(v_{2})
contains at least
\frac{1}{2}|W|
elements from
W
, and therefore
i>1
, i.e.
v_{i}
has a parent
v_{i-1}
.

T(v_{i})
contains less than
\frac{1}{2}|W|
vertices from
W
, as both of its at most two children contain less than
\frac{1}{4}|W|
elements from
W
.
Therefore, the edge connecting
v_{i}
and
v_{i-1}
is the desired edge.
∎

Lemma 18.7
.

Every class of graphs with bounded rankwidth is distance-
\infty
flip-breakable.

Proof.

Fix a number
k
and let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of graphs of rankwidth at most
k
.
We will show that
\mathcal{C}
is distance-
\infty
flip-breakable for
N(m):=4m
using
2^{k}+2^{2^{k}}
flips.
For every graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
there is
a rooted subtree
T
of a binary tree with leaves
V(G)
,
such that for every edge
e\in E(T)
,
the bipartition
X\uplus Y
of the leaves of
T

into the leaves on either side of
e
,
has cut-rank at most
k
.
Let
W\subseteq V(G)
be a set of size
4m
.
By
Lemma

18.6
, there exists an edge
e
such that in the corresponding bipartition
X\uplus Y
of
V(G)
, both
X
and
Y
each contain at least
m
many vertices of
W
.
Observe that since
\mathrm{rk}_{G}(X,Y)\leqslant k
,
X
induces at most
2^{k}
distinct neighborhoods over
Y
.
Then there is a
(2^{k}+2^{2^{k}})
-flip
H
of
G
in which there are no edges between
X
and
Y
:
the corresponding partition of
V(G)
partitions the vertices of
X
into
2^{k}
parts depending on their neighborhood in
Y
and partitions the vertices of
Y
in
2^{2^{k}}
parts depending on their neighborhood in
X
.
∎

A set
W
of vertices of
G
is
well-linked
, if for every bipartition
A\uplus B
of
V(G)
,
the cut-rank of
A\uplus B
satisfies
\mathrm{rk}_{G}(A,B)\geqslant\min(|A\cap W|,|B\cap W|)
.
We use the following two facts.

Fact 18.8

(
[
40
, Thm. 5.2]
)
.

Every graph of rankwidth greater than
k
contains well-linked set of size
k
.

Fact 18.9

(
[
55
, Lem. D.2]
)
.

Let
G
be a graph
and
A\uplus B
a bipartition of
V(G)

with
\mathrm{rk}_{G}(A,B)>k
.
Then for every
k
-flip
H
of
G
there is some edge
ab\in E(H)
with
a\in A
and
b\in B
.

Lemma 18.10
.

Every class of graphs with unbounded rankwidth is not distance-
\infty
flip-breakable.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of graphs with unbounded rankwidth.
Assume towards a contradiction that
\mathcal{C}
is distance-
\infty
flip-breakable with bounds
N(\cdot)
and
k
.
By
18.8
, there exists a graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
that contains a well-linked set
W
of size at least
N(k+1)
.
By distance-
\infty
flip-breakability, there exists a
k
-flip
H
of
G
and two sets
A,B\subseteq W
of size
k+1
each, such that no two vertices
a\in A
and
b\in B
are in the same component in
H
.
We can therefore find a bipartition of
\mathcal{X}\uplus\mathcal{Y}
of the connected components of
H
, such that
\mathcal{X}
contains all the components containing a vertex of
A
and
\mathcal{Y}
contains all the components containing a vertex of
B
.
Components containing neither a vertex of
A
nor of
B
can be distributed arbitrarily among
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
.
Let
X:=\bigcup\mathcal{X}
and
Y:=\bigcup\mathcal{Y}
.
Then
X\uplus Y
is a bipartition of
V(H)
, and there is no edge between
X
and
Y
in
H
.
Since
W
is well-linked we have that the cut-rank
X\uplus Y
satisfies

\mathrm{rk}_{G}(X,Y)\geqslant\min(|X\cap W|,|Y\cap W|)\geqslant\min(|A|,|B|)=k+1.

By
18.9
, there must be an edge
ab\in E(H)
with
a\in X
and
b\in Y
; a contradiction.
∎

Combining
18.5
,
Lemma

18.7
, and
Lemma

18.10
now yields
Theorem

18.4
.

18.3
Distance-
\infty
Deletion-Breakability Characterizes Bounded Treewidth

Definition 18.11
.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}
is
distance-
\infty
deletion-breakable
,
if there exists a function

N:\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
with

|W|\geqslant N(m)

there exist a set
S\subseteq V(G)
with
|S|\leqslant k
and subsets
A,B\subseteq W\setminus S
with
|A|,|B|\geqslant m
such that in

G-S
, no two vertices
a\in A
and
b\in B
are in the same connected component.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following.

Theorem 18.12
.

A class of graphs has bounded treewidth if and only if it is distance-
\infty
deletion-breakable.

We start with the forward direction.
We assume familiarity with treewidth and (nice) tree decompositions. See for example
[
15
]
for an introduction.

Lemma 18.13
.

Every class of graphs with bounded treewidth is distance-
\infty
deletion-breakable.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a graph class of treewidth at most
k-1
.
We show that
\mathcal{C}
is distance-
\infty
deletion-breakable with bounds
N(m):=4(m+k)
and
k:=k
.

Consider a graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
of treewidth at most
k-1
and a subset
W
containing at least
N(m)=4(m+k)
vertices of
G
.
We fix a nice tree decomposition of
G
of width
k
, and associate with every bag
t
the set
V(t)\subseteq V(G)
consisting of all
vertices that are contained either in
t
or in a descendant of
t
.
We consider a walk that starts at the root of the nice tree decomposition
and walks downwards towards the leaves. Whenever the walk reaches a join node, it proceeds
towards the child
t
that maximizes
|V(t)\cap W|
.
As each node has at most two children, the cardinality
|V(t)\cap W|
of the current node
t
of the walk can decrease at most by a factor
\frac{1}{2}
with each step.
Hence, as in the proof of
Lemma

18.6
, we reach at some point a node
t
with

m+k\leqslant\frac{1}{4}|W|\leqslant|V(t)\cap W|<\frac{1}{2}|W|\leqslant 2(m+k).

Let
S\subseteq V(G)
be the vertices in this bag
t
and choose
A=(V(t)\cap W)\setminus S
and
B=W\setminus V(t)
.
Note that
|S|\leqslant k
and
|A|,|B|\geqslant m
.
By the definition of tree decompositions, the vertices
S
act as a separator in the desired sense:
In
G-S
, no two vertices
a\in A
and
b\in B
are in the same connected component.
∎

The backwards direction will follow easily from the Grid-Minor theorem by Robertson and Seymour.
First some notation.
A graph
H
is a
minor
of a graph
G
if there exists a
minor model

\mu
of
H
in
G
.
A minor model is a map
\mu
that assigns to every vertex
v\in V(H)
a connected subgraph
\mu(v)
of
G
and to every edge
e\in E(H)
an edge
\mu(e)\in E(G)
satisfying

•

for all
u,v\in V(H)
with
u\neq v
:
V(\mu(u))\cap V(\mu(v))=\emptyset
;

•

for every
(u,v)\in E(H)
:
\mu((u,v))=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})
for vertices
u^{\prime}\in V(\mu(u))
and
v^{\prime}\in V(\mu(v))
.

Possibly deviating from our notation in previous sections, in this section the
k
-grid
is the graph on the vertex set
[k]\times[k]
where two vertices
(i,j)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
are adjacent if and only if
|i-i^{\prime}|+|j-j^{\prime}|=1
.
We can now state the Grid-Minor theorem.

Fact 18.14

(
[
52
, Thm. 1.5]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of graphs with unbounded treewidth.
Then for every
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{C}
contains a graph which contains the
k
-grid as a minor.

Lemma 18.15
.

Every class of graphs with unbounded treewidth is not distance-
\infty
deletion-breakable.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that
\mathcal{C}
has unbounded treewidth but is distance-
\infty
deletion-breakable with bounds
N(\cdot)
and
k
.
Let
t:=N(2k+2)
.
By
18.14
, there is a graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
such that there exists a minor model
\mu
of the
t
-grid in
G
.
Let
W:=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{t}\}\subseteq V(G)
be a set
representing
the bottom row of the
t
-grid: we pick one vertex
v_{i}
from the subgraph
\mu((i,1))
for each
i\in[t]
.
We apply distance-
\infty
flip-breakability to the set
W
in
G
, which yields a set
S\subseteq V(G)
of size
k
and disjoint sets
A,B\subseteq W\setminus S
, each of size
2k+2
, such that in
G-S
there is no path from a vertex in
A
to a vertex in
B
.
Let
i_{A}\in[t]
be an index such that each of the sets

A_{1}:=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{i_{A}}\}\cap A\quad\text{and}\quad A_{2}:=\{v_{i_{A}+1},\ldots,v_{t}\}\cap A

contains
k+1
elements of
A
. Pick
i_{B}
,
B_{1}
, and
B_{2}
symmetrically.
Assume first
i_{A}\leqslant i_{B}
. Then
i<j
for all
v_{i}\in A_{1}
and
v_{j}\in B_{2}
.
Let
A_{\star}:=\{(i,1):v_{i}\in A_{1}\}
be the vertices from the bottom row of the
t
-grid represented by
A_{1}
and likewise let
B_{\star}:=\{(j,1):v_{j}\in B_{1}\}
.
In
Fig.

22
it is easy to see that the vertices from
A_{\star}
and
B_{\star}
can be matched by
k+1
disjoint paths in the
t
-grid.

Figure 22:
Pairing the vertices of
A_{\star}
and
B_{\star}
with disjoint paths in the
t
-grid.

By the definition of minor, also
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
can be matched by
k+1
disjoint paths in
G
.
We now reach the desired contradiction, as we assumed that no paths run between
A
and
B
in
G-S
, but removing the at most
k
vertices from
S
can destroy at most
k
paths.

In the case where
i_{A}>i_{B}
, we have
i<j
for all
v_{i}\in B_{1}
and
v_{j}\in A_{2}
and argue symmetrically.
∎

Combining
Lemma

18.13
and
Lemma

18.15
now yields
Theorem

18.12
.

18.4
Distance-
\infty
Flip-Flatness Characterizes Bounded Shrubdepth

Definition 18.16
.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}
is
distance-
\infty
flip-flat
,
if there exists a function

N:\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
with

|W|\geqslant N(m)
there exists a subset
W_{\star}\subseteq W
with
|W_{\star}|\geqslant m

and a
k
-flip
H
of
G
such that in
H
, no two vertices
u,v\in W_{\star}
are in the same connected component.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following.

Theorem 18.17
.

A class of graphs has bounded shrubdepth if and only if it is distance-
\infty
flip-flat.

To prove that bounded shrubdepth implies distance-
\infty
flip-flatness, we work with
flipdepth
, a parameter that is functionally equivalent to shrubdepth.
It is defined as follows.
The single vertex graph
K_{1}
has flipdepth
0
.
For
k>0
, a graph
G
has flipdepth at most
k
, if it
is a
2
-flip of a disjoint union of (arbitrarily many) graphs of flipdepth at most
k-1
.

Fact 18.18

(
[
34
, Thm. 3.6]
)
.

A class of graphs has bounded shrubdepth if and only if it has bounded flipdepth.

More precisely,
[
34
, Thm. 3.6]
shows the functional equivalence of shrubdepth and the graph parameter
SC-depth
.
The definition of SC-depth is obtained from the definition of flipdepth by replacing the
2
-flip with a
set complementation
, that is, the operation of complementing all the edges in an arbitrarily subset of the vertices.

2
-flips generalize set complementations, but any
2
-flip can be simulated by performing at most three set complementations.
Therefore, flipdepth and SC-depth are functionally equivalent.

Lemma 18.19
.

For every graph
G
of flipdepth at most
k
and every set
W\subseteq V(G)
,
there exists a subset
W_{\star}\subseteq W
with
|W_{\star}|\geqslant|W|^{\frac{1}{2^{k}}}

and a
4^{k}
-flip
H
of
G
,
such that in
H
, no two vertices from
W_{\star}
are in the same connected component.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by induction on
k
.
If
k=0
, we have
G=K_{1}
where the statement holds.
For the inductive step assume
G
has flipdepth at most
k+1
.
Then
G
is
2
-flip of a graph
H
that is a disjoint union of graphs of flipdepth at most
k
.
If in
H
at least
|W|^{\frac{1}{2}}
vertices of
W
are in pairwise different components, we are done.
Otherwise, there exists a component
C
of
H
which contains at least
|W|^{\frac{1}{2}}
vertices of
W
.
By assumption,
H[C]
has flipdepth at most
k
.
By induction there is a
4^{k}
-flip
H_{\star}
of
H[C]
and a set
W_{\star}\subseteq W
of size at least
|W_{\star}|\geqslant|W|^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{k}}}
such that all vertices from
W_{\star}
are in pairwise different components in
H_{\star}
.
Refining

•

the size
2
partition of the flip that produced
H
from
G
,

•

the size
2
partition which marks the component
C
in
H
,

•

the size
4^{k}
partition of the flip that produced
H_{\star}
from
H[C]
,

yields a partition witnessing a
4^{k+1}
-flip of
G
in which
all vertices from
W_{\star}
are in pairwise different components as desired.
∎

Corollary 18.20
.

Every class of bounded shrubdepth is distance-
\infty
flip-flat.

We will use the following proof strategy for the other direction.

Fact 18.21

(
[
47
, Thm. 1.1]
)
.

Every class of unbounded shrubdepth transduces the class of all paths.

Lemma 18.22
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of graphs that is distance-
\infty
flip-flat.
Every class that is transducible from
\mathcal{C}
is also distance-
\infty
flip-flat.

Lemma 18.23
.

The class of all paths is not distance-
\infty
flip-flat.

It is easy to see that combining
18.21
,
Lemma

18.22
,
Lemma

18.23
yields the following.

Lemma 18.24
.

Every class of graphs with unbounded shrubdepth is not distance-
\infty
flip-flat.

Together with
Corollary

18.20
, the above lemma proves
Theorem

18.17
.
It remains to prove
Lemma

18.22
and
Lemma

18.23
.
The former is an immediate consequence of the following fact where

\varphi(x,y)
is a formula in the language of colored graphs
and
\varphi(G)
denotes the graph with vertex set
V(G)
and edge set
\{uv:G\models\varphi(u,v)\lor\varphi(v,u)\}
.

Fact 18.25

(
[
55
, Lem. H.3]
)
.

For every formula
\varphi(x,y)
and
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
there exists
s,\ell\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for every colored graph
G
and for every
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
, there exists an
\ell
-flip
H^{\prime}
of
H:=\varphi(G)
such that for every two vertices
u
and
v
adjacent in
H^{\prime}
we have that
\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(u,v)\leqslant s
.

Proof of
Lemma

18.22
.

Assume the class
\mathcal{C}
is distance-
\infty
flip-flat with bounds
N(\cdot)
and
k

and transduces the class
\mathcal{D}
using the formula
\varphi(x,y)
.
We will show that also
\mathcal{D}
is flip-flat with bounds
N(\cdot)
and
\ell
, where
\ell
is the bound obtained from
18.25
for
\varphi
and
k
.
Let
H\in\mathcal{D}
,
m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, and
W\subseteq V(G)
be a set of size at least
N(m)
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
transduces
\mathcal{D}
, we have
H=\varphi(G)[V(H)]
for some colored graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
.
By distance-
\infty
flip-flatness, there is a
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
and a subset
W_{\star}\subseteq W
of size at least
m
whose vertices are pairwise in different components in
G^{\prime}
.
By
18.25
, there is also an
\ell
-flip
H^{\prime}
of
\varphi(G)
in which the vertices of
W_{\star}
are in pairwise different components.
It follows that
H^{\prime}[V(H)]
is the desired
\ell
-flip of
H
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

18.23
.

Assume towards a contradiction that the class of all paths is distance-
\infty
flip-flat with bounds
N(\cdot)
and
k
.
Let
G
be the path containing
N(8k+2)
vertices.
By flip-flatness there exists a
k
-coloring
\mathcal{K}
of
G
and
F\subseteq\mathcal{K}^{2}
such that
G_{\oplus}:=G\oplus_{\mathcal{K}}F
contains at least
8k+2
components.
Let
\mathcal{B}:=\{C\in\mathcal{K}:|C|\geqslant 5\}

be the
big
color classes and
W:=\bigcup(\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{B})
be the at most
4k
vertices contained in
small
color classes.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the subgraph of
G
obtained by isolating
W
and let
F^{\prime}:=F\cap\mathcal{B}^{2}
be the restriction of
F
to
\mathcal{B}
.

G^{\prime}_{\oplus}:=G^{\prime}\oplus_{\mathcal{K}}F^{\prime}
is a subgraph of
G_{\oplus}
: every edge
uv
in
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
has no endpoint in
W
and is therefore also present in
G_{\oplus}
.
Since
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
and
G_{\oplus}
share the same vertex set,
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
has at least as many components as
G_{\oplus}
.
In order to arrive at the desired contradiction, it remains to bound the number of components in
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
.
Towards this goal, we first bound the number of components in
G^{\prime}
.
As
G^{\prime}
is obtained from a path by isolating at most
4k
vertices,
G^{\prime}
contains at most
8k+1
components: the path is cut in at most
4k
places leading to
4k+1
components plus the additional at most
4k
isolated vertices.

Claim 18.26
.

If two vertices
u
and
v
are adjacent in
G^{\prime}
, then they are connected in
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
.

Proof.

Since
u
and
v
are adjacent, they are from big color classes
\mathcal{K}(u)
and
\mathcal{K}(v)
.
Assume the adjacency between
\mathcal{K}(u)
and
\mathcal{K}(v)
was flipped, as otherwise we are done.
As
G^{\prime}
has maximum degree two, we have
|N_{1}^{G^{\prime}}[u]\cup N_{1}^{G^{\prime}}[v]|\leqslant 4
.
If
\mathcal{K}(u)=\mathcal{K}(v)
then there exists a vertex in that class that is adjacent to none of
u
and
v
in
G^{\prime}
and therefore adjacent to both of them in
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
and we are done.
Otherwise, there are three vertices
U\subseteq\mathcal{K}(u)
non-adjacent to
v
in
G^{\prime}
and three vertices
V\subseteq\mathcal{K}(v)
non-adjacent to
u
.
Again using the fact that
G^{\prime}
has maximum degree two, we find
u^{\prime}\in U
and
v^{\prime}\in V
that are non-adjacent in
G^{\prime}
. It follows that
(u,v^{\prime},u^{\prime},v)
is a path in
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
.
∎

It follows that
G^{\prime}_{\oplus}
(and also
G_{\oplus}
) contains at most
8k+1
components; a contradiction.
∎

18.5
Distance-
\infty
Deletion-Flatness Characterizes Bounded Treedepth

Definition 18.27
.

A class of graphs
\mathcal{C}
is
distance-
\infty
deletion-flat
,
if there exists a function

N:\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
with

|W|\geqslant N(m)
there exists
a set
S\subseteq V(G)
with
|S|\leqslant k
and a subset
W_{\star}\subseteq W\setminus S
with
|W_{\star}|\geqslant m
such that in

G-S
, no two vertices
u,v\in W_{\star}
are in the same connected component.

In this subsection we relate distance-
\infty
deletion-flatness
to the graph parameter
treedepth
.
The single vertex graph
K_{1}
has treedepth
1
.
For
k>1
, a graph
G
has treedepth at most
k
if there exists a vertex
whose deletion splits
G
into a disjoint union of (arbitrarily many) graphs of treedepth at most
k-1
.

Theorem 18.28
.

A class of graphs has bounded treedepth if and only if it is distance-
\infty
deletion-flat.

Proof.

Essentially, the definitions of treedepth and distance-
\infty
deletion-flatness are obtained from the definitions of flipdepth and distance-
\infty
flip-flatness by replacing flips with vertex deletions.
Proving that every class of bounded treedepth is distance-
\infty
flip-flat is therefore analogous to the proof of
Lemma

18.19
.
The other direction follows by combining the following easy facts.

1.

If a class has bounded treedepth then so does its closure under taking subgraphs.

2.

Every class of unbounded treedepth contains all paths as subgraphs.
[
44
, Proposition 6.1]

3.

The class of all paths is not distance-
\infty
flip-flat.

∎

19
Binary Structures

In this section, we lift the some of the
characterizations of monadically dependent graph classes to the more general setting of binary structures, that is, structures equipped with a finite number of binary relations.
More precisely, with the appropriate notion of flips for binary structures defined further below, we prove the following.

Theorem 19.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite signature consisting of binary relation symbols, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of
\Sigma
-structures. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent,

2.

\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable,

3.

for every
h\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
there is some
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that
no structure
G\in\mathcal{C}
contains a (minimal) transformer of length
h
and order
n
.

As an application, we demonstrate that in the setting of ordered graphs, this result allows us to deduce a key result of
[
7
]
,
stating that monadically dependent classes of ordered graphs have bounded grid rank, and hence bounded twin-width. Moreover, on the non-structure side,
we derive that monadically independent classes of ordered graphs contain some specific patterns that were also exhibited in
[
7
]
.
Those patterns form the main ingredient for the forbidden subgraph characterization of monadic dependence for classes of ordered graphs given in
[
7
]
.

The proof of Theorem
19.1
follows by using standard ideas.
We use the fact that for any monadically dependent class of binary structures we can find a monadically dependent graph class,
such that one can transduce back and forth between those classes via a straightforward transduction.
We moreover use the observation that flip-breakability and transformers are preserved under (quantifier-free) transductions.

19.1
Notions

We first introduce the setting of binary structures, and define monadic dependence and flip-breakability for classes of binary structures.

Structures.

Fix a signature
\Sigma
consisting of one or more relation symbols, where each symbol has a specified
arity
, which is a positive integer. The signature
\Sigma
is
binary

if every relation symbol
R\in\Sigma
has arity exactly two.
A
\Sigma
-structure

G
consists of a domain, denoted
V(G)
, and an interpretation of each relation symbol
R\in\Sigma
of arity
k
as a relation
R_{G}\subseteq V(G)^{k}
.
A graph
G
is represented as a structure over the signature
\{E\}

consisting of one binary relation symbol
E
, which is interpreted
as the set of pairs
(u,v)\in V(G)^{2}
such that
uv\in E(G)
.
In particular, each class of graphs can be viewed as a class of
\{E\}
-structures.

Gaifman Graph and Distances.

The
Gaifman graph
of a
\Sigma
-structure
G

is the graph with vertex set
V(G)
and
edges between vertices
u\neq v

if and only if there is some symbol
R\in\Sigma
and tuple
\bar{u}\in R_{G}
such that both
u
and
v

occur in
\bar{u}
.
For two elements
u,v\in V(G)
, we write
\textnormal{dist}_{G}(u,v)
to denote the distance between
u
and
v
in the Gaifman graph of
G
.

Colored Structures.

If
G
is a
\Sigma
-structure and
k\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
then a
k
-coloring

G^{+}
of
G

is specified by a function mapping each element of
V(G)
to exactly one color in
\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
Denoting by
\Sigma^{(k)}

the signature
\Sigma
together with the
k
unary predicates
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}
,
we may view a
k
-coloring of
G
as the
\Sigma^{(k)}
-structure
G^{+}

obtained from
G
by expanding
G
by unary relation symbols
representing the respective color classes.

Transductions and Monadic Dependence.

Fix two relational signatures
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
relation
symbols. An
abstract transduction

T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
is a binary relation between
\Sigma
-structures and
\Gamma
-structures.
For a
\Sigma
-structure
G
we write
T(G)
to denote the class of

\Gamma
-structures that are related to
G
via
T
,
and say that
T

transduces

H
from
G
if

H\in T(G)
.
For a class
\mathcal{C}
of
\Sigma
-structures we write
T(\mathcal{C})

to denote
\bigcup\{T(G):G\in\mathcal{C}\}
.

A (first-order)
transduction

T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
is specified
by a number
k
of colors, and a collection of first-order formulas
\varphi_{R}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})
,
for each symbol
R\in\Gamma
with arity
r
,
where each of the formulas
\varphi_{R}
is in the signature
\Sigma^{(k)}
. The transduction
T
is defined as follows.
First, for a
\Sigma^{(k)}
-structure
G^{+}
,
let
T_{0}(G^{+})
denote the structure
H
with domain
V(G^{+})
,
in which each relation
R\in\Gamma
of arity
r
is interpreted as

R_{H}\quad:=\quad\{\ (v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\in V(G)^{r}\ :\ G^{+}\models\varphi(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\ \}.

For a
\Sigma
-structure
G
,
T(G)

consists of all structures that can be obtained (up to isomorphism) in the following steps:

1.

color
G
using
k
colors,
obtaining a
\Sigma^{(k)}
-structure
G^{+}
,

2.

construct the structure
H:=T_{0}(G^{+})
as defined above,

3.

take an induced substructure of
H
.

This finishes the description of
T
.

By a
transduction
we mean a first-order transduction.

Definition 19.2
.

A class
\mathcal{C}
of
\Sigma
-structures
transduces
a class
\mathcal{D}
of
\Gamma
-structures
if there exists a first-order transduction
T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma

such that
\mathcal{D}\subseteq T(\mathcal{C})
.

Definition 19.3
.

A class
\mathcal{C}
of
\Sigma
-structures is
monadically dependent

if
\mathcal{C}
does not transduce the class of all (finite) graphs.

Flips.

We use the notion of flips for binary structures defined in
[
55
, Sec. 7]
.
For a
\Sigma
-structure
G
and partition
\mathcal{P}
of
V(G)
,
a
\mathcal{P}
-flip

F
of
G
is specified by a
\Sigma
-structure

F
with domain
\mathcal{P}
. Applying
F
to
G
results
in the structure
G^{\prime}
with domain
V(G)
, in which each
symbol
R\in\Sigma
is interpreted as the relation

R_{G^{\prime}}~~:=~~R_{G}~~\triangle\bigcup_{(P,Q)\in R_{F}}P\times Q,

where
\triangle
denotes the symmetric difference.

A
k
-flip
of
G
is a structure
G^{\prime}
obtained as above,
for some partition
\mathcal{P}
of
V(G)
into
k
parts.

Flip-breakability.

Definition 19.4
.

Fix a signature
\Sigma
consisting of binary relation symbols.
A class
\mathcal{C}
of
\Sigma
-structures is
flip-breakable
if for every radius
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
there exists an unbounded function
f_{r}\colon\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
k_{r}\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
there exist subsets
A,B\subseteq W
with
|A|,|B|\geqslant f_{r}(|W|)
and a
k_{r}
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
such that

\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A,B)>r.

In other words,
for every
r\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
,
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
W\subseteq V(G)
there are subsets
A,B\subseteq W
with
|A|,|B|\geqslant U_{\mathcal{C},r}(|W|)
and a
\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C},r)
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
such that
\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A,B)>r.

Transformers and Minimal Transformers.

All the notions
from Section
11

– of
meshes
,
vertical/horizontal meshes
,

conducting pairs
,
(minimal) transformers
–
can be
interpreted verbatim in the setting of binary structures.
Indeed, all those notions are defined in terms
of the atomic types of pairs of vertices,
\mathrm{atp}_{G}(u,v)
,
in a (colored) graph
G
, which makes sense in any structure.

We have defined all the notions involved in the statement of Theorem
19.1
. We now introduce some tools that will be used in its proof.

19.2
Flip Transfer Lemma

We repeat a lemma from
[
55
]
, which allows to “transfer” flips
along a transduction, without decreasing distances too much.
We state the lemma in greater generality than
[
55
, Lem. H.3]
,
namely for binary structures. However, the proof remains the same,
and is a straightforward consequence of locality of first-order logic.

Lemma 19.5

(
[
55
, Lem. H.3]
)
.

Fix two finite signatures
\Gamma,\Sigma
consisting of binary relation symbols and numbers
k,c,q\geqslant 1
.
Let
T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
be a transduction involving
c
colors
and formulas of quantifier rank at most
q
.
Let
G
be a
\Sigma
-structure and let
H\in T(G)
.
For every
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
there is a
\mathrm{const}(k,c,q)
-flip
H^{\prime}
of

H
such that:

\displaystyle\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{q}\cdot\textnormal{dist}_{H^{\prime}}(u,v)\qquad\text{for all $u,v\in V(G)$.}

(14)

The next lemma is an easy consequence.

Lemma 19.6
.

Fix two finite signatures
\Sigma,\Gamma
consisting of binary relation symbols.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of
\Sigma
-structures,
which transduces a class
\mathcal{D}
of
\Gamma
-structures.
If
\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable then
\mathcal{D}
is flip-breakable.

Proof.

Let
T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
be a transduction
witnessing that
\mathcal{C}
transduces
\mathcal{D}
, where
T
involves
c
colors.
Let
q
be maximal quantifier rank of the formulas involved in
T
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that

\mathcal{D}
is the class of structures of the form
T_{0}(G^{+})
,
where
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
G^{+}
is a
c
-coloring of
G
, since flip-breakability is preserved by taking the hereditary closure of a class of structures.

Fix a radius
r
. We prove that
\mathcal{D}
satisfies the
condition in flip-breakability for radius
r
.
By assumption,
\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable for radius
r\cdot 2^{q}
.
This means that there is a constant
k=\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C},q,r)

such that for every graph
G\in\mathcal{C}
and set
W\subseteq V(G)

there is a
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
and two sets
A,B\subseteq W
of size
U_{\mathcal{C},q,r}(|W|)
such that

\displaystyle\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A,B)>r\cdot 2^{q}.

(15)

Let
H\in\mathcal{D}
, so that
H=T_{0}(G^{+})
for some
G\in\mathcal{C}
and
c
-coloring
G^{+}
of
G
. Let
W\subseteq V(H)=V(G)
.
Then there is a
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
and two sets
A,B\subseteq W
as described above.
Let
H^{\prime}
be the
\ell
-flip of
H
as described in Lemma
19.5
, for some
\ell=\mathrm{const}(k,c,q)\leqslant\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C},q,r,T)\leqslant\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{D},r)
.
Then combining (
15
) with (
14
)
we conclude that

\displaystyle\textnormal{dist}_{H^{\prime}}(A,B)>r.

(16)

This proves that the class
\mathcal{D}
is flip-breakable with radius
r
.
As
r
is arbitrary, the lemma follows.
∎

19.3
Encoding Binary Structures in Graphs

We now show how binary structures can be encoded in graphs,
in a way which preserves monadic dependence. This encoding is folklore,
but we need to analyze some of its properties related to our context.

To this end, it is convenient to use
transductions with copying
, defined below.
First, the
copying operation
, parameterized by a signature
\Sigma
and number
\ell
of copies and denoted
C_{\ell}
, is an abstract transduction
from
\Sigma
-structures to
\Sigma\cup\{M\}
-structures,
where
M
is a binary relation symbol not in
\Sigma
,
and is defined as follows.
Given an input
\Sigma
-structure
G
,
define the
\Sigma\cup\{M\}
-structure
C_{\ell}(G)
,
with vertex set
V(G)\times[\ell]
,
such that

\displaystyle C_{\ell}(G)_{M}

\displaystyle=\{((v,i),(v,j)):v\in V(G),i,j\in[\ell]\},

\displaystyle C_{\ell}(G)_{R}

\displaystyle=\{((v_{1},i),(v_{2},i),\ldots,(v_{r},i)):(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\in R_{G},i\in[\ell]\}\qquad\textit{for $R\in\Sigma$ of arity $r$}.

A (first-order)
transduction with copying

T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma

is a composition of a copying operation

C_{\ell}\colon\Sigma\to(\Sigma\cup\{M\})
, for some
\ell\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, and a transduction
S\colon(\Sigma\cup\{M\})\to\Gamma
.
More precisely, given a
\Sigma
-structure
G
,
we set
T(G):=S(C_{\ell}(G))
.
We say that
T
is
quantifier-free

if all the formulas defining the underlying transduction
S

are quantifier-free formulas.

Transductions with copying are closed under composition, as expressed by the following
folklore result
(see, e.g.,
[
31
, Lem. 2.5]
).

Fact 19.7
.

Let
T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
and
S\colon\Gamma\to\Delta
be two transductions with copying. Then the composition
S\circ T\colon\Sigma\to\Delta
is (definable by) a transduction with copying.

Allowing transductions with copying instead of usual transductions in the definition of monadic dependence would not affect the notion. This is also folklore, but we sketch a proof below for completeness.

Fact 19.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of
\Sigma
-structures such that
for some transduction with copying
T
,
the class
T(\mathcal{C})
contains every graph.
Then
\mathcal{C}
transduces the class of all graphs
via a transduction without copying.

Proof sketch.

This can be deduced from the result of Baldwin and Shelah
[
4
, Thm. 8.1.8]
.
We sketch a more direct argument.
Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
transduces the class of all graphs using a transduction
T
with copying. Then
T
is of the form
T=S\circ C_{\ell}
for some transduction
S

and number
\ell
, where
C_{\ell}
is the
\ell
-times copying operation.
Let
K^{(1)}_{n}
denote the
1
-subdivision of the
n
-clique.
In particular,

\displaystyle\{K^{(1)}_{n}:n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\}\subseteq T(\mathcal{C})

Fix
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then there is a structure
G\in\mathcal{C}

such that
K^{(1)}_{n}\in T(G)
.
We prove that there is a fixed collection
(T_{p,q}:p,q\in[\ell])
of
\ell^{2}
many transductions (depending only on
T
) such that
K^{(1)}_{m}\in T_{p,q}(G)
for some
m\geqslant U_{T}(n)
and
p,q\in[\ell]
.
By the pigeonhole principle, since
n
is arbitrarily large, and since
\mathcal{C}
is hereditary, this proves that
there is a fixed transduction
T_{p,q}

such that
\{K^{(1)}_{m}:m\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}\}\subseteq T_{p,q}(\mathcal{C})
.
Therefore,
\mathcal{C}
transduces the class of
1
-subdivided cliques, which in turn transduces the class of all graphs.
The conclusion follows by composing the two transductions
without copying, obtaining a single transduction without copying.

Denote the principal vertices
of
K^{(1)}_{n}
by
(a_{i}:i\in[n])
,
and the subdivision vertices by
(b_{i,j}:i,j\in[n],i<j)
.
As
T
creates
\ell
copies, we can assume that
V(K^{(1)}_{n})\subseteq V(G)\times[\ell]
.
Let
\pi_{1}\colon V(G)\times[\ell]\to V(G)

and
\pi_{2}\colon V(G)\times[\ell]\to[\ell]

be the projection mappings selecting the first and second entry of a pair, respectively.

Color each edge
\{i,j\}
, where
i<j\in[n]
, of the clique
K_{n}

with the color
(\pi_{2}(a_{i}),\pi_{2}(b_{ij}))
.
By Ramsey’s theorem,
there is a subset
I\subseteq[n]
of size
U_{\ell}(n)

such that all edges have the same color.
It follows that there are
p,q\in[\ell]

such that
\pi_{2}(a_{i})=p
for all
i\in I

and
\pi_{2}(b_{i,j})=q
for all
i<j\in I
.

Let
K^{(1)}_{I}
be the subgraph of
K^{(1)}_{n}

induced by the vertices
A=\{a_{i}:i\in I\}
and
B=\{b_{i,j}:i<j\in~I\}
.
Then
K^{(1)}_{I}
is (isomorphic to) the
1
-subdivision of the clique on
|I|\geqslant U_{\ell}(n)
vertices.
By another Ramsey argument,
it is not difficult to show that
by replacing
I
by a subset of size
U(|I|)

(which we denote
I
as well),
we may ensure that the mapping
\pi_{1}
, restricted to

V(K^{(1)}_{I})\subseteq V(G)\times[\ell]
,
is injective
(this is similar to the argument in Lemma
12.2
).

For
i\in[\ell]

let
c_{i}\colon V(G)\to V(G)\times[\ell]

denote the function that maps each vertex
v\in V(G)

to the copy
(v,i)
of
v
in
V(G)\times[\ell]
.
Let
\varphi(x,y)
be the formula underlying the transduction
S
, and let
k
denote the number of colors that are involved in this formula.
Define sets
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\subseteq V(G)
as

\displaystyle A^{\prime}:=\pi_{1}(A)\qquad B^{\prime}:=\pi_{1}(B).

(17)

It follows from the above that
|A^{\prime}|=|I|,|B^{\prime}|={|I|\choose 2}
,
A^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
Furthermore, there is some

k
-coloring
H^{+}
of
H:=C_{\ell}(G)

such that

\displaystyle(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},\,\{ab:a\in A^{\prime},b\in B^{\prime},H^{+}\models\varphi(c_{p}(a),c_{q}(b))\})\quad\cong\quad K^{(1)}_{I}.

(18)

The
k
-coloring
H^{+}
of
H

induces a
k^{\ell}
-coloring
G^{+}
of
G
,
where each vertex
v\in V(G)
is colored
by the
\ell
-tuple of colors of the
\ell
copies of the vertex
v
in
H^{+}
.

Claim 19.9
.

There is a formula
\varphi_{p,q}(x,y)
, depending only on
p,q
, and
\varphi
, such that
the following holds for all
a,b\in V(G)
:

G^{+}\models\varphi_{p,q}(a,b)\quad\iff\quad H^{+}\models\varphi(c_{p}(a),c_{q}(b)).

Proof sketch.

We show more generally that for every formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})
and for each
f\colon[t]\to[\ell]
,
there is a formula
\varphi_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})
, depending only on
\varphi
and
f
,
such that the following holds for all
a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}\in V(G)
:

G^{+}\models\varphi_{f}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{t})\quad\iff\quad H^{+}\models\varphi(c_{f(1)}(a_{1}),\ldots,c_{f(t)}(a_{t})).

This is proved by induction on the size of
\varphi
. In the base case
\varphi
is an atomic formula and the formula
\varphi_{f}
is constructed directly. In the inductive step, we only need to consider formulas
\varphi
of the form
\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2}
,
\neg\varphi^{\prime}
or
\exists x.\varphi^{\prime}
, where
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi^{\prime}
are formulas smaller than
\varphi
.
The first two cases, of boolean combinations, follow immediately from the inductive hypothesis.
In the case of a formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})=\exists x_{t+1}.\varphi^{\prime}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t+1})
define

\varphi_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}):=\exists x_{t+1}.\bigvee_{f^{\prime}\supset f}\varphi^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t+1}),

where the disjunction ranges over all functions
f^{\prime}\colon[t+1]\to[\ell]
extending
f\colon[t]\to[\ell]
,
and the formula
\varphi^{\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
is obtained by inductive assumption.
It is straightforward to verify that the formula
\varphi_{f}
has the required property. This completes the induction.
The claim then follows by setting
\varphi_{p,q}(x,y):=\varphi_{f}(x,y)
, where
f(1)=p
and
{f(2)=q}
.
∎

From
19.9
and
(
18
)
it follows that

(A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime},\,\{ab:a\in A^{\prime},b\in B^{\prime},G^{+}\models\varphi_{p,q}(a,b)\})\quad\cong\quad K^{(1)}_{I}.

Moreover, the formula
\varphi_{p,q}
only depends on
\varphi
and the indices
p,q\in[\ell]
.

Let
T_{p,q}
denote the transduction without copying which first colors the given input structure using
k^{\ell}
colors,
then applies the formula
\varphi_{p,q}(x,y)
,
defining the edges of a graph, and finally, takes an arbitrary induced subgraph.
The argument above proves that for every
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
there are
p,q\in[\ell]
and
m\geqslant U_{T}(n)
such that

K^{(1)}_{m}\in T_{p,q}(G).\qed

Corollary 19.10
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a monadically dependent class of
\Sigma
-structures
and
T\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
is a transduction with copying then

T(\mathcal{C})
is monadically dependent.

Proof.

Assume towards a contradiction that
T(\mathcal{C})
transduces the class of all graphs,
that is, there is a transduction
S\colon\Gamma\to\{E\}

such that
(S\circ T)(\mathcal{C})
contains the class of all graphs.
As
S\circ T
is a transduction with copying by Fact
19.7
,
by Fact
19.8
it follows that
\mathcal{C}
transduces the class of all graphs, that is,
\mathcal{C}
is monadically independent; a contradiction.
∎

The following lemma allows us to encode binary structures in graphs.

Lemma 19.11
.

Fix a finite signature
\Sigma
consisting of binary relational symbols, and let
\Gamma=\{E\}
denote the signature of graphs.
There are

•

a quantifier-free transduction with copying
F\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
,

•

a transduction (without copying)
T\colon\Gamma\to\Sigma
,

such that

\displaystyle G\in T(F(G))\qquad\text{for every $\Sigma$-structure $G$.}

(19)

Proof sketch.

Fix a
\Sigma
-structure
G
, and let
V
denote its vertex set.
We define the graph
F(G)
as follows.
The vertex set is

V(F(G)):=V_{0}\uplus V_{1}\uplus\biguplus_{R\in\Sigma}V_{R},

where
V_{0},V_{1}
, and
V_{R}
, for
R\in\Sigma
, are disjoint copies of

V
.
That is,
V(F(G))
consists of
|\Sigma|+2
copies of
V(G)
.
Write
c_{i}(v)\in V_{i}
for the copy of vertex
v\in V
in
V_{i}
,
for
i\in\{0,1\}\cup\Sigma
.
The edges of
F(G)
are

•

\{c_{0}(u),c_{R}(v)\}
, for every
R\in\Sigma
and
(u,v)\in R_{G}
,

•

\{c_{0}(v),c_{1}(v)\}
, for every
v\in V(G)
, and

•

\{c_{1}(v),c_{R}(v)\}
, for every
v\in V(G)
and
R\in\Sigma
.

We skip the straightforward argument that the operation
F
can be implemented as a transduction with copying.
We now argue that there is a fixed transduction
T

which recovers
G
from
F(G)
.

By identifying
V(G)
with
V_{0}
by via the bijection
c_{0}
,
we observe that
for every pair
u,v\in V(G)=V_{0}
and relation symbol
R\in\Sigma
,
the following conditions are equivalent:

•

(u,v)\in R_{G}
,

•

there are vertices
v^{\prime}\in V_{R}
and
v^{\prime\prime}\in V_{1}

such that
\{u,v^{\prime}\},\{v,v^{\prime\prime}\},\{v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}\in E(F(G))
.

The transduction
T
colors each of the
|\Sigma|+2
copies of
V
comprising
V(F(G))
by a separate color, using
|\Sigma|+2
many colors jointly, represented by unary predicates.
For each relation
R\in\Sigma
, the transduction contains a formula
\varphi_{R}(x,y)
in the signature of
|\Sigma|+2
-colored graphs.
The formula
\varphi_{R}(x,y)
recovers the relation
R

by verifying the second condition described above,
which is clearly expressible by a first-order formula involving the unary predicates representing the copies and a binary relation representing adjacency in
F(G)
.

It follows from the equivalence of the two conditions above that
G\in T(F(G))
. As the transduction
T
does not depend on the
\Sigma
-structure
G
,
the statement follows.
∎

The following lemma can be seen as a technical amendment to the statement of Lemma
19.11
.

Lemma 19.12
.

Let
F
be the transduction constructed in Lemma
19.11
.
For every
\Sigma
-structure
G
, if
G
contains a transformer of length

h
and order
n
, then there is a graph
H\in F(G)
that contains a transformer of length at most
3h
and order
n
.

Proof sketch.

We use the transformer notation introduced in
Section

11.1
.
Fix a
\Sigma
-structure
G
and let
H\in F(G)
be the graph denoted as
F(G)
in the proof of
Lemma

19.11
.
Let
c_{i}\colon V(G)\to V(H)
be the functions that maps each vertex
v\in V(G)

to the corresponding copy of
v
in
V(H)
, for
i\in\{0,1\}\cup\Sigma
.

Since each
c_{i}
is injective, it follows that
for every mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
in
G

and each
i\in\{0,1\}\cup\Sigma
,
the composition
c_{i}\circ M
is a mesh in
H
.
Furthermore, it is easy to verify that if
M,M^{\prime}
is a regular pair of meshes
in
G
, then the pair
c_{i}\circ M,c_{j}\circ M^{\prime}
is a regular pair of meshes in
H
, for each
i,j\in\{0,1\}\cup\Sigma
.

We now argue that if
M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
is a conducting pair
of meshes in
G
, then there is
R\in\Sigma
such that each pair of consecutive meshes in the sequence

c_{0}\circ M,c_{R}\circ M^{\prime},c_{1}\circ M^{\prime},c_{0}\circ M^{\prime}

is conducting.

Namely, as the pair
M,M^{\prime}
is conducting, it is regular and not homogeneous. It follows that there is some binary relation symbol
R\in\Sigma
such that
R_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i,j))
is not the same for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
.
By definition of the edge relation in
H=F(G)
,
it follows that

E(c_{0}(M(i,j)),c_{R}(M^{\prime}(i,j)))
is not the same for all
i\in I
and
j\in J
.
It follows that the pair
c_{0}\circ M,c_{R}\circ M^{\prime}
is not homogeneous in
H
. As it is regular (see above), it is conducting.
Furthermore, by construction of
F(G)
, the pairs

(c_{R}\circ M^{\prime},c_{1}\circ M^{\prime})
and
(c_{1}\circ M^{\prime},c_{0}\circ M^{\prime})
are conducting, as
E(H)
forms a matching between the corresponding copies
V_{R}
and
V_{1}
of
V
, and between the copies
V_{1}
and
V_{0}
of
V
.

By a similar argument as above, it is easy to verify that
if
M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
is vertical (resp. horizontal) in
G
,
then
c_{0}\circ M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
is vertical (resp. horizontal) in
H
.

Finally, suppose that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{h}
is a transformer in
G
.
By the arguments above,
there are
R_{1},\ldots,R_{h-1}\in\Sigma
such that the following sequence of meshes is a transformer in
H
:

c_{0}\circ M_{1},c_{R_{1}}\circ M_{2},c_{1}\circ M_{2},c_{0}\circ M_{2},c_{R_{2}}\circ M_{3},c_{1}\circ M_{3},c_{0}\circ M_{3},\ldots,c_{R_{h-1}}\circ M_{h},c_{1}\circ M_{h},c_{0}\circ M_{h}

∎

Lemma 19.13
.

Let
F\colon\Sigma\to\Gamma
be a quantifier-free transduction with copying, where
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
consist of binary relational symbols.
Let
G
be a
\Sigma
-structure and
H\in F(G)
.
If
H
contains a transformer of length
h
and order
n
then
G
contains a transformer of length
h
and order
U_{h,F}(n)
.

Proof sketch.

Suppose
F
is a composition of the
\ell
-copying operation with a quantifier-free transduction
S
without copying that uses
k
colors.
Let
H\in F(G)
. By definition, there is a
k
-coloring
G_{\ell}^{+}
of
C_{\ell}(G)
such that
H
is an induced subgraph of
S_{0}(G_{\ell}^{+})
. In particular
V(H)\subseteq V(G)\times[\ell]
.

Consider the mapping
\pi\colon V(H)\to V(G)
that maps each copy of a vertex
v
to
v
itself.
This mapping is injective when restricted to a single copy
V(G)\times\{i\}
, for some
i\in[\ell]
.
Every mesh
M\colon I\times J\to V(H)
in
H

induces a function
\pi\circ M\colon I\times J\to V(G)
.
Note that
\pi\circ M
might not be injective, thus it is not necessarily a mesh in
G
.
However, if
M
is a
monocopy mesh
in
H
, that is,
V(M)\subseteq V(G)\times\{i\}
for some
i\in[\ell]
,
then
\pi\circ M
is a mesh in
G
.

We call a mesh
M
in
H

monochromatic
, if all of its vertices have the same color in
G^{+}_{\ell}
.
Using the fact that
S
is quantifier-free,
it is straightforward to verify that if a monochromatic monocopy mesh
M
is vertical (resp. horizontal) in
H
,
then
\pi\circ M
is vertical (resp. horizontal) in
G
,
and that if
(M,M^{\prime})
is a conducting pair of monochromatic monocopy meshes in
H
,
then
(\pi\circ M,\pi\circ M^{\prime})
is a conducting pair of meshes in
G
.

Note that if
H
contains a transformer
T
of length
h
and order
n
, then it also contains a transformer
T^{\prime}=(M_{1},\ldots,M_{h})
of length
h
and order
U_{h,\ell,k}(n)
in which each mesh
M_{i}
is a monochromatic monocopy mesh,
by a Ramsey argument similar to Lemma
11.20
.
It follows from the above that the sequence
\pi\circ M_{1},\ldots,\pi\circ M_{h}
is a transformer in
G

of length
h
and order
U_{h,\ell,k}(n)\geqslant U_{h,F}(n)
.
∎

19.4
Proof of Theorem
19.1

We now prove Theorem
19.1
.

Proof of Theorem
19.1
.

Fix a finite signature
\Sigma
consisting of binary relational symbols, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of
\Sigma
-structures.

Let
F\colon\Sigma\to\{E\}
and
T\colon\{E\}\to\Sigma
be the transductions from Lemma
19.11
.
Denote
\mathcal{D}:=F(\mathcal{C})
. Note that
\mathcal{D}
is a class of graphs and
\mathcal{C}\subseteq T(\mathcal{D})
by (
19
).
Then
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent if and only if

\mathcal{D}
is monadically dependent, by Corollary
19.10
.

We now show that
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent if and only if it is flip-breakable.
Suppose first that
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent.
Hence,
\mathcal{D}
is also monadically dependent, and therefore is flip-breakable
by Theorem
13.1
.
Since
\mathcal{C}\subseteq T(\mathcal{D})
, it follows that

\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable by
Lemma
19.6
.
Conversely, assume now that
\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable.
By Lemma
19.6
, every graph class transducible from
\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable.
As the class of all graphs is not flip-breakable,
it follows that
\mathcal{C}
is monadically dependent.

The fact that the class of all graphs is not flip-breakable
follows immediately from Theorem
13.1
,
as clearly the class of all graphs is monadically independent.
One could also argue more directly, by showing that already the class
of
1
-subdivided cliques is not flip-breakable with radius
2
,
by a combinatorial argument similar as in the proof Lemma
8.10
.

We now show that if
\mathcal{C}
is monadically independent then there is some
h\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathcal{C}
contains transformers of length
h
and arbitrarily large order.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is monadically independent, neither is
\mathcal{D}
.
Therefore, by Theorem
13.1
,

\mathcal{D}
is not prepattern-free. By Proposition
11.6
, there is some
h\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
, some graph
H_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
contains a transformer of length
h
and order
n
.
As
H_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{D}=F(\mathcal{C})
, for every
n\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
there
is some
G_{n}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
H_{n}\in F(G_{n})
.
By Lemma
19.13
,

G_{n}\in\mathcal{C}
contains a transformer of length
h
and order
U_{F,h}(n)
.
It follows that
\mathcal{C}
contains transformers of length
h
and arbitrarily large order, as required. Moreover, the transformer can be converted to a minimal transformer of order
U_{F,h}(n)
, by Lemma
11.21
.

Finally, suppose that
\mathcal{C}
contains transformers of length
h
and arbitrarily large order.
By Lemma
19.12
,
\mathcal{D}
contains transformers of length at most
3h
and arbitrarily large order.
Therefore,
\mathcal{D}
is monadically independent, and thus neither is
\mathcal{C}
.

One way to argue that
\mathcal{D}
is monadically independent is by showing directly that
\mathcal{D}
transduces the class of all graphs, using the transformers. Another way is
to observe that
\mathcal{D}
contains one of the families of patterns,
as described in Proposition
10.14
,
by the same proof as in that proposition. Hence,
\mathcal{D}
is monadically independent by Theorem
13.1
.

This concludes the proof of Theorem
19.1
.
∎

19.5
Two-Set Variant of Flip-Breakability

The following is a two-set variant of flip-breakability.

Theorem 19.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monadically dependent class of binary structures.
For every
r\geqslant 1
, structure
G\in\mathcal{C}
and sets
A,B\subseteq V(G)
with
|A|=|B|=m
,
there are subsets
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
of size
U_{r,\mathcal{C}}(m)
,
and a
\mathrm{const}(r,\mathcal{C})
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
such that

\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})>r.

Proof.

By Theorem
19.1
, the class
\mathcal{C}
is flip-breakable.
Applying flip-breakability to the set
A
and radius
2r
,
we get two subsets
A_{0},A_{1}\subseteq A

of size
U_{r,\mathcal{C}}(|A|)

and a
\mathrm{const}(r,\mathcal{C})
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
such that

\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(A_{0},A_{1})>2r.

By the triangle inequality, every
b\in B

is at distance greater than
r
from either
A_{0}
or
A_{1}
in (the Gaifman graph of)
G^{\prime}
.
By the pigeonhole principle, there is a subset
B^{\prime}
of
B
with
|B^{\prime}|\geqslant|B|/2

and
A^{\prime}\in\{A_{0},A_{1}\}
,
such that all elements of
B^{\prime}
are at distance larger than
r
from
A^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
. This yields the conclusion.
∎

19.6
Ordered Graphs and Twin-Width

We now derive two consequences of Theorem
19.1

in the context of ordered graphs.
An
ordered graph
is a structure
G=(V,E,<)
, where
(V,E)
is a graph and
<
is a total order on
V
. This is naturally viewed as a binary structure over the signature consisting of two binary relation symbols
E
and
<
.

We reprove the following result of
[
7
]
.
For two sets
A,B
of vertices of a (ordered) graph
G
,
let
r_{G}(A,B)
denote the maximum
of the cardinalities of the following two sets

\{N(a)\cap B:a\in A\},\quad\{N(b)\cap A:b\in B\}.

In other words,
r_{G}(A,B)
is the maximum of the number of distinct rows, and the number of distinct columns, of the
A\times B
adjacency matrix between the sets
A
and
B
.
A
convex partition
of an ordered graph
G=(V,E,<)
is a partition
\mathcal{P}
of
V
such that every part of
\mathcal{P}
is an interval with respect to
<
.

A class
\mathcal{C}
of ordered graphs has
bounded grid rank

if there are constants
\ell,m
such that for all
{G=(V,E,<)\in\mathcal{C}}

and convex partitions
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}
of size
\ell
, there
are two intervals
A\in\mathcal{L}
and
B\in\mathcal{R}
such that

r_{G}(A,B)\leqslant m
. We remark that this definition of bounded grid rank is equivalent to the definition we give in the introduction.

Theorem 19.15

(
[
7
, Thm. 1, (
iv
)
\rightarrow
(
ii
)]
)
.

Every monadically dependent class of ordered graphs has bounded grid rank.

In
[
7
]
it is furthermore proved that for classes of ordered graphs, bounded grid rank implies bounded twin-width.
This is shown using a separate argument involving the Marcus-Tardos theorem.
Theorem
19.15

can be deduced from Theorem
19.1

(actually, the two-set variant, Theorem
19.14
),
as we show further below.

We also show how another of the central results of
[
7
]
can be deduced from Theorem
19.1
.
Whereas Theorem
19.15
above describes the structure that is found in monadically dependent classes of ordered graphs,
the statement below is on the non-structure side, and describes
the patterns
that can be found in monadically independent classes of ordered graphs.
The following is a reformulation of
[
7
, Thm. 5, (
iii
)
\rightarrow
(
v
)]
.

Theorem 19.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a class of ordered graphs.
If
\mathcal{C}
is monadically independent then
for every
n
and bijection
\sigma\colon[n]\to[n]
,
there is an ordered graph
G=(V,E,<)\in\mathcal{C}
, increasing vertex sequences
a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}
and
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{n}
in
V
,
and a symbol
\alpha\in\{=,\neq,\leqslant,\geqslant\}

such that

\{a_{i},b_{j}\}\in E(G)\iff j\,\alpha\,\sigma(i)\qquad\text{for all $i,j\in[n]$.}

The following two subsections are dedicated to deriving these two theorems as consequences of
Theorem

19.1
.

19.7
Proof of Theorem
19.15

We use a simple lemma from
[
55
, Lem. G.2]
,
which allows us to analyze flips of a totally ordered set
(V,<)
, viewed as a binary structure.

Lemma 19.17

(
[
55
]
)
.

Let
L=(V,<)
be a totally ordered set (viewed as binary structure) and
L^{\prime}
be a
k
-flip of
L
. Then there is a set
S
with
|S|\leqslant k
such that any two vertices of
V-S
with no vertex of
S
between them in the order are at distance at most
2
in the Gaifman graph of
L^{\prime}
.

Proof of Theorem
19.15
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a monadically dependent class of ordered graphs.
Let
G=(V,E,<)\in\mathcal{C}
, and let
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{R}
be two convex partitions of
G
with
|\mathcal{L}|=|\mathcal{R}|=\ell
for some constant
\ell=\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})
that will be specified later.
Let
L=\{\min(A):A\in\mathcal{L}\}
and
R=\{\min(B):B\in\mathcal{R}\}
denote the sets of minimal elements of the intervals in
\mathcal{L}
and in
\mathcal{R}
, respectively. Then
|L|=|R|=\ell
.

Apply Theorem
19.14

with radius
r=5
,
yields sets
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
and
R^{\prime}\subseteq R

of size
U_{\mathcal{C}}(\ell)
,
and a
k
-flip
G^{\prime}
of
G
, for some
k\leqslant\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})
,
such that

\displaystyle\textnormal{dist}_{G^{\prime}}(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})>5.

(20)

Denote
G^{\prime}=(V,E^{\prime},<^{\prime})
.
Note that
(V,<^{\prime})
is a
k
-flip of
(V,<)
.
Let
S
be the set given by Lemma
19.17
.
Then
|S|\leqslant k\leqslant\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})
.
There is a fixed constant
\ell
depending only on
\mathcal{C}
such that

|\mathcal{L}|=|\mathcal{R}|=\ell
implies

|L^{\prime}|=|R^{\prime}|>|S|
.
This is because

|L^{\prime}|=|R^{\prime}|\geqslant U_{\mathcal{C}}(\ell)>|S|

for sufficiently large
\ell
, as
|S|\leqslant\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})
.
Fixing this sufficiently large constant
\ell=\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})
, we henceforth assume that
|L^{\prime}|=|R^{\prime}|>|S|
.

It follows that there is some interval
A\in\mathcal{L}
with
\min(A)\in L^{\prime}
and
A\cap S=\emptyset
.
By Lemma
19.17
,
the elements of
L
are pairwise at distance at most
2
in the Gaifman graph of
G^{\prime}
.
Similarly, there is an interval
B\in\mathcal{R}

with
\min(B)\in R^{\prime}
so that the elements of
B
are pairwise at distance at most
2
in the Gaifman graph of
G^{\prime}
.
Suppose some
u\in A
and
v\in B
are adjacent in the Gaifman graph of
G^{\prime}
.
It follows that
\min(A)
and
\min(B)
are at distance at most
5
in the Gaifman graph of
G^{\prime}
.
As
\min(A)\in L^{\prime}
and
\min(B)\in R^{\prime}
, this contradicts (
20
).

Therefore, the sets
A\in\mathcal{L}
and
B\in\mathcal{R}

span no edges in
G^{\prime}
.
As
(V,E^{\prime})
is a
k
-flip of
(V,E)
,
it follows that
r_{G}(A,B)\leqslant\mathrm{const}(k)\leqslant\mathrm{const}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

19.8
Proof of Theorem
19.16

We now prove Theorem
19.16
, by analyzing the minimal transformers in ordered graphs.
We use the following lemma.
If
I,J
are two indexing sequences
then the lexicographic order
<_{IJ}
on
I\times J
is defined as usual.
Denoting by
I^{R}
and
J^{R}
the reverse sequences of
I
and
J
, then we have in total

8
lexicographic orders on
I\times J
,
namely
<_{KL}
where
K\in\{I,I^{R}\}
and
L\in\{J,J^{R}\}
, or

K\in\{J,J^{R}\}
and
L\in\{I,I^{R}\}
.

Lemma 19.18
.

Let
I,J
be two indexing sequences of length
|I|=|J|\geqslant 3
,
and let
(V,<)
be a totally ordered set.
Let
M\colon I\times J\to V
be a regular mesh in
(V,<)
.
Then for one of the eight lexicographic orders
<_{\text{lex}}
described above,
we have that

M(i,j)<M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\quad\iff\quad(i,j)<_{\text{lex}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\qquad\text{for all $(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in I\times J$}.

Furthermore,
for
I^{\prime}=I-\{\min(I),\max(I)\}
,
J^{\prime}=J-\{\min(J),\max(J)\}
with
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|>3
,
we have that
M_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}

is either horizontal and not vertical,
or is vertical and not horizontal in
(V,<)
.

Proof.

Let
i_{-1}<i_{0}<i_{1}\in I
and
j_{-1}<j_{0}<j_{1}\in J
.
As
<
is a total order,
we have that
M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{1},j_{0})

or
M(i_{0},j_{0})>M(i_{1},j_{0})
.
Replacing
I
with
I^{R}
if necessary, we may assume that

\displaystyle M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{1},j_{0}).

(21)

Similarly, replacing
J
with
J^{R}
if necessary, we may assume

\displaystyle M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{0},j_{1}).

(22)

By regularity of
M
we have that

M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{1},j_{-1})\quad\iff\quad M(i_{-1},j_{1})<M(i_{0},j_{0}).

Thus, exactly one of the following holds:

M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{1},j_{-1})\quad\text{or}\quad M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{-1},j_{1}).

Exchanging the roles of
I
and
J
if necessary, we may assume

\displaystyle M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{1},j_{-1}).

(23)

Now, by regularity of
M
, we derive that

\displaystyle M(i_{0},j_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:lex2}}}{{<}}M(i_{0},j_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:lex3}}}{{<}}M(i_{1},j_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:lex2}}}{{<}}M(i_{1},j_{1}).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle M(i_{0},j_{0})<M(i_{1},j_{1}).

(24)

In the following, we use (
21
),(
22
),(
23
),(
24
)
to verify that for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J

M(i,j)<M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\qquad\iff\qquad\text{$i=i^{\prime}$ and $j<j^{\prime}$, or $i<i^{\prime}$}.

For the right-to-left implication, assume first that
i=i^{\prime}
and
j<j^{\prime}
.
Then
M(i,j)<M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})

follows by regularity and (
22
).
If
i<i^{\prime}
then
M(i,j)<M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
follows from
(
21
),(
23
),(
24
).
We prove the left-to-right implication by contrapositive.
Observe that the negation of the condition in the right-hand-side is equivalent to either
i>i^{\prime}
, or
i=i^{\prime}
and
j\geqslant j^{\prime}
.
Assuming additionally that
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, this is equivalent
to the right-hand side condition above, with
(i,j)
swapped with
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
Thus, by the implication proved already, we conclude that

M(i,j)>M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
, obtaining the negation of the left-hand side condition, and proving the contrapositive.
Therefore,
<
agrees with the lexicographic order
<_{IJ}
on
V(M)
, proving the first part of the statement.

We now verify the second part of the statement. By symmetry, we may assume that
<
agrees with
<_{IJ}
on
V(M)
.
Let
I^{\prime}=I-\{\min(I),\max(I)\}
and
J^{\prime}=J-\{\min(J),\max(J)\}
and assume
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|>3
.
We observe that
M|_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is vertical and not horizontal in
(V,<)
.
Verticality is witnessed by the function
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V
defined by
a(i):=M(i,\min(J))
.

We show that
M_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is not horizontal.
Assume
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V

is such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{atp}(M(i,j),b(j^{\prime}))\qquad\text{depends only on
$\mathrm{otp}(j,j^{\prime})$ for all $i\in I^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}$}

(25)

We show that
\mathrm{atp}(M(i,j),b(j^{\prime}))

is the same for all
i\in I^{\prime}
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
.
This will prove that
M_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is not horizontal.

Fix any
j,j^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
with
\min(J^{\prime})<j^{\prime}<\max(J^{\prime})
.
As
M_{I^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}}
is injective
and
|I^{\prime}|>1
,
we have that
M(i,j)\neq b(j^{\prime})
for some
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Suppose that

M(i,j)<b(j^{\prime})\text{\quad for some $i\in I^{\prime}$},

the other case being symmetric.
By (
25
), we have that

M(\max(I^{\prime}),j)<b(j^{\prime}).

Pick any
i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
with
i^{\prime}<\max(I^{\prime})
.
As
<
agrees with
<_{IJ}
on
V(M)
, for
all
j^{\prime\prime}\in J^{\prime}
we have:

M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime})<M(\max(I^{\prime}),j)<b(j^{\prime}).

In particular,
M(i^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime})<b(j^{\prime})
for all
j^{\prime\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
As
\min(J^{\prime})<j^{\prime}<\max(J^{\prime})
,
combined with (
25
), this implies that

\mathrm{atp}(M(i,j^{\prime\prime}),b(j^{\prime}))
is the same for all
i\in I^{\prime},j^{\prime},j^{\prime\prime}\in J^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof sketch for Theorem
19.16
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a class of ordered graphs which is monadically independent.
By Theorem
19.1
,
there is some
h\in\mathrm{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathcal{C}
contains minimal transformers of
length
h
and arbitrarily large order.
Let
G=(V,E,<)
be an ordered graph
and let
T=(M_{1},\ldots,M_{h})
be a minimal transformer in
G
of order at least
6
and length
h
.
By Lemma
19.18
and reducing the order of
T
by at most
2
,
we may assume that every mesh in
T

is either horizontal and not vertical, or vertical and not horizontal in
(V,<)
.
In particular, every mesh in
T
is either horizontal or vertical in
G
(possibly both).
By minimality, the meshes
M_{2},\ldots,M_{h-1}

are neither horizontal nor vertical, so it follows that
h\leqslant 2
.

Let
n\geqslant 6
.
As argued above, we can find a transformer
T
in
\mathcal{C}
of length
h\in\{1,2\}
and order
n
,
where each mesh in
T
is either horizontal and not vertical, or vertical and not horizontal in
(V,<)
.
We show that the following holds for some
m\geqslant U(n)
.
For every permutation

\sigma\colon[m]\to[m]

there are strictly monotone functions

a\colon[m]\to V
and

b\colon[m]\to V

such that one of the following cases holds
(uniformly) for all
i,j\in[m]
:

(=)

\{a(i),b(j)\}\in E(G)\iff j=\sigma(i)
,

(\neq)

\{a(i),b(j)\}\in E(G)\iff j\neq\sigma(i)
,

(\leqslant)

\{a(i),b(j)\}\in E(G)\iff j\leqslant\sigma(i)
,

(>)

\{a(i),b(j)\}\in E(G)\iff j>\sigma(i)
.

As
n
is arbitrarily large, this will prove the theorem (observe that the case
(>)
can be reduced to the analogous case
(\geqslant)
by shifting
b
).

Suppose first that
h=2
, that is,
T=(M,M^{\prime})

for some conducting pair of meshes

M,M^{\prime}\colon I\times J\to V(G)
,
where

M
is vertical and not horizontal, and
M^{\prime}
is horizontal and not vertical.
By the same argument as in Lemma
12.5
,
we conclude that
the atomic type
\mathrm{atp}_{G}(M(i,j),M^{\prime}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is
not the same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
, but is the
same for all
i,i^{\prime}\in I
and
j,j^{\prime}\in J
with
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.
It is not difficult to see that in the setting of ordered graphs,
this implies
M
and
M^{\prime}
to be either matched or co-matched in the (unordered) graph
(V,E)
.

Up to reindexing, assume that the indexing sequences of
M
and
M^{\prime}
are
I=[n]
and
J=[n]
.
Let
\sigma\colon[n]\to[n]
be any permutation; we view
\sigma
as a bijection
\sigma\colon I\to J
.

As
M
is not horizontal in
(V,<)
, the linear order
<
agrees on
V(M)

with one of the four lexicographic orders
<_{KL}
on
I\times J
with
K\in\{I,I^{R}\}
and
L\in\{J,J^{R}\}
.
Reversing the order of
I
if necessary,
we may assume that the sequence

(M(i,\sigma(i)):i\in I)\subseteq V(M)

is strictly increasing.
Similarly, as
M^{\prime}
is not vertical, by reversing the order of
J
if necessary, we may assume that the sequence

(M^{\prime}(\sigma^{-1}(j),j):j\in J)\subseteq V(M^{\prime})

is strictly increasing.
Moreover, if
M
and
M^{\prime}
are matched,
for all
i\in I,j\in J
we have that

(M(i,\sigma(i)),\ M^{\prime}(\sigma^{-1}(j),j))\in E(G)\quad\iff\quad j=\sigma(i).

If
M
and
M^{\prime}
are co-matched,
the condition becomes
j\neq\sigma(i)
.

Set
a(i):=M(i,\sigma(i))
and

b(j):=M^{\prime}(\sigma^{-1}(j)j)
for
i\in I
and
J\in J
.
Then

\{a(i),b(j)\}\in E(G)\iff j=\sigma(i)\qquad\text{for all $i\in I,j\in J$}.

Moreover,
(a(i):i\in I)
and
(b(j):j\in J)

are strictly increasing.
Therefore, the case
(=)
or
(\neq)
described above occurs.
This completes the analysis in the case when
T
is a transformer of length
2
.

Suppose now that
T
is a transformer of length
1
and order
n
,
that is,
T
consists of a single mesh
M
which is regular, and is both vertical and horizontal in
G=(V,E,<)
.
We argued already that
M
is either vertical or horizontal in the totally ordered set
(V,<)
, but not both.
Suppose (by symmetry) that
M
is vertical and not horizontal in
(V,<)
.
As
M
is horizontal in
(V,E,<)
but not in
(V,<)
,
it needs to be horizontal in
(V,E)
.
By
Lemma

12.14
,

M^{\mathsf{T}}
is capped in the graph
(V,E)
.
This means
that for some
\alpha\in\{=,\leqslant\}
, the mesh

M^{\mathsf{T}}
is
\alpha
-capped in
H
, where
H
is either the graph
(V,E)
,
or its edge-complement:
There is some function
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V
, where
J^{\prime}=J-\{\min(J),\max(J)\}
,
so that

\displaystyle\{M(i,j),b(j^{\prime})\}\in E(H)\quad\iff\quad j\,\alpha\,j^{\prime}\qquad\text{for all $i\in I$ and $j,j^{\prime}\in J$}.

(26)

As
M
is not horizontal in
(V,<)
,
the order
<
agrees on
V(M)
with
<_{KL}
for
K\in\{I,I^{R}\}
and
L\in\{J,J^{R}\}
.
Reversing the order of
I
if necessary,
we have that
<
agrees with
<_{IJ}
or with
<_{IJ^{R}}
.

By the Erdős-Szekeres theorem, replacing
J^{\prime}
by a subsequence of length
m\geqslant U(n)
, we may assume that the function
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V

is strictly monotone with respect to
<
.
Reversing the order of
J
if necessary,
we may assume that
b\colon J^{\prime}\to V
is strictly increasing.

Let
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
be any subsequence such that
|I^{\prime}|=|J^{\prime}|=m\geqslant U(n)
.
Up to reindexing, assume that
I^{\prime}=[m]
and
J^{\prime}=[m]
.
Let
\sigma\colon[m]\to[m]
be an arbitrary permutation; we view
\sigma
as a bijection
\sigma\colon I^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
.

Set
a(i):=M(i,\sigma(i))
for
i\in I^{\prime}
.
Then
a\colon I^{\prime}\to V
is strictly increasing,
as
<
agrees on
V(M)
with
<_{IJ}
or with
<_{IJ^{R}}
.
By (
26
) we have

\{a(i),b(j)\}\in E(H)\quad\iff\quad\sigma(i)\,\alpha\,j\qquad\text{ for }i\in I^{\prime},j\in J^{\prime}.

We conclude that one of the two cases
(=)
or
(\neq)

holds if
\alpha
is
=

(depending on whether
H
is
(V,E)
or its edge-complement)
and one of the two cases
(\leqslant)
or
(>)
holds if
\alpha
is
\leqslant
.
This completes the sketch of the proof.
∎
